Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/ledger_loss.rs` (S1 a storage refilled and a joining device sees all; S2 a deleted `ledger/` directory: signing and sync stop with the advice, status reads, the rebuild restores the own head and the other device's batches, the next batch is the old head + 1 and the other device accepts it without a fork; S2 after pruning: the own checkpoint is adopted; S2 damaged `heads.json`, `pushed.json`, `pending.json` set aside and noted, a missing newest batch fetched again; S3 a restored device fenced by the storage check, adopts its later batches and continues, the other device sees no fork; S3 two copies of one identity that published different batches: refused, remembered across restarts, the original undisturbed; S4 the ledger gone from the storage and both devices: restore finds nothing and re-enrols, `--from-storage` writes the claims, verifies everything, runs again idempotently, a new device pulls byte-identical files and `fsck --verify` is clean, the second device re-enrols and syncs; S4 in the service: one automatic rebuild, a damaged object not claimed but queued for repair; S5 a garbage `view.enc` rebuilt; `ledger status`), `engine::ledger_recovery::tests` (batches signed after a restore and never published are set aside, their events signed again, no fork) and the lab scenario `ledger-loss` (S2 with the command line and S4 with the service on two VMs and an S3 bucket).

## 39. Media-aware parallel verification and hardware-decoded thumbnails

**Why.** Every path that re-hashes stored data read one object, hashed it on the same thread, then read the next. On an SSD or a network storage that leaves the disk or the link idle most of the time; on a spinning disk, by contrast, parallel reads only add seeks. Verification now reads in parallel wherever the medium allows it and stays strictly sequential on spinning disks.

**Medium detection** (`crate::media`, per path, at the start of each run):

- *Linux and Android*: the filesystem type of the mount holding the path (`/proc/self/mountinfo`, longest mount point): `nfs`, `nfs4`, `cifs`, `smb3`, `9p`, `ceph`, `glusterfs`, FUSE mounts of network tools (`fuse.sshfs`, `fuse.rclone`, `fuse.s3fs`, `fuse.davfs2`, `fuse.gcsfuse`, …) and the like are *network*; `tmpfs` is *solid-state*; other FUSE filesystems (mergerfs, encfs, …), ZFS and overlay are *unknown*. Otherwise the path's device (`st_dev`; for filesystems with anonymous devices such as btrfs, the mount's source device node) is looked up in `/sys/dev/block/MAJ:MIN`: a partition is followed to its whole disk, a device-mapper or md device to its slaves (*rotational* when any slave is, so dm-crypt or LVM over a spinning disk counts as spinning whatever its own queue reports), and `queue/rotational` decides.
- *macOS*: `statfs` names network filesystems (`nfs`, `smbfs`, `afpfs`, `webdav`, macFUSE); otherwise `diskutil info -plist <mount>` gives `SolidState`, cached per device for the life of the process.
- *Windows*: a remote drive (`GetDriveTypeW`) is network; otherwise the volume is asked for its seek penalty (`IOCTL_STORAGE_QUERY_PROPERTY`, `StorageDeviceSeekPenaltyProperty`).
- *iOS*: solid-state.
- S3 and rclone storages are network; a directory storage is whatever its path is on; a pool read as a whole (by `fsck --verify` and automatic verification, which take whichever attached disk holds a copy) is the slowest of its attached disks, while each disk read on its own (scrub, `disk check`, `disk insert`) is its own medium.

Anything not recognised is *unknown* and read like a spinning disk: sequential reads are never wrong, only slower.

**Readers** (`crate::parverify`). Each place read is a lane with its own reader threads: rotational and unknown one; solid-state min(4, cores / 2) (at least one); network 8, as many as the uploader threads. One reader takes its lane's items strictly in their order, so a spinning disk is read exactly as before; a pool of hasher threads (at most one per core) hashes what the readers loaded, so even with one reader the hashing of an item overlaps the read of the next. The override is `config.json` `verify_parallelism`, a map from a storage or pool name, `folder:<name>`, `block-cache` or `*` (every place not named) to `"auto"` (the default), `"sequential"` or a number of readers (1 to 32), for when detection is wrong. BLAKE3's own multithreading (`rayon`) is not used: it would add a dependency set the program does not otherwise have, and hashing several objects at once already keeps the cores busy.

**Order and bounds.** Callers hand over their items in the order a sequential loop would read them (keys in order for a scrub target; the planned order for automatic verification; object by storage for `fsck --verify`), and get the results back in that order on their own thread, whatever order the reads complete in. Their loops are unchanged: they count progress, collect damage and advance a scrub cursor only past items they have seen, and may stop at any item (a read error, a storage that became unreachable), after which nothing more is started and whatever was read ahead is dropped unseen. A crash therefore never skips an unchecked item, budgets (planned before reading, section 33; `max_blocks` and `max_bytes` of section 20) are spent exactly, the ledger events, repair queue entries and reports equal those of a sequential run, and live activity counts as before. Memory is bounded: an item's size is reserved from 256 MiB before it is read and returned once it is hashed (an item larger than that is read alone), and nothing more than 256 items ahead of the one awaited is started. Local files are hashed as a stream with a 1 MiB buffer; a file whose recorded entry changes before its turn (restored earlier in the same run) is hashed again in turn.

**Where.** Bit-rot checks of storages, pool disks, local files and the block cache (section 33); `fsck --verify`; automatic verification (section 20); `disk check` (also its pass over objects the index does not know) and the verification step of `disk insert` (section 34). Snapshot completeness checks of backups (section 31) look only at the ledger and read nothing, so they are unchanged.

**Hardware-decoded thumbnails.** Video frames for thumbnails (section 10) are taken with `ffmpeg -hwaccel auto -ss <t> -i <file> -an -frames:v 1 -vf scale=… -f image2pipe -vcodec mjpeg -`: the seek comes before the input, so ffmpeg jumps to the nearest key frame instead of decoding up to the position; with no `-hwaccel_output_format`, hardware-decoded frames are copied back to system memory, where the scale filter and the JPEG encoder run. When that attempt exits with an error or gives no image, the same frame is taken once more in software; when the software attempt then works, the process remembers that hardware decoding fails here and decodes later videos in software straight away (a clip with no frame at one second, or a file ffmpeg cannot read at all, says nothing about the hardware and is not remembered). The 20-second limit applies to each attempt. `config.json` `thumbnails.hwaccel` is `"auto"` (default) or `"off"` (software only). Android and iOS make video frames with their own media frameworks and are unchanged.

**Not done.** Medium detection does not look through ZFS pools, mergerfs or loop devices backed by files; a medium detected wrongly is corrected with `verify_parallelism`, which has no command or interface yet (edit `config.json`). Reads across the disks of one pool read as a whole are not spread per disk. The ffmpeg path does not choose a specific accelerator; `-hwaccel auto` takes the first one ffmpeg finds.

Tests: `media::tests` (fake `/sys` trees: a spinning and a solid-state disk, partitions, dm-crypt over a spinning partition, md over mixed disks, dm over md, dm over an SSD; mountinfo parsing with escaped spaces, network and FUSE types, btrfs through its source device, tmpfs; device number decoding; the `diskutil` plist; combined media), `parverify::tests` (settings and their `config.json` spellings; one reader reads in key order; a spinning lane stays sequential and in order next to a parallel one; SSD lanes read in parallel and hand back in order; a stop is never passed and nothing after it is handed back; memory under the cap and an item larger than the cap; budget picks read exactly; a panicking reader does not hang the caller; an ignored benchmark of 2000 objects of 256 KiB), `thumbs::tests` (with a fake ffmpeg: hardware first, `off`, a hardware failure falls back and is remembered, a short clip does not count against the hardware; settings; an ignored test with a real ffmpeg making a test video with `-f lavfi -i testsrc` and taking its thumbnail both ways), and the unchanged scrub, disk pool, repair, upkeep and verification tests.

## 40. Disk bookkeeping and the next disk to dock

A pool with several disks on a shelf raises the question which one to fetch next. The device keeps per-disk bookkeeping and ranks the disks from it, without reading any disk, so the background service recomputes the ranking after every sync and whenever a disk comes or goes.

**Bookkeeping.** The disk registry of the pool index (section 19) also keeps, per disk, `free_bytes` and `space_measured_utc` next to `capacity_bytes`: the file system's free and total bytes (statvfs; `GetDiskFreeSpaceExW` on Windows) as measured at every attach (at most once a minute while attached), `disk add`, the end of the tidy, verify and fill phases of `disk insert`, `disk check`, after a fill or a push wrote to the disk (when its index is flushed), and `disk eject`. `write_failed_utc` records a write that read back wrong twice (section 34) and is cleared by the next write that reads back right. `varsto disk list --json`, `GET /api/disks` and the rows of the service summary carry, per disk:

- `free_bytes` (live while attached, else the last measurement), `capacity_bytes`, `used_bytes` (by Varsto), `reserve_bytes` (`max(reserve_percent × capacity, min_reserve_bytes)`), `space_measured_utc`;
- `pending_deletes` and `pending_delete_bytes` (deletions queued while the disk was away), `unneeded_objects` and `unneeded_bytes` (copies on the disk of objects the ledger no longer has a record for, or that were dropped from this pool on purpose), and `reclaimable_bytes`, their sum: what the next insert frees;
- `verify_after_days`, `oldest_check_utc` (the oldest per-copy `checked`; 0 when a copy was never checked here, null when the disk holds nothing), `copies_overdue` and `bytes_overdue` (copies whose last check is older than `verify_after_days`, every copy with 0) and `overdue_share` (their share of the disk's copies);
- `only_copy_objects`, `only_copy_bytes` and `only_copy_overdue`: objects this disk holds and no other disk of the pool, and no other storage in the ledger (transferrers do not count), and how many of those are overdue;
- `lacking_bytes`: what the pool lacks that this disk could take, i.e. blocks of current files whose placement takes the pool and that the pool holds on no disk (estimated ciphertext size), plus, under a disk group rule, blocks held in fewer places than the rule asks and not yet in this disk's place; `fill_potential_bytes = min(free − reserve, lacking_bytes)`, 0 for a retired or failing disk;
- `repair_objects` (objects on the disk whose copies are in the repair queue, section 26) and `fetch_objects` and `fetch_bytes` (what a pending step of the fetch plan reads from it, section 34);
- `fsck`: the last forced file system check of the disk's partition from `disk-checks.json`, found through `disks.conf` by pool and label; `failing` and `failing_reason`, set when that check left errors (`uncorrected`) or could not run (`failed`), or when `write_failed_utc` is set; `last_seen_utc`, `last_verified_utc`, `retired`.

The ledger-derived fields (`unneeded_*`, `only_copy_*`, `lacking_bytes`, `fill_potential_bytes`, `repair_objects`, `fetch_*`) are null in the service's 30-second disk listing, which reads only the pool index; `disk list` and `/api/disks` compute them (`Engine::disk_report`). `varsto disk list [--sort name|free|used|checked|reclaimable|seen] [--reverse]` prints a table (name on the sticker, pool, place, state, free / total, reclaimable, oldest check with the overdue share, last seen, file system check). Each column sorts in its useful direction: name A to Z, free, used and reclaimable largest first, checked and seen oldest first; unknown values last, ties by name; `--reverse` flips it.

**Ranking.** `Engine::disk_suggestions` (`varsto disk suggest [--pool P] [--json]`, `GET /api/disk/suggest[?pool=P]`) scores every disk with the sum of these factors, each giving one reason in words:

| Factor | Points |
|---|---|
| Overdue checks | `100 × s × (1 + min(late / verify_after_days, 1))`, where `s` is the overdue share of the disk's bytes and `late` how far the oldest check is past the limit (a factor of 1 with `verify_after_days` 0) |
| Only copy | `80 × only_copy_overdue / copies`, plus 10 when the disk holds any only copy |
| Fetch plan | 70 when a pending step reads from the disk |
| Repair | 50 when a queued repair needs an object on it |
| Fill | `40 × fill_potential_bytes / lacking_bytes` |
| Reclaimable | `30 × min(reclaimable_bytes / (capacity / 3), 1)` |
| File system | 25 when the last check corrected errors, plus `10 × min(days since it / 365, 1)` |
| Unseen | `20 × min(days since last seen / 365, 1)`; 20 for a disk this device never saw |

X bytes overdue give at least `100 × X / used` points and X bytes to free at most `90 × X / capacity`, so at equal sizes a check always outranks reclaiming. Disks that are away are ranked by score, then the one seen longest ago, then label; the first is suggested when it scores at least 5, with its reasons ("«Blue 4TB»: 62 % of its copies (1.2 TB) were last checked over 180 days ago (oldest 2025-11-02); 41.0 GB can be freed; last seen 3 months ago") and the command to run next, `varsto disk insert "Blue 4TB"`. Attached disks are scored the same way and listed apart (they need checking, not docking). Retired and failing disks are never suggested; they are listed with a warning (and with how many objects exist only on them). The JSON answer is `{suggestions: {computed_utc, top, ranked, attached, excluded, overdue}, settings: {notify_overdue}}`; each suggestion has `pool, label, disk_id, place, attached, score, reasons: [{kind, points, text}], last_seen_utc, summary, command`.

**Reminder.** `config.json` `disk_suggest.notify_overdue` (default on; `varsto disk suggest --remind on|off`, `POST /api/disk/suggest/settings {notify_overdue}`, a checkbox on the Storages page) lets the service raise a desktop notification at most once a week (`<home>/state/disk-reminder.json`) when a disk that is away has overdue copies, naming the disk, its reasons and the command.

**Interface.** Each pool card on the Storages page shows its disks as a table whose column headers (name, place, free space, used, reclaimable, last checked, last seen, file system) sort it, the choice kept in the browser; above it "Suggested next: «Blue 4TB» — reasons" with the `varsto disk insert` command and a copy button, since the interface does not mount drives. An attached disk that scores is offered with Check now (`disk check`), and retired or failing disks are shown with their warnings. In English, Finnish and Swedish.

Tests: `crates/varsto-core/src/disksuggest.rs` (overdue beats reclaimable at equal size, later checks and larger shares weigh more, only-copy risk, retired and failing disks excluded, ties by last seen with attached disks apart, fill, fetch and fsck reasons, sorting by every column, quoting), `crates/varsto-core/src/pool.rs` (every bookkeeping field from the index and given ledger facts, a failing write recorded and cleared, a retired disk takes nothing), `crates/varsto-core/tests/disk_pool.rs` (space measured at add, check, insert and eject and kept while away; overdue copies, queued deletions and what the pool lacks; the suggestion, the weekly reminder, a failed fsck excluding a disk), `crates/varsto-cli/src/desktop.rs` (the API) and `crates/varsto-cli/tests/cli_smoke.rs` (`disk list --sort`, `disk suggest`).

## 41. Recovery package guide and the progress bar at the top

**Recovery package guide.** `varsto recovery guide [--format text|markdown|html] [--output FILE]` (`--json` for the structure) and `GET /api/recovery/guide` (`?format=text` for the English text) describe what to keep outside the devices so that the whole vault can be restored after every device is lost, and the restore steps for a new device. User documentation: [docs/recovery.md](../recovery.md). The guide is built from this device's configuration and keyring only (`Engine::recovery_guide`, `recovery_guide::build`, which is pure): storages (endpoint, bucket, region, prefix, class, place; rclone remote and the section of `rclone.conf`; directory paths; transferrers marked as no recovery source), disk pools (disks by label and place, last seen, the copies rule, the matching `disks.conf` lines), Strongroom folders (enrolled key labels; one key or a software key is flagged), the organization (name, administrators), backups, the other devices and replicas, and restic settings when `config.json` has them (the repository with any `user:password@` replaced by placeholders, the paths of the password and credentials files, never their contents or the password command). No secret is read: not the vault key, the S3 secret keys (nor the access key IDs), rclone's tokens or the restic password. Printing the kit records `state/recovery-kit.json` (`printed_utc`, `key_epoch`, `shares`), so the kit item is "done", "out of date" (printed before a key epoch change) or left to the user.

**Help page.** `GET /api/help` and `varsto help-now` (`--json` for the structure) tell where this device stands and what to do next: the phase (`no_vault`, `locked`, `ready`; the endpoint answers without a vault and while the vault is locked), where the data is (devices, storages with their quota and drain state, folders with the storages and devices holding them, bytes by kept copies, pool disks, trash, backups, restic), what needs attention (policies, ledger, repair and bit-rot findings, quotas, drains, overdue disks, paused or stopped service, failed snapshots, low disk space, organization approvals, recovery kit not printed or out of date), and the next steps chosen by fixed rules, most urgent first, each with why, how, an interface action (page and element or dialog) and the command line equivalent. It is built by `help::build` (pure) from `Engine::help_inputs`, which reads only the configuration, the ledger view, the folder states, the cached trash and what the service already measured: no storage is listed and nothing goes over the network. Sentences are `{t, p}` templates like the recovery guide's, translated by the interface.

Every sentence is `{t, p}`: an English template with `{name}` placeholders and their values. The command line renders the templates in English; the interface translates the template like any other string (`ui/i18n/<lang>.json`) and inserts the values untranslated, so the facts come from one place and the wording is translated with the rest of the interface. Commands are shell lines with `<...>` placeholders and are never translated; each `varsto` line is parsed with the command-line definition in the tests. The interface page (Recovery package; Settings links to it) keeps the user's ticks in `localStorage` per vault, prints through a print stylesheet that leaves only the checklist, and downloads the checklist as text in the page's language with the ticks.

Restore order in the guide: install and verify the release; rebuild the words from shares if needed; `varsto join` (it asks for the words) through a hot bucket, else a directory (an rclone-only vault mounts the remote first, since join takes a directory or a bucket); the organization's devices (an administrator adds the new device; the root words appoint administrators only among listed devices); the other storages under their old names; pool disks one at a time (`disk insert` with the saved `disks.conf` lines on Linux); folders (`folder attach`, Strongroom folders selective, then `strongroom unlock`); backups (`backup restore`); files only on cold storage (`repair --from-cold`, `move --confirm-cold-read`); revoking the lost devices and printing a new kit. A restic repository is listed as a way without Varsto.

**Progress bar at the top.** The interface shows, under the top bar, this device's task that has run for 5 seconds or more (the oldest when several, "N tasks · ..." in front): its sentence from the same templates as the Now card, the percentage and the expected finish, a determinate bar or a striped one when the total is unknown, `role="progressbar"` with `aria-valuenow` (absent when unknown) and `aria-valuetext`. When the last task ends it shows "Done" for a second and hides. `GET /api/activity?mine=1` answers with this device's tasks only and does not make the service ask the peers; the page asks every second while the bar shows or a request the user started is running, every five seconds otherwise (on Overview and Peers the Now card's two-second answers feed it), and not while the page is hidden. New task kinds: `download` with step `file` (fetching one file: `folder fetch`, the interface's download, a placeholder) and `export` (decrypting a file of a folder kept encrypted on the device), `folder_purge` (deleting a removed folder's data from the storages) and `strongroom_convert` (re-encrypting a folder into a Strongroom or under a new key).

Tests: `recovery_guide::tests`, `crates/varsto-core/tests/recovery_guide.rs` (a vault with a directory, a pool with a disk and its `disks.conf` line, a cold archive bucket, a hot bucket with a prefix, an rclone remote, a Strongroom and restic settings: every item and step present, the kit status before and after printing and after a key change, a member device, and the vault key, words, passphrase, S3 secret and access key ID, restic password and repository password found in none of the text, Markdown, HTML and JSON forms), `varsto` binary tests `recovery_guide_tests` (every `varsto` command of guides for a bucket, directory, rclone-only and member vault parses with the CLI), `crates/varsto-core/tests/activity_coverage.rs` (fetching one large file and purging a folder are tasks with their totals).

## 42. Importing git-annex repositories, cloud drives and local directories

**Why.** Data that already lives elsewhere (dozens of disks holding clones of git-annex repositories, a Dropbox or OneDrive account, an old drive) should come under Varsto's management without being copied by hand, and without anything at the source being changed except, on request, emptying a local directory once its files are safe. `crates/varsto-core/src/import/` reads the sources, `engine/imports.rs` brings them in.

**One folder per source.** Each source becomes one Varsto folder, recognised by its *identity*: a git-annex repository by the root commits of its `git-annex` branch (every clone shares them, so each disk holding a clone continues the same folder; a repository whose branch has two roots after two independently created repositories were merged matches either); an rclone remote by `rclone:<type>:<remote name>:<path>`; a local directory by `dir:<absolute path>`. A new folder is named by `--folder`, else after the source (the repository's directory name, the last path component of the remote path or "Dropbox"/"OneDrive"/"Google Drive" for a whole drive, the directory name). An existing folder of that name that holds anything else is refused; a source imported before always continues its own folder (a different `--folder` is refused).

**The import book.** Per folder, `state/imports/<folder>.json` holds: `kind` and `identity`; `display` (where it came from); `repositories` (git-annex UUID to description, from every `uuid.log`) and `dead` (from `trust.log`); `imported` (content id to `{hash, size, utc, from, verified, sha256?}`: the folder's keyed content hash of the file, where it was read from, whether it was checked against the source's hash, and for local directories the SHA-256 of what was read); `damaged` (content id to the copies whose content did not match: `{from, path, utc, detail}`); `tree` (path to `{id, size, mtime_ns}`, the source's files as last listed), `tree_ref` (a commit id, or the listing time) and `tree_utc`; `missing_holders` (content ids not imported, to the git-annex UUIDs whose location logs claim them); `gone_at_source` (paths listed before and gone now); `runs` (per copy of the source, the git-annex UUID or the identity: name, location, device, last time, ids present, imported, bytes, damaged, failed, and whether everything it held was in the vault afterwards); `removals` (local directories: `{path, size, sha256, removed_utc}` per removed source file) and `source_path`. Content ids: a git-annex key; `git:<blob id>` for a file committed to git itself; `rclone:<hash type>:<hash>:<path>` or `rclone:<size>:<mtime ns>:<path>` without a hash; `dir:<size>:<mtime ns>:<path>`.

The book is synced: each device publishes its whole book as `vault/imports/<folder>/<device>/<written utc, 20 digits>.enc` on its metadata storages, zstd-compressed JSON `{device, folder, written_utc, book}` sealed with XChaCha20-Poly1305 under `folder metadata key.derive("import-book", [folder id])` with associated data `("import-book", vault, folder, device, written)`, deleting its older records (the pattern of the access records, section 13). Readers take the newest record per device, skip removed devices, and merge: identities, repositories, imported ids, damage and removals are unions (the first `imported` entry of an id is kept), the file list with the newest `tree_utc` wins, runs keep the newest per copy. A device continuing an import first merges the others' books, so another device can carry on from other disks. A book is published at the end of a run and every ten minutes during one. Why not the ledger: the book is replaced as a whole, can be large, and only matters to devices that import.

**A run.** `Engine::import_run(source, options)`: merge the books; find or create the folder; attach it here when it is not (selectively by default: imported files are placeholders and their blocks are only on the storages; with `--dest DIR` without `--selective`, a full copy in `DIR`; a folder kept encrypted on this device is refused) and pull it; list the source; plan; read; save. The plan, per listed path: the folder already has it with the imported content hash: done. A different file is at the path in Varsto (edited after the import, or there before it), or the path was deleted in Varsto after it was imported and the source did not change: kept as it is and reported (Varsto's side wins; nothing at the source is lost either). An unknown file (or placeholder) in the way on this device: kept and reported. Content already imported under another path: the new path gets the same blocks without reading (linked). Content readable here: read. Otherwise: *awaiting import*. Every content id is read once, however many paths use it.

**Reading.** Content is streamed: chunked with the block size for its size, hashed with the folder's keyed hash, encrypted and written to the folder's write targets (placement and pools as for a push; a pool without an attached disk or without room is left out for the rest of the run; transferrers are not written) by four upload threads, while the source's hash is computed over the same bytes: git-annex `SHA256`, `SHA512`, `SHA384`, `SHA224`, `SHA1`, `MD5` and `BLAKE2B160/224/256/384/512`, `BLAKE2S256` keys and their `E` variants (the hash is the key name up to the first dot); `git-sha1:<size>` / `git-sha256:<size>` blob ids; the rclone hashes `dropbox` (SHA-256 over the SHA-256 of each 4 MiB block), `quickxor` (Microsoft's QuickXorHash, 160 bits, little-endian, hex), `sha256`, `sha1`, `md5` and `blake3`. The size is checked against the key's or the listing's size. Only when everything matches does the content count: `chunk_stored` events for every copy written (and `chunk_on_device` with a full local copy), and a new version of every path that uses it (a `FileState` with this device's clock, as a local edit). On a mismatch nothing counts: objects this content alone put on the storages are deleted again, the copy is recorded as damaged with its key, path and the two hashes, and the paths stay awaiting import, to be taken from another disk. A read error is reported as failed. Content without a hash (WORM and URL keys, remotes without hashes, local files) is imported unverified and counted as such. `--no-verify` skips the checks. With a selective attachment every block must have reached a storage, or the run stops (nothing would hold it).

**Awaiting import.** A path whose content is not readable here is never put in a manifest: other devices see neither an empty file nor a deletion, and a device that does not know about imports sees nothing at all. It is listed by the book: `varsto folder files` and the interface's file list show it with state `awaiting_import` and the size (and time) from the source (`Engine::list_files_with_pending`); `varsto import status --missing` lists them. A later run (any device, any disk) that can read the content completes them.

**Resuming.** During a run, every 500 imported ids or 20 seconds, the ledger events are committed first, then the folder state, then the book is saved (so the book never names content the state lacks); the manifest and the book are published every ten minutes and at the end, also when the run fails. A run that was interrupted is simply started again: ids in the book are not read again, and an id read but not yet saved is read once more (its new version is identical and no new version is made).

**git-annex.** Read with git plumbing only (`GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0`): `git config annex.uuid`, `git for-each-ref` (the local `git-annex` branch and every `refs/remotes/*/git-annex`), `git rev-list --max-parents=0`, `git ls-tree -r -z -l --full-tree <commit>` (default HEAD; `--ref` a branch or tag), and one `git cat-file --batch` for symlink targets, small blobs (pointer files `/annex/objects/<key>`, files kept in git) and the logs. A key's object is `.git/annex/objects/<hashdir>/<key>/<key>` with `hashDirMixed` (non-bare) or `hashDirLower` (bare; both are tried), read directly in on-disk path order. No git-annex command runs; no lock is taken; nothing is written to the repository. Symbolic links that are not annexed files and submodules are reported, not imported. After a run the missing ids are looked up in the location logs (`<hashDirLower>/<key>.log` on every git-annex branch; per UUID the newest line, present when its status is `1`; this repository and dead ones left out) and summed per repository: "Insert next: «disk-07 photos» (has 1 234 of the missing files, 210 GiB)". Location logs can be out of date, which the report says. git-annex metadata (`.log.met`) is not imported: Varsto has no tags on files yet.

**rclone.** The user's own rclone (`VARSTO_RCLONE`, else `rclone`) with the user's configuration (`RCLONE_CONFIG` passes through). `varsto import dropbox|onedrive` check the remote's type with `rclone config dump`, of which only `type` is kept (tokens are dropped unread), and print how to create one (`rclone config create <name> dropbox`; the sign-in is the user's own step in the browser) when it is missing. The hash type is the provider's own (dropbox: `dropbox`, onedrive: `quickxor`, drive: `sha256`, s3 and similar: `md5`, b2, box, pcloud, mega: `sha1`; other backends: the first of those `rclone backend features` lists; none for local, alias, crypt and other wrappers) unless `--hash-type` says otherwise. Listing: `rclone lsjson -R --files-only --no-mimetype [--hash-type T]`, read line by line as rclone writes it. Entries without a size (Google Docs, Dropbox Paper, OneNote: documents only the provider opens) are reported, not imported; names are imported as they are (section 46; only empty, `.`, `..` and NUL components and names starting with `.varsto` are reported); names that differ only in case are imported and noted. Reading: batches of up to 256 files or 1 GiB copied with one `rclone copy --files-from-raw <list> --transfers N --checkers N --no-traverse` into a staging directory (one process per batch: 300 small files took 0.07 s this way against 19 s with one `rclone cat` each, measured with the local backend), each file handed over and deleted. The staging directory is on the destination folder's filesystem, `<folder>/.varsto-import-staging/batch-<pid>/` (hidden from scans like every `.varsto` name), or in the directory `--staging DIR` names; earlier versions used `state/import-downloads/` in the device directory, which could fill the system disk. Before each batch the staging filesystem must have the batch's size plus 1 GiB free, else the import stops with a message naming the directory, what is free and what is needed (free some space or give `--staging` on a disk with room; the next run continues where this one stopped). The batch directory is removed after every batch and however the run ends, and the default staging directory with it. A file the batch did not put where its name says (rclone writes `:` and the like as look-alike characters on Windows) is copied once more under a plain staging name with `rclone copyto`. A file that does not match its hash is downloaded again once (`rclone copyto`) before it counts as damaged. `--transfers` defaults to 4. Running the import again brings changed files over as new versions and skips unchanged ones (same id); files deleted at the source are kept in Varsto and reported (there is no `--mirror-deletes` yet; it could move them to the trash, section 43).

**Local directories.** Walked in path order without following symbolic links (reported, never followed or removed), special files and unreadable entries reported, names starting with `.varsto` left out. A source that is, holds or lies inside an attached Varsto folder or the Varsto home is refused, as are a destination inside the source and a source inside the destination.

**Removing source files** (`varsto import dir --remove-source`, `varsto import cleanup <folder>`, the interface's checkbox). Opt in only; refused for git-annex repositories (a `.git/annex` or `annex/objects` directory) besides the refusals above. A file is removed only when, checked at removal time: (1) it is a regular file reached without links, with the size and modification time recorded at import, and its SHA-256 read now equals the one recorded; (2) the folder's state has that path with the imported content hash; (3) every block has a copy claimed on a storage that is not a transferrer, and one such copy was read back now and hashed to its object name; (4) the folder's policy (its own, else the default policy of section 14) evaluates to `ok` for this file's blocks (`policy::evaluate` over these blocks only, so "verified by another device within N days" must hold for each). A folder whose policy was cleared needs (1) to (3). Files that do not qualify stay and are listed with the reason ("waiting for the policy (…): 3 chunks were not verified by another device within 30 days"); they go on a later run or `import cleanup` once eligible. Removal deletes the file, then directories left empty up to (never including) the source root. Each removal is logged in the book. The command line lists the count and bytes and asks before removing unless `--yes`; `import cleanup --dry-run` and a plan remove nothing.

**Status and completion.** `varsto import status [<folder>] [--missing]`: per imported folder the identity, files and content ids total, imported, missing (and bytes), awaiting, damaged ids still without a good copy and damaged copies seen, files gone at the source, every copy read (name, location, last import, imported, damaged, complete), which copies to insert next, and removals. `varsto import verify <folder>` (exit code 1 unless complete): every path of the source's list is in the folder with the imported content, every block has a storage copy and the folder's policy is met, with the copies of the source that held nothing the vault lacks; then the source (an annex disk) is no longer needed. Nothing at a source is ever deleted by verify.

**Surfaces.** `varsto import git-annex <repo> [--ref R] [--no-verify]`, `varsto import rclone|dropbox|onedrive <remote:path> [--hash-type T] [--transfers N] [--staging DIR]`, `varsto import dir <dir> [--remove-source] [--yes]`, each with `[--folder NAME] [--dest DIR] [--selective] [--dry-run] [--json]`; without `--dest` a folder that is not attached here goes selectively to the folder root (`VARSTO_FOLDER_ROOT`, else `~/Varsto/<folder>`). The background service is paused during an import. Progress: an activity task (section 32) of kind `import` with the steps `list`, `read` (bytes) and `cleanup` (files), shown as a progress line on a terminal. API: `GET /api/import/status`, `POST /api/import/dir {source, folder, dest, selective, remove_source}` (the report, with the removal plan as `cleanup` when asked), `POST /api/import/cleanup {folder, apply}`. The interface has an Import page: a local folder is imported there (destination, the removal checkbox with its conditions, the report, the files waiting and a button to remove the eligible ones after a confirmation); git-annex and cloud imports are shown with their commands. English, Finnish and Swedish.

**Limits.**

- A git-annex repository's content is matched by key; files committed to git directly are read from the git objects. Special remotes, the git-annex journal (uncommitted log changes), metadata and unlocked files' working-tree copies are not read (an unlocked file's content is read from the annex object store, where git-annex keeps it).
- Location logs may be stale or missing for disks never synced with this clone; the suggestions are hints.
- Names are kept as they are (section 46): `12:30.jpg` from a git-annex repository or a cloud drive keeps its name. Only names that are not UTF-8, have an empty, `.` or `..` component or a NUL, or start with `.varsto`, are reported as not imported. The report counts the names a Windows device writes under a mapped local name (`mapped_on_windows`, `N name(s) ... hold characters Windows does not allow` on the command line); that is information only.
- The source's file list is kept in memory and in the book (about 200 bytes per file); a listing of millions of files needs that much memory.
- One run at a time per folder; a run holds the engine.
- Content whose hash cannot be checked (WORM and URL keys, local files, remotes without hashes) is imported as read.

Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/import_annex.rs` (a repository with locked, unlocked, WORM, git-only, duplicate and nested files built with git-annex; a clone on a second "disk"; an interrupted run resumed; a damaged object on disk one taken from disk two; awaiting files listed with the disk that has them; another device continuing from disk two without reading anything already imported; the result byte-identical through a third device; `import status`, `--missing` and `verify`; a name clash refused), `crates/varsto-core/tests/import_rclone.rs` (the local backend with a temporary configuration: Dropbox hashes checked, a re-run reads nothing, a changed file comes over, a deleted one is kept and reported, a wrong hash retried and reported with its objects deleted, a case-only difference noted, a missing or mistyped remote; 10 000 files ignored by default), `crates/varsto-core/tests/import_dir.rs` (removal only when eligible, a file changed after import kept, files waiting for another device's verification removed once it happened, empty directories pruned and the root kept, a link never followed, refusals, dry runs), and unit tests of keys, hash directories, logs, pointer files, Dropbox and QuickXor vectors (from rclone), lsjson lines, times and book merging.

## 43. Trash (0.0.1-alpha.14)

**What it is.** A file deleted on any device of the vault is kept in the vault's *trash* for a number of days (the *trash days*: 30 unless set otherwise, per vault and per folder) and can be restored on every device that holds the folder key, from the storages. Before this the only trash was the local copy a device keeps when it applies another device's deletion (section 7); that copy remains, follows the same days, and is the fast way to restore when present.

**Records.** A trash entry is the last live version of a deleted file: path, size, modification time, content hash, block list (chunk id, object name, size, key epoch), deletion time (UTC), deleting device and the Lamport clock of the deletion; its id is the first 16 bytes of BLAKE3 over a fixed label, folder, path, device and clock. Each device keeps the entries of the deletions *it* found (a scan that sees a file gone) and the entries it took out of the trash (*removals*: id, time, device, restored or purged, deletion time) in one *trash record* per folder, `vault/trash/<folder>/<device>/<written utc, 20 digits>.enc`: zstd JSON sealed with XChaCha20-Poly1305 under `derive(folder metadata key, "trash-record", folder)`, associated data `trash-record`, vault, folder, device, time; written to every metadata storage when it changed (with the push that found the deletion), the device's older records deleted, the newest one per device read (like access records, section 13). Readers open it with any metadata key of the folder (members of a shared folder too), skip devices they do not know or that were removed, and accept an entry only if the writer is the deleting device, its id recomputes and its path and block sizes pass the manifest checks (section 28). A file whose content is still in the folder under another path when the deletion is found (a rename or move) is not trashed. Why not the tombstones of the manifests: a tombstone carries no blocks and is replaced as soon as a new file takes the path, while the trash must keep the deleted version also then.

**The trash of a folder** is the union of the entries of every device's newest record, less the entries any device removed, less those older than the trash days. On an owner device the removals written by members of a shared folder (other people) do not count, so another person cannot take entries out of the owner's trash. A record keeps an entry, and a removal, seven days past the entry's expiry so every device sees it go; then the writer forgets it.

**Settings.** `vault/trash-settings/<device>/<utc>.enc` holds the days, the days per folder and the trash storage (its name on the writing device and its identity, `vault/storage-id.json`, so devices that name it differently find it); JSON sealed under `derive(folder-record key, "trash-settings")`, associated data `trash-settings`, vault, device, time; the newest record of a trusted device wins. Members of shared folders cannot read or change them and keep 30 days. Days run from 1 to 3650; a folder set to 0 follows the vault again.

**Blocks of the trash are in use.** Until an entry expires or is purged its blocks count like those of a current file:

- a data move of a whole folder (section 23) moves them too (a move of idle files does not);
- removing a storage (section 20) copies first every block of the trash that would otherwise lose its last copy (one copy is required; the folder's copy rules are for current files);
- `fsck` counts them as referenced (not as unreferenced objects) and counts a claim on a cold storage, which it does not list, as a copy;
- a device that holds a file with the same content again, or a restored file, writes the blocks back to the folder's storages: a copy on the trash storage does not keep a current file's block off them (`WriteTargets::wants`).

Policies (section 14) and repair (section 26) are about current files; the trash's blocks are not counted there.

**Dropping blocks.** When an entry expires or is purged, its blocks are queued on the device that sees it (`state/trash/drops.json`). Before blocks are judged the device pulls every attached folder, so a file restored or created again elsewhere is known. A queued block is dropped only if no current file, no version still to be downloaded, no entry still in the trash and no other folder (a backup included) uses its object; then `chunk_dropped` is recorded for every storage of this device that holds it, the batch is sealed and pushed, and only then is the object deleted where no newer claim exists (as for data moves, section 23). On a cold storage whose price model has a minimum storage duration (`varsto storage price`), a block is not deleted before that many days after it was stored there; it waits in the queue. Folders not attached here, or that could not be pulled, are judged later. Each device drops what is on its own storages; one that never runs leaves its copies in place (space, not data, is lost).

**Trash storage.** `varsto trash set --storage NAME` names a storage of the vault, typically a cheap or cold one. It takes no blocks of current files (unless a folder's placement names it). The upkeep copies each block that only trash entries use to it (from the block cache, a warm storage or a peer), checks the copy (read back from a warm storage; by name only on a cold one, as data moves do), records `chunk_stored` (and `chunk_verified` when read back), records `chunk_dropped` for this device's other warm storages, seals and pushes, and then deletes those copies. Copies on other cold storages stay. A device that does not have the trash storage keeps the trashed blocks where they are. Setting a trash storage runs the upkeep at once.

**Restore.** `varsto trash restore <folder> <path>... [--to DIR] [--confirm-cold-read]` restores entries: a path names a file or every entry under a directory; per path the newest deletion. A file goes back to its own path when that is free (no current file, no version waiting, nothing on disk or as a placeholder), otherwise to `name (restored).ext`, `name (restored 2).ext`, ...; with `--to` under that directory of the folder (the part of the path below the selected path's parent kept). Nothing is overwritten. The content comes from this device's local trash copy when one has the entry's size and content hash, otherwise from the blocks (block cache, peers, the storages), each checked against its object name and chunk id, the whole file against the content hash. A block that no warm storage of this device and no cache holds but a cold storage does needs `--confirm-cold-read`; without it the restore stops before reading anything and says how much is on which cold storage and what reading it costs (`trash_restore_plan`). The restored file is written into the folder under its local name (section 46; local trash copies are looked for under the local form of their names too), or into the block cache in a folder kept encrypted here, with the deleted version's modification time and pushed as a new version, so every device gets it and its blocks go back to the folder's storages; the entry is recorded as restored and leaves the trash everywhere; the local trash copy used is removed.

**Purge.** `varsto trash purge <folder> <path>... | --all [--yes]` removes entries for good (removal "purged") and drops their blocks as above at once, on this device's storages; other devices drop theirs at their next upkeep. Local trash copies of the entries are deleted.

**Upkeep.** `Engine::trash_upkeep` runs in the background service once a day after a sync (`varsto trash upkeep` runs it by hand): read every device's records and the settings, queue the blocks of entries that expired or were purged since, forget long-expired entries in this device's record and publish it, move trashed blocks to the trash storage, drop what is queued, and delete local trash copies older than the trash days.

**Commands, interface, activity.** `varsto trash list [--folder F] [--json]` (path, size, when and by which device deleted, expiry, whether a local copy exists, whether restoring needs a cold read), `varsto trash status` (settings, totals, bytes per storage, local copies, next expiry, blocks waiting), `varsto trash set [--days N] [--folder F --days N] [--storage NAME | --no-storage]`, restore, purge and upkeep as above; the API is `/api/trash` (`GET`, `?folder=`), `/api/trash/status`, `POST /api/trash/restore-plan`, `/api/trash/restore`, `/api/trash/purge`, `/api/trash/set`, `/api/trash/upkeep`. The interface has a Trash page (under More on phones): search and folder filter, restore to the original place or another directory (a dialog with the fee warning before a cold read), delete permanently, empty the trash (typed confirmation), and the days and trash storage. Activity kinds `trash_restore`, `trash_purge` and `trash_move`.

**Limits.**

- Only deletions found by a version with the trash are recorded; deletions made earlier are only in the local trash copies.
- A file overwritten by a new version is not in the trash: only deletions are; the version history (section 50) keeps replaced versions.
- A Strongroom conversion or re-key (section 17) moves the folder to a new id; its trash stays with the old id and is not reached, and the old blocks are deleted by the conversion.
- Removing a folder with `--purge` deletes its trash records with the rest; without `--purge` they stay on the storages.
- The race of any garbage collection remains: a device that wrote a file with the same content as a purged or expired entry, and did not upload a block because the ledger showed it stored, finds the block missing only after the drop is in the ledger; its next push writes it back (`upload_missing` checks every current file against the ledger). Restored files are not affected (their entries are never queued).
- Repair (section 26) does not rewrite copies on the trash storage, and filling a pool disk takes current files only.
- Members of a shared folder see the owner's trash of that folder and can restore from it; their purges and restores do not take entries out of the owner's trash, and they cannot change the settings.
- A device removed from the vault: its records are no longer read, so its deletions leave the trash (their blocks stay until another device's storage removal or folder purge).

Tests: `varsto_core::engine::trash::tests` (restored names, path selection, checks of other devices' entries), `crates/varsto-core/tests/trash.rs` (a deletion on device A is listed on device B at once with device and expiry; B restores from the storages after losing its local copy, byte for byte, to the original path, and A gets the file back while the entry leaves both trashes; a path taken again gives `name (restored).ext`; a whole directory restores into another directory; a rename is not trashed; the local trash copy is used when the storage lost every block and is cleared after the trash days; a whole-folder data move takes trashed blocks along, storage removal copies them first, `fsck` counts them, and the file restores afterwards; a folder's own days postpone expiry, after the vault's days expired blocks are dropped (recorded in the ledger) while blocks a current file uses again and the kept file's blocks stay; purging one entry and emptying the trash on B leave only the kept file's blocks and empty A's list; a cold trash storage receives no current blocks, takes the trashed ones off the warm storage, B's restore is refused without the cold read and succeeds with it, the restored blocks are back on the warm storage, and a purge waits for the cold minimum duration until a later upkeep; the daily schedule) and `crates/varsto-cli/tests/trash_cli.rs` (list, status, set, restore of a directory, purge with and without confirmation, upkeep).

## 44. Storage quotas and draining a storage

**Why.** Switching providers means emptying one storage onto the others, and that must never leave a folder with fewer copies than its policy asks for, not even for a moment. Some storages also have a limit (an S3 bucket with a quota, a plan of so many terabytes) that Varsto must not run into.

**Shared storage settings.** A storage's quota and drain are vault-wide: a quota only one device knows would be broken by the others' uploads, and a storage being emptied must stop receiving blocks from every device. Prices stay a device's own estimate in `config.json` (section 20); these settings are published like a folder's placement (section 23). Every change is one sealed record, `vault/storage-settings/<storage key>/<device>/<updated_utc>.enc` (20-digit time), on every storage that holds records, drained ones included; in an organization it is signed as placement records are (`storage-settings-signature` over vault, storage key, device, time and ciphertext hash; the signed envelope's `folder` field carries the storage key). It is encrypted under the folder-record key of the current epoch with AAD `storage-settings-record` + vault id + storage key, and contains `{storage, name, device, updated_utc, settings: {quota_bytes?, quota_objects?, drain?: {phase: draining|drained, started_utc, driver, remove_when_empty, budget_bytes_per_day?, confirm_cold_read, drained_utc?}}}`. The newest record of a storage wins on every device (`pull_registry`); records from devices that are not trusted, or, in an organization, from non-administrators when members may not add storages, are ignored. A device keeps its copy in `config.json` (`storage_settings`, by its own storage name, with the record time, also when the settings are empty so an older record does not come back).

The *storage key* names a storage the same way on every device although names are local: the storage identity of section 20 (`vault/storage-id.json`) when it can be read (cached in `state/storage-keys.json`), the pool identity for a disk pool, and otherwise (a cold class, offline) a keyed hash of the name under a key derived from the vault key. While any storage has settings, every device publishes its storage names with their identities (section 20) and keeps the other devices' names for its own storages (`state/storage-aliases.json`), so that usage and drains count copies the ledger records under another device's name for the same storage.

**Quotas.** `quota_bytes` (and optionally `quota_objects`) is the most Varsto keeps on the storage. Usage is counted from the ledger: every object claimed there and not dropped, once, under any device's name for the storage, plus an estimate of the records, ledger batches, manifests and thumbnails (the size of this device's ledger plus 64 KiB and 8 objects per folder, or what `varsto storage quota <name> --measure` found by listing the storage and reading its records). Writers keep 1 % of the quota free (`usable = quota - quota/100`) and take room per object as they plan writes:

- a push writes a block to every storage its placement takes that has room; a storage without room is skipped, the push report names it (`storages_full`: "storage X is at its quota (Y of Z)") and the block goes to the folder's other storages; when none has room the block stays on this device (`chunks_without_storage_copy`) and the next push tries again;
- a data move (section 23) keeps a block whose new storage has no room, and so does moving trashed blocks to the trash storage (section 43; a trash storage being drained takes none); storage removal (`plan_storage_removal`) copies only to storages with room; repair re-writes existing copies and is not limited;
- the status (`storage list`, `/api/storage/quotas`) shows `used_bytes`, `used_objects`, `percent`, and `state` `ok`, `warning` (90 % or more) or `full`; the background service notifies once when a sync found a storage at its quota, and a policy that is at risk or violated lists the full storages among its reasons ("... : new blocks cannot go there").

Disk pools keep space free on each disk instead (section 19) and transferrers empty themselves, so neither takes a quota.

**Draining.** `varsto storage drain <name>` publishes `drain` with `phase: draining` and this device as the *driver*. From then on no device writes new blocks there: `WriteTargets::wants` refuses a storage being drained or drained, for pushes, backups, repair, disk inserts and data moves, and a placement that names only storages being drained falls back to every storage. The storage stays readable. The driver works through the blocks in runs (the service runs one after each sync, at most 4096 blocks or 2 GiB per run, and the command line runs one at once unless `--background`), within `budget_bytes_per_day` (bytes copied to other storages per UTC day; the first block of a day always goes):

1. Every block some claim places on the storage, under any device's name for it (section 20 identities), is worked out from the ledger. The copies that stay are the other claimed copies on storages this device knows (transferrers excluded, replicas included). The folder's copy rules (`min_copies`, `min_per_place`; not the verification window) are capped at what the copies met *before*, the storage being drained included, exactly as for a move (section 23): a drain never makes a policy weaker, and does not make a policy it did not meet a reason to keep a copy elsewhere.
2. A block of a current file (or a known snapshot) is copied to every storage the folder's placement takes (drained storages excepted) that lacks it and has room under its quota; a block nothing uses any more only needs one copy elsewhere. Then the rules must hold for the copies that stay plus the planned ones, and there must be at least one; otherwise the block stays and the reason is reported ("folder X needs 2 copies in external storage (S3 and the like) and no other storage there can take them: add another cloud storage or lower the policy", with "(storage Y is at its quota (...))" when a quota is why). A block whose only readable copy is on a cold storage being drained stays until `confirm_cold_read`.
3. Blocks that only need dropping come first (no egress), then the others oldest first. The ciphertext comes from the files on this device (re-encrypted, which is deterministic), the block cache, another readable storage, or the storage being drained. Each new copy is written, read back and hash-checked (a cold target is checked by name only), and recorded (`ChunkStored`, `ChunkVerified`); the rules are checked again with the copies actually made; then the copy is recorded as dropped under every name it was claimed under (`ChunkDropped`). Each group of 64 blocks is sealed and pushed as one ledger batch before any of its objects is deleted from the storage, and an object still claimed there, or claimed on a storage this device cannot match to one of its own, is not deleted.
4. When no claim places a block on the storage, the block objects still there that no claim counts are deleted (drops recorded by an interrupted run), and every other object on the storage except its identity (`vault/storage-id.json`) is copied to every other storage that holds records and lacks it: ledger batches, manifests, records, snapshots and thumbnails. If there is no such storage, or a record can be read only there (a cold class), the drain waits and says so. Then `phase: drained` is published: no device writes to the storage or reads records from it any more (`metadata_storages` leaves it out). With `remove_when_empty` the driver then removes it from its configuration as `storage remove` does (`StorageRetired`); otherwise it stays, empty and marked drained, until the user removes it or stops the drain to use it again.

`--stop` (`POST /api/storage/drain {name, stop: true}`) publishes the settings without `drain`: the storage takes new blocks again, and blocks already moved stay where they are (a dropped copy is not written back while another exists, section 23). Running `storage drain <name>` on another device makes that device the driver. Progress is kept by the driver in `state/drains.json` (bytes when it started, bytes today, bytes moved, the reasons blocks wait); `varsto storage drain status` and `GET /api/storage/drains` show phase (`draining`, `blocked` when the last run moved nothing because of those reasons, `drained`), blocks and bytes left, percent by bytes, an estimated finish (the pace so far, at most the daily budget), the budget and the reasons. The live activity of a run is kind `drain`, steps `copy` and `records` (section 32).

`varsto storage drain <name> --plan` and `GET /api/storage/drain-plan?name=` show the plan without changing anything: blocks and bytes on the storage, blocks that are only dropped, blocks copied first with the bytes per receiving storage, bytes read from the storage itself with their egress and retrieval cost from the price data (section 20), what the storage still bills for its minimum storage duration, days at the daily budget, whether every folder's policy holds without it and why not, and notes (cold storage, quotas, cold targets). The interface shows the same in the Drain dialog of each storage row, with the budget, "remove when empty" and, for cold storage, the cold-read confirmation; the row then shows the drain's progress bar and a Stop button. The Prices and quota dialog sets the quota, and the row shows a usage bar against it.

**Commands and API.** `varsto storage drain <NAME> [--plan] [--stop] [--remove-when-empty] [--budget-gb-per-day N] [--confirm-cold-read] [--background]`, `varsto storage drain status`, `varsto storage quota <NAME> [--bytes 1TB | --clear] [--objects N] [--measure]` (sizes in kB, MB, GB, TB or KiB, MiB, GiB, TiB), `--json` on all of them; `storage list` shows the quota, use and drain state. `GET /api/storage/quotas`, `POST /api/storage/quota {name, bytes ("1TB", bytes, or "" to clear), objects, clear}`, `GET /api/storage/drains`, `GET /api/storage/drain-plan?name=&budget_gb_per_day=&confirm_cold_read=`, `POST /api/storage/drain {name, stop, remove_when_empty, budget_gb_per_day, confirm_cold_read}` (the service runs it after its next sync).

**Limits.**

- Usage is an estimate between `--measure` runs: records are estimated, objects inside packs count one each, and another device's claims count under this device's name only once that device has published its storage names (it does at its next sync while any storage has settings). Two devices writing at the same moment can together pass the 1 % margin; the next push of each sees the claims and stops.
- A quota is not a provider's hard limit: a write the provider refuses is an ordinary write error.
- A device that has not synced since a drain started can still write a block there; the driver sees the claim and moves it, also after the drain finished.
- The policy rules a drain keeps are the copy rules; the verification window (`verified_within_days`) starts again for the new copies, which the drain itself reads back (a check by the writer, not by another device).
- Copies on storages this device does not know (another device's own storage under a name that maps to nothing here) are not counted as copies that stay, and their objects on the storage being drained are not deleted.

Tests: `varsto_core::storage_settings::tests` (sizes, the margin, records open only for their storage and writer and, when required, signed), `crates/varsto-core/tests/storage_drain.rs` (a drain copies every block to the new storage, reads it back and records the check before the drop, the policy is never weaker after any batch, the records are on the other storages, a drained storage takes no writes, stopping makes it an ordinary storage again; a policy that cannot be met elsewhere stops the drain with "add another cloud storage or lower the policy" and nothing leaves until a storage is added; a drain resumes after the device stops and removes the storage when empty; a quota sends what does not fit to the other storage, makes blocks wait when no storage has room, puts the reason in the policy report and lets them go when there is room; a drain fills a storage only up to its quota and says so; quotas and drains reach a device that names the storages differently, which respects them, and that device's copies are moved by the other device's drain).

**Placement and drain agree.** A drain is refused while a folder's placement still names the storage (by name, or by its place when it is the only open storage in that place); the message names the folders and says to change their placement first, in the CLI, the API and the interface alike. In the other direction a storage being drained or drained cannot be named by a placement (`set_placement` refuses it), so a folder is never left with a target that takes nothing. A drain also ends by dropping every location the view still lists on the storage, claims or a verification made by a device that had not yet seen the drop, for every file: a verification alone, after a drop, no longer brings the storage back into the view (`ledger::apply_drops`).

## 45. Local debug logs

Each program writes a local debug log (see `docs/architecture/logging.md`): `home/logs/varsto-<component>.log` (`service`, `desktop-app`, `cli`, `tray`, `mcp`, `ffi-ios`, `ffi-android`, `app`), lines of `<RFC 3339 UTC time with ms> <LEVEL> <component>[<pid>/<thread>] <module>: <message> key=value…`, rotated at 10 MiB into `varsto-<component>.<n>.log.zst` (n = 1 newest … 4), at most 64 MiB in the directory (phones: 4 MiB, 3 rotated, 24 MiB). Directory 0700, files 0600. Default level `debug`; `--log-level`, then `VARSTO_LOG`, then `log_level` in `config.json` (a level or a filter like `debug,varsto_core::p2p=trace`).

The logs are local files of the device, like `state/`: they are not part of the vault format, never written to a storage, never synced or served to peers, and their format may change between versions without migration.

**Never in a log:** key material of any kind (vault, device, folder, share, replica, peer, signing and KEM keys), passphrases, recovery words and shares, the service token, MCP keys, share, replica and organisation tokens, storage secrets (S3 secret access keys, rclone tokens), restic passwords, security-key secrets, plaintext file contents, decrypted manifests and other decrypted metadata. **Allowed:** file and folder names and paths, device and storage names, endpoints and buckets, object names, sizes, timings, peer addresses, error messages. Secret types redact themselves, fields with secret names are redacted, and every line passes a scrubber for registered secrets, registered keys (by keyed hash), token shapes and recovery-word runs.

**Diagnostics bundle** (`varsto log bundle`, `POST /api/logs/bundle`): a zstd-compressed tar `varsto-logs/…` of the log files (decompressed, scrubbed again), the end of `service.log` and `restic-run.log`, `about.json`, `status.json` (the service's `/api/state`, when it runs) and `config.json`.

**Restic settings are not secrets to the scrubber.** The restic password itself never reaches a log (`tests/log_secrets.rs`). The repository string (section 35), which may carry `user:password@` for REST and SFTP repositories, and the password command are configuration: they are in `config.json` and so in the diagnostics bundle, the repository string appears in `restic-run.log` and in log lines about restic runs, and the recovery guide (section 41) prints the repository with `user:password@` replaced by placeholders but names the password command's existence. Registering the repository's userinfo and the password command with the scrubber, and redacting them in the bundle, is an open hardening item (S8). API: `GET /api/logs` → `{dir, level, source: flag|env|config|default, bytes, files, can_open}`, `POST /api/logs/level {level}` (saved as `log_level`), `POST /api/logs/open` (computers). Mobile library: `void varsto_log(int level, const char *msg)` (1 error … 5 trace) writes an app line under module `app`.

Tests: `varsto_core::logging` (filters, format, timestamps, rotation and cap, tar, scrubber shapes and field names) and `crates/varsto-core/tests/log_secrets.rs` (every secret type through every log macro form, in the files and in a bundle; file modes).

The scrubber also cuts the login of every URL, and a support bundle carries a redacted `config.json`: section 60.

## 46. Original names and local name mapping

File names are kept as they are everywhere: in manifests, imports, conflict copies, the interface and the command line. A device whose filesystem does not take a name writes the file under a *local name* and maps it back when it scans, so the vault path never changes because of one device's filesystem.

**Vault paths** (`names::check_vault_path`, also `util::check_rel_path`). A vault path is UTF-8, at most 4096 bytes, relative, with `/` as the separator; every component is a non-empty name other than `.` and `..`, and no NUL appears. Anything else is part of a name: `:`, `\`, `?`, `*`, `"`, `<`, `>`, `|`, control characters 1-31 and 127, a trailing dot or space, a Windows device name (`CON.txt`), emoji, combining characters. A manifest with any other path is refused (section 28). Unicode normalisation is not changed: a scan records the bytes the filesystem returns (a Linux or APFS name stays as written, NFC or NFD; HFS+ returns NFD, so a NFC name written there comes back NFD and looks renamed, as it did before this section). Names that are not UTF-8 are not synced (a scan skips them; imports report them). Object keys on storages keep the stricter rule (`util::check_portable_rel_path`: no `:`, backslash, control character or Windows device name), because every platform writes them as they are.

**Showing names.** The interface and the command line show names as they are, `:` and `\` included; control characters and the direction overrides U+202A-U+202E and U+2066-U+2069 are shown as `\u{..}` escapes (`names::display`, `show` in the command line, `showName` in the interface), so that a name cannot move the cursor, recolour a terminal or pass for another name. `--json` output, the API and MCP carry the names unchanged.

**The Windows mapping** (`names::windows_encode_component`, `windows_decode_component`). Per name component: each of `< > : " \ | ? *` and each control character 1-31 is written as the private-use character U+F000 + its code (`:` is U+F03A; the scheme of Cygwin, Samba and the Windows SFM driver, so Explorer shows a look-alike character); a trailing dot or space is written as U+F02E or U+F020; and a Windows device name (`CON`, `PRN`, `AUX`, `NUL`, `COM1`-`COM9`, `LPT1`-`LPT9`, `COM¹`-`COM³`, `LPT¹`-`LPT³`, `CONIN$`, `CONOUT$`, any case, with any extension, ignoring spaces before the first dot) gets the last character of the device name mapped the same way: `CON.txt` is written `CO\u{F04E}.txt`, `nul` is written `nu\u{F06C}`. Decoding turns U+F000 + c back into c only for the characters the encoder maps (those above, `.`, space, ASCII letters and digits, `$` and `¹²³`), never into `/` or NUL, so it is the exact inverse on every name without private-use characters. On Windows a scan therefore reads private-use characters U+F001-U+F0FF in local names as escapes: a file a person names `a\u{F03F}b` on Windows is stored as `a?b`. That interpretation applies on Windows only; on Linux and macOS such characters are ordinary characters.

**Per platform.** Linux and Android (ext4 and the like): names as they are. Windows: the Windows mapping, and case-insensitive. macOS: names as they are (`:` is legal at the POSIX layer and Finder shows it as `/`), case-insensitive on the default APFS and HFS+ volumes. Whether a folder's filesystem ignores case is probed once per folder directory and process: an entry already in the directory is looked up with its case swapped (the same file, by device and inode, means case-insensitive; nothing is written, so a backup's source is left alone), and an empty directory gets a probe file `.varsto-case-probe-<random>` that is looked up in upper case and removed at once (exFAT and FAT on Linux are case-insensitive too). A directory that does not exist yet, or cannot be written, gets the platform's default (case-insensitive on Windows and macOS). A Linux disk formatted exFAT, FAT or NTFS refuses some names Linux takes: before a name with a character Windows forbids or a trailing dot or space is written, its directories are created and an empty file is tried; when that fails with EINVAL (or ENOENT) the path falls back to the Windows mapping. Storages are not affected (objects are named by hashes).

**Case collisions.** On a case-insensitive filesystem two vault paths that differ only in case (or, folded, in Unicode normalisation: names are compared lower-cased and NFC) would be one file. The first one written keeps its name; the next gets a local name with ` (2)` (` (3)`, ...) before the extension (`photo.jpg` becomes `photo (2).jpg` next to `Photo.JPG`), and a directory takes the spelling already on disk (`dir/y.txt` is written into an existing `Dir/`). The pull report lists such files (`renamed_locally`: `"photo.jpg -> photo (2).jpg"`), the command line and the interface's log show them; nothing is lost, and editing the renamed copy edits the vault file it stands for. Which one of the two gets the suffix depends on the order they arrive in.

**The local name map** (`names::LocalNames`, `local_names` in `state/<folder>.json`, absent when empty). Per folder and device: vault path -> local path (both with `/`), for every file whose local path differs from its vault path (mapping, fallback, collision, a directory's other spelling) or whose local name a scan would decode differently. Placement (`LocalNames::place`, used for every file Varsto writes into a folder: pulls, placeholders, conflict copies, fetches, imports, uploads through the API, moves, restored backups) reuses a recorded local path, else computes one as above and records it. A scan maps every local path back through the map, else through the platform's decoding (Windows) or as it is; a file that a scan maps through decoding but that would be written under another name is recorded too. So a mapped file never looks deleted, added or renamed. Records go when the file is deleted (pulled or scanned) or found gone. Every reader of a local file (thumbnails, uploads, the viewer, MCP `local_path`, `list_files`' `disk`, the file-manager actions through `locate_path`, scrub, the restic mirror check, wipes) goes through the same map. Trash copies and exported files use the platform's mapping; damaged copies set aside by scrub are named from the local name and reported by their vault name.

**Limits.** The probe tells only whether the folder's top directory ignores case; a case-sensitive directory below a case-insensitive root (or the reverse) is not detected. A Linux exFAT driver that silently drops trailing dots instead of refusing them makes such a file look renamed. Case folding follows Rust's Unicode lower-casing and NFC, close to but not exactly the tables of NTFS and APFS. The map lives on one device: another device does not know this one's local names.

Tests: `names::tests` (vault path rules, traversal refused, the Windows mapping round trip and injectivity on the affected set, device names, case collisions with directories and normalisation twins, the exFAT fallback with a simulated refusal, symbolic links refused), `crates/varsto-core/tests/original_names.rs` (`:`, `\`, `?`, `*`, `"`, `<`, `|`, trailing dot and space, `CON.txt`, `nul`, a control character, emoji and both normalisations of a name round-trip between two Linux devices byte for byte; a simulated Windows device writes the mapped names, reads them back without a change, updates and deletes through them and stores a name typed with an escape decoded; a simulated case-insensitive device keeps colliding files under ` (2)` and edits flow back; traversal refused), the import tests (`12:30.jpg` and others keep their names from git-annex, a directory and rclone; the staging directory is on the destination folder's filesystem or where `--staging` says, removed afterwards, and a batch that does not fit stops the run with the free-space message).

## 47. Folder safety: the folder marker, the mass-deletion guard, send-only and receive-only folders

**Why.** A synced folder whose disk is not mounted leaves an empty mount point. A scan of it found every file gone, and the deletions reached every device (only a missing root stopped the scan). The same happens when a script or a slip deletes most of a folder at once. And some devices should take part in a folder one way only: a camera phone that sends its pictures but should never get the others' edits, or a laptop that follows a folder without publishing what is changed on it. These ideas come from Syncthing (folder marker, send-only and receive-only folders); `crates/varsto-core/src/engine/folder_safety.rs` implements them.

**The marker.** Every folder mounted with plain files has a file `.varsto-folder` in its root: JSON `{"varsto": "folder", "folder_id": "<hex>", "name": "<name>", "note": "..."}` (the name is informational). It is written when the folder is created or attached here, when the restic mirror attaches it, and moved with the directory (the restic mirror's move, Strongroom conversions rewrite it with the new folder id). Like every `.varsto*` name it is never a file of the folder (scans skip it, restic excludes it). Before anything reads the directory as the folder's state or writes into it (pull, push, fetch, placeholders, writes through the app), the root must carry the folder's own marker:

- *missing* (an empty mount point, a deleted marker, a missing root): the folder is paused on this device with "folder X: the marker .varsto-folder is missing at <root> — is the disk mounted? Nothing is synced until it is back; `varsto folder repair-marker X` if the directory is right";
- *another folder's id, or unreadable*: paused the same way ("belongs to another folder (<id>) — is the right disk mounted?").

A paused folder is not scanned, so nothing reads as deleted, and nothing is downloaded into it. `sync` reports the pause per folder (`PullReport.paused`: folder, kind `marker-missing` / `marker-other` / `deletions-held`, message, path) and goes on with the other folders; `pull`, `push` and `fetch` return it as an error. `status` shows it per folder (`paused`), the service notifies once per pause, and the interface shows a banner with "The directory is right" (`repair-marker`). Once the marker is back the folder resumes by itself.

*Older mounts.* A mount from before markers has none. This device records in `state/<folder>.marked` that the marker was seen; until then a root without a marker is adopted (marked now) when no current file of the folder is known yet, or when at least half of its current files (or their placeholders) are in the directory; otherwise it is paused as missing (the upgrade happened while the disk was not mounted). Folders kept encrypted on this device hold their files in the block cache under the vault home, so the rule does not apply to them; a backup's source is only read and is not a mount.

**The mass-deletion guard.** Per folder and device (`FolderMount.deletion_guard`, absent = default): deletions are *held* when more than `percent` % of the folder's current files **and** more than `min_files` files, or more than `max_files` files, go at once (default: more than 50 % and more than 100, or more than 1000; `percent` of 100 or more turns the share rule off, `max_files` 0 the count rule; `enabled: false` turns the guard off).
