- *This device's scan* finds more files gone than that: the deletions are not made versions (nothing is published), the folder's state records them (`FolderState.held`: paths, the folder's file count, time) and the folder is paused. Files that come back (a disk mounted again, files moved back) leave the hold; when none is left the folder resumes by itself. `varsto folder confirm-deletions X` makes them deletions (published with the next sync; files back meanwhile are left out); `--discard` restores them from the storages or peers (in a selective folder an unpinned file comes back as a placeholder; files that cannot be fetched now stay held).
- *Another device's file list* would delete more files here than that: the deletions of that manifest are held with the remote tombstones, the manifest is not applied and the device's `last_seen` is not advanced (so nothing of it is lost), and the folder is paused. `confirm-deletions` applies the held deletions (the files go to this device's trash as usual); the manifest is then read again and its deletions match. `--discard` keeps the files: each one gets a new version of this device that includes the tombstone's version vector, so it is newer than the deletion and every device gets the file back.

**Modes.** Per folder and device (`FolderMount.mode`: `normal`, `send-only`, `receive-only`; `varsto folder mode X <mode>`, the interface's Sync settings dialog):

- *send-only*: the device publishes its changes but applies no other device's change to its files. Pull still reads their manifests; every remote state that would change something here is kept in `FolderState.not_applied` (and files waiting for a copy move there too); entries this device's own changes have caught up with are dropped. Status counts them (`remote_not_applied`), `varsto folder pending X` lists them, and `varsto folder override X` (interface: "Override: make everyone match this device") replaces each with a new version of this device's state (its content, or a deletion for a file it never had) whose version vector includes the remote one, published with the next sync. Back to normal, what waited is merged and applied at the next pull.
- *receive-only*: the device applies other devices' changes but publishes none of its own. Its scan notes added, modified and deleted files in `FolderState.local_changes` instead of making versions (their blocks are not offered either); the published manifest stays the vault's state. Status counts them (`local_changes`), `varsto folder revert X` (interface: "Revert: restore the vault version") moves changed and added files to this device's trash and fetches the vault's versions (deleted ones are fetched back). A remote change of a locally changed file replaces it the same way, the local copy going to the trash first. Back to normal, the noted changes are scanned again and published. Not offered for folders kept encrypted on this device.

**With the other features.** A folder this device holds as a read-only member (section 55) is guarded by the viewer's own scan, which keeps local changes aside instead of noting them: send-only is refused for it and receive-only adds nothing. On a receive-only device, metadata changes (section 49) are not published either, and a version (section 50) is not restored into the folder. Paths the ignore rules cover (section 48) are neither noted nor counted by the mass-deletion guard. A file that waits for disk space (section 53) after its local change went to the trash shows as a local deletion until it arrives. Local names (section 46) are used for the marker checks, the held deletions and restores. A folder that pauses is announced once as a `folder.paused` event (section 54).

**Download order.** When many files are fetched, the newest (by the manifest's modification time) come first: a pull orders each manifest's files newest first (and files waiting for a copy, and what waited in send-only mode), the first sync after attaching included; turning selective sync off fills placeholders newest first; folders kept encrypted here fill their block cache newest first; restoring held deletions and reverting go newest first. A fetch plan from pool disks keeps its grouping by disk (one disk after another, as chosen) and copies the blocks of the newest files first on each disk; its files are written newest first. Repairs of storage copies keep their own order.

**Commands and interface.** `varsto folder repair-marker X`, `confirm-deletions X [--discard]`, `mode X [normal|send-only|receive-only]`, `override X`, `revert X`, `pending X` (what waits for a decision; who changed what is `varsto changes`, section 57), `deletion-guard X [--percent N] [--min-files N] [--max-files N] [--off] [--default]`; with the service running they go through it (`POST /api/folder/safety` with `folder` and `action`), otherwise the command syncs the folder after the change. `varsto status` and `sync` print pauses and waiting changes. The interface shows one banner per folder that is paused or has changes waiting, with the actions and a list of the files, a "paused" and a mode pill, and a Sync settings dialog (mode, deletion guard). English, Finnish and Swedish.

Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/folder_safety.rs` (an empty mount point without its marker publishes no deletion, pauses the folder and resumes with nothing lost; a missing root; another folder's marker until repaired; pull and fetch write nothing into an unmarked root; older mounts adopted only when their files are there; a local mass deletion held, discarded and restored, released when the files come back, confirmed and then held again on the other device until confirmed there, or discarded there and restored on every device; deletions below the threshold and a per-folder guard; send-only ignores remote changes, still publishes and overrides; send-only back to normal; receive-only keeps local edits unpublished and reverts with the local copies in the trash, a remote update of a locally changed file, back to normal publishes; downloads newest first through a recorded download order, for a pull and for placeholders filled).

**Camera upload folders (Android).** The camera card creates its folder send-only (`POST /api/folder {name, plain, mode: "send-only"}`): the phone publishes the photos and never applies other devices' changes into it. Unless the user ticks "Keep plain files on this phone" the folder is kept encrypted on the phone (section 10), as every folder added on a phone is; and freeing an uploaded file no longer turns an encrypted folder selective (a freed file turns a plain folder selective, as before), so the folder shows as an ordinary one.

## 48. Ignore rules

**Why.** Folders collect files nobody wants on every device: editor swap files, `.DS_Store`, build output, a phone's thumbnails cache. Rules say which paths Varsto leaves alone (`crates/varsto-core/src/ignore_rules.rs`, `engine/folder_rules.rs`).

**Where rules live.** A `.varstoignore` file at the folder's root is an ordinary file of the folder: it is scanned, published and pulled like any other, so every device shares it (it is the one `.varsto*` name that is synced; restic leaves out only `.varsto-*`). A `.varstoignore` in a subdirectory applies to that subtree, its patterns relative to its directory. Device-local patterns are kept in this device's configuration (`FolderMount.ignore_local`, a list of lines) and never leave it. Files of more than 256 KiB are read up to that size; lines longer than 1024 bytes are skipped.

**Syntax** (gitignore): one pattern per line; blank lines and lines starting with `#` are skipped; trailing spaces are dropped unless escaped (`\ `); `!` re-includes; `\#` and `\!` are literal; a trailing `/` matches directories only; a leading or middle `/` anchors the pattern to the directory of its file, otherwise it matches a name at any depth below it; `*` matches any run of characters within one path component, `?` one character, `[...]` a class (ranges, `!` or `^` to negate; an unclosed `[` is literal); `**` as a whole component matches zero or more components (`**/x`, `a/**/b`), a trailing `/**` everything inside (not the directory itself); `\` escapes the next character. Matching is case-sensitive, on manifest paths (`/` separators).

**Deciding.** The last matching pattern of a source decides. Sources by precedence, highest first: this device's local patterns, then the `.varstoignore` of the deepest directory containing the path, up to the root's. The first source with a match decides. A path inside an ignored directory is ignored whatever patterns say about the path itself (it cannot be re-included, as in git): the directories above a path are decided first. The matcher is written for Varsto (about 300 lines with tests) rather than taken from the `ignore` crate, which would bring regex, globset and crossbeam.

**Effect.** Only on devices that keep plain files of the folder (not a folder kept encrypted on a device, section on block caches, and not a backup's source, which has its own selection).

- *Scan*: ignored directories are not entered and ignored files are not read; they are neither added nor updated. A path in the folder's state that the rules cover is not deleted when it disappears here: ignored files are not published and not deleted elsewhere. A file already in the vault that becomes ignored stays in the vault as its last version: this device no longer updates it (its edits and its removal stay local) while other devices still may. `PushReport.files_ignored` counts ignored entries (an ignored directory once).
- *Pull*: an incoming version (or deletion) of a path this device's rules cover is not written: it is kept aside in the folder state (`ignored_remote`, path to the newest such version) and reported once per version (`PullReport.files_ignored`). When the rule no longer covers it (a rule removed, a `.varstoignore` changed), the next pull applies the kept version if it is newer than this device's (a file made here meanwhile stays, and the other devices resolve the conflict when they see it).
- The rules in force are read at the start of each scan and pull: a `.varstoignore` that arrives in a pull applies from the next one.

**Suggestions.** The interface offers, never applies by itself: `.DS_Store`, `Thumbs.db`, `desktop.ini`, `~$*`, `*.tmp`, `.~lock.*#`.

**Surfaces.** `varsto folder ignore <folder> [--list] [--add P]... [--remove P]... [--local]` (without `--local` the root `.varstoignore` is edited: a line added at the end or every equal line removed; the change is published by the next sync; with `--json` the listing `{folder, synced, nested, local, suggested, applies_here}`); `varsto folder check-ignore <folder> <path>` (a trailing `/` or an existing directory asks about a directory) answers `{ignored, pattern, source, line, matched_path}` (`source` is the `.varstoignore` path or `local`; `matched_path` the path or the ignored directory above it). API: `GET /api/folder/ignore?folder=`, `POST /api/folder/ignore {folder, synced?, local?}` (text of the root file, an empty text removes it; the local list), `POST /api/folder/check-ignore {folder, path, synced?, local?}` (the texts being edited stand for the saved ones: a preview). MCP keys have no access to these routes. The interface: Files, the folder's settings button: a text box with the shared file, the suggestions as buttons that add a line, a list of this device's own patterns, and a "test a path" box; English, Finnish and Swedish.

**Limits.** Case-sensitive everywhere (also on case-insensitive filesystems). Rules are not applied to what other devices publish into the vault: they only decide what this device sends and writes. A device that keeps the folder encrypted ignores nothing.

Tests: `ignore_rules` unit tests (names at any depth, comments and escapes, negation and last-match-wins, no re-inclusion under an ignored directory, anchoring, `**` forms, directory-only patterns, wildcards and classes, nested files, local precedence, lazy reading from disk, line editing); `crates/varsto-core/tests/ignore_and_metadata.rs` (ignored files neither published nor deleted elsewhere, a file that becomes ignored kept in the vault, `check-ignore` and a preview, local rules not synced, incoming ignored files skipped and reported once, a held-back version applied once the rule is removed); `crates/varsto-cli/tests/cli_smoke.rs` (the commands with `--json`).

## 49. File metadata: permissions, symbolic links, extended attributes, ownership

**Why.** Syncing only content loses what makes a script executable, a link a link, or a Finder tag a tag. Varsto syncs the metadata each device can represent, and never lets a device that cannot represent something erase it (`crates/varsto-core/src/fsmeta.rs`).

**Record.** `FileState` has an optional `meta` (absent: nothing known; manifests without metadata are unchanged):

```json
"meta": {"v": 1, "mode": 493, "symlink": "../shared/config",
         "xattrs": {"user.xdg.tags": "cmVk"},
         "owner": {"uid": 1000, "gid": 1000, "user": "ana", "group": "ana"}}
```

Every field but `v` is optional, and absent means *not known*, never *none* (`"xattrs": {}` means known to have none). `v` is the record version (1); a device that meets a higher version neither applies nor changes the record: it carries it unchanged in its own versions. `mode`: the permission bits, rwx for user, group and others (`0o777`); setuid, setgid and sticky are neither recorded nor applied, so no device can plant a setuid file on another (a record with them is accepted, the bits are masked when applied). `symlink`: the entry is a symbolic link with this target, exactly as read (relative targets stay relative; an absolute target, starting with `/`, `\` or a drive letter, is synced as given and flagged in listings, since it may point elsewhere on another device); a link entry has `size` 0, no chunks, and `content_hash` = keyed hash (folder hash key) of `"varsto-symlink\0" ‖ target`, which no file content can share. `xattrs`: name to value in standard base64. `owner`: numeric ids and, where known, names.

**Change identity.** A file's identity for sync is its content hash together with `meta`: a scan that sees the content unchanged but the metadata changed makes a new version (this device's clock in the version vector) with the same chunks, so nothing is uploaded or downloaded (`PushReport.metadata_changed`); a pull whose content hash matches the file on disk only applies the metadata. `Manifest::files_hash` covers `meta`, so such a version is published. In a merge, concurrent versions with the same content (deleted alike, same content hash) whose metadata differs pick the later change deterministically (higher `modified_clock`, then device id) with the merged version vector and no conflict copy; other concurrent edits merge as before (section on manifests).

**Observing without flapping.** Per file the local index keeps `meta_seen`, what this device last saw on disk. A scan observes the entry (`fsmeta::observe`: only what this system can see and the folder's settings allow) and publishes, per property, the observed value only when it differs from `meta_seen`; otherwise the vault's value stays (`fsmeta::combine`). A property this device cannot see (setting off, no support, a filesystem that does not keep it) is never part of what is observed, so its value in the vault is carried into this device's new versions unchanged: such a device never publishes its removal. After a pull writes a file and its metadata, `meta_seen` is what the disk shows afterwards, so a filesystem that silently drops a property (mode bits on exFAT, FAT, many phone storages) does not report the difference back as a change. Attributes this system cannot hold (a `com.apple.*` name on Linux) are kept from the vault's value when the observed set changes. A metadata change on such a device publishes only the property that changed.

**Platform rules.**

| | Linux | macOS | Windows | Android, iOS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| permissions | mode bits | mode bits | read-only flag only: marking read-only clears the write bits of the vault's mode, clearing it sets the owner's write bit; other bits kept | mode bits (where the storage keeps them; otherwise carried) |
| symbolic links | created | created | created when allowed (Developer Mode or the privilege), else a note | created |
| extended attributes | `user.*` only | all synced names | none (carried) | none (carried) |
| ownership | as below | as below | none (carried) | none (carried) |

A folder kept encrypted on a device has no plain files: metadata is carried, links are kept in the vault only.

**Symbolic links.** Links are never followed: the scan reads them with `readlink` (`walkdir` without following, so a link to a directory is one entry and nothing below it is scanned); a target that is not UTF-8 or longer than 4096 bytes leaves the link out (not published, not deleted). A link is written as a link (`<path>` replaced atomically by renaming a new link over it; a directory is never replaced), deleted by moving the link itself to the trash. Where the system refuses a link, a JSON note `<path>.varsto-link` (`{varsto: "symbolic link", target, hint}`) is written instead and reported (`PullReport.links_as_notes`); the scan treats the note as the link being present (neither a change nor a deletion). Every path Varsto writes, removes or reads is refused when a directory above it inside the folder is a link (`fsmeta::join_entry`, `check_no_link`): reading a file through Varsto (interface, MCP, viewer) refuses a link or a path through one, so a link received from another device cannot expose files outside the folder. Thumbnails are not made for links. Dangling links sync like any other. With links off on a device, its links are left out (neither published nor deleted), and incoming links are kept aside like ignored files (`PullReport.links_skipped`) and created once links are turned on.

**Extended attributes.** Never synced: `com.apple.quarantine`, `com.apple.provenance`, `com.apple.macl`, `com.apple.lastuseddate#PS`, `com.apple.rootless`, `com.apple.decmpfs`, `com.apple.system.Security`, the code-signature names `com.apple.cs.*` listed in `fsmeta`, and the `security.`, `system.`, `trusted.` and `com.apple.system.` namespaces (per-device noise, or writable only by the system or root). Read and written without following links (`llistxattr` and friends on Linux, `XATTR_NOFOLLOW` on macOS); not on links. At most 64 KiB of names and values per file: a file with more has its attributes left as the vault has them (not observed). Applying makes the synced attributes this system can hold equal to the record (removing ones the record lacks); others on disk are left alone.

**Ownership.** Off by default per folder (`ownership`), because ids rarely mean the same on two devices. Observed as uid, gid and their names. Applied (`lchown`) only when running as root (names first, ids where a name is unknown here) or when both names exist here with the same ids as recorded; otherwise ignored (nothing reported). As a non-root user that means the change is a no-op or refused quietly.

**Settings** (per folder, per device, `FolderMount.meta`, written only when not default): `permissions` (on), `symlinks` (on), `xattrs` (on), `ownership` (off). Off: the property is neither observed nor applied on this device; the vault's value is carried. `varsto folder set <folder> [--permissions on|off] [--symlinks on|off] [--xattrs on|off] [--ownership on|off]` (without options: shows them); `GET /api/folder/settings?folder=` (`{settings, caps}`, what this system can represent) and `POST /api/folder/settings {folder, permissions?, symlinks?, xattrs?, ownership?}`; the interface: checkboxes in the folder's settings, and the file details show the link target (flagged when absolute), permissions (`rwxr-xr-x (0755)`), owner and attribute names. `varsto folder files` shows `-> target` for links.

**Validation.** A manifest entry's `meta` is refused when `mode` has bits beyond `0o7777`, a link target is empty, longer than 4096 bytes or has a NUL, an attribute name is empty, longer than 255 bytes or has a NUL, a value is not base64, names and values exceed 64 KiB, an owner name is empty, longer than 256 bytes or contains NUL, `/` or `:`, or a link entry has content.

**Elsewhere.** Backups record metadata like folders (default settings) and a restore creates links as links and applies permissions and attributes this system can hold. The restic mirror counts a link as complete when the link in the mirror has the recorded target. Scrub's repaired copies and fetched placeholders get their metadata applied.

**Failure model.**

- A device that cannot represent a property, or has it off, cannot remove it from the vault; but it also cannot *change* it: a chmod on Windows other than the read-only flag is lost, and so is an attribute added on a phone.
- Concurrent metadata changes on two devices: the later one wins everywhere, without a conflict copy; the earlier one is lost (content never is).
- A filesystem that drops what is applied keeps the vault's value without reporting it; the file on that device simply differs. `metadata_problems` in the pull report lists what could not be applied (a refused `chmod`, `setxattr`).
- A malicious device can publish links pointing anywhere and any permission bits: Varsto never follows links itself and never applies setuid, setgid or sticky; making a file executable or world-writable is possible, as with any synced change. Trust in other devices of the vault is as before (section 28).
- The ownership rule cannot tell whether a name means the same person on two machines; that is why it is off by default.

Tests: `fsmeta` unit tests (a property taken only when it changed here, Windows read-only mapping, attributes this system cannot hold kept, newer records carried, ownership as non-root left alone, validation, mode text) and `manifest` (concurrent metadata changes need no conflict copy); `crates/varsto-core/tests/ignore_and_metadata.rs`: a chmod syncs with `chunks_uploaded == 0` and `chunks_downloaded == 0` and is applied, nothing flaps back; a device simulated without mode bits (`Engine::set_meta_caps`) edits the content and the mode survives; settings off: incoming permissions not applied and local ones not published, ownership off by default and published when turned on; links round trip (relative, absolute and flagged, dangling, to a directory, out of the folder) and are never followed (a read through a link or through a linked directory is refused), a changed target and a deleted link arrive, links off on a device; Linux `user.*` attributes round trip, change and removal (skipped where the temporary filesystem has none), and attributes off keep them.

## 50. Version history (0.0.1-alpha.14)

**What it is.** When a file is replaced by a new version, the previous version is kept as a *history entry* and can be restored on every device that holds the folder key, from the storages, on a thinning schedule (Syncthing's "staggered versioning"): every version for an hour, then at most one per hour for a day, one per day for 30 days and one per week until the *history days* (90 by default, per vault and per folder, or off). 90 days is three times the trash days: a mistaken overwrite is often noticed later than a deletion, and the thinning keeps it cheap (one version per week after the first month). The machinery is the trash's (section 43): the same records, the same liveness rules for blocks, the same trash storage, upkeep and restore checks.

**Entries.** A history entry has the shape of a trash entry: path, size, modification time and content hash of the replaced version, its block list, when it was replaced (UTC), the replacing device, the Lamport clock of the new version and (new, also in trash entries) the device that wrote the version (`author`, shown, not checked). Its id is computed like a trash entry's from folder, path, replacing device and clock; a deletion and a replacement never share a clock, so ids do not collide. The device that writes the new version records the previous one: a scan that finds a file's content changed, a write into a folder kept encrypted here (`record_cached`), a restore. A device that applies another device's new version records nothing (the writer did), and a conflict keeps the losing version as a conflict copy as before. A version whose blocks are on no storage and in no cache of the writing device (written and replaced between two uploads) cannot be restored and is not recorded. Backups (section 31) keep no history.

**Records.** Entries are in the device's trash record of the folder (`vault/trash/<folder>/<device>/<utc>.enc`, section 43) under `versions`; readers accept them with the same checks as trash entries (writer is the replacing device, the id recomputes, path and block sizes pass the manifest checks). Records written before this section have none; older readers ignore the field. The history of a folder is the union of every device's newest record, revoked devices aside, thinned as below.

**Thinning.** For each path separately, with `t` the time a version was replaced and `now` the device's clock: a version is kept if `now - t` is under an hour; otherwise only if no newer version (by time, then clock, then id) was replaced in the same clock hour (`t / 3600`) while it is under a day old, the same UTC day (`t / 86400`) while under 30 days, or the same week counted from the Unix epoch (`t / 604800`) until the history days; older ones are let go. The buckets are fixed and nested, and a version's bucket only grows as it ages, so a version let go never becomes kept again, and every device that knows the same versions keeps the same ones (`history_kept`). A version this device has queued for dropping is never kept again, also if its clock goes back.

**Settings.** The vault's history days and the days per folder are fields of the trash settings record (`vault/trash-settings/<device>/<utc>.enc`, section 43): `history_days` (90 when absent, 0 = off) and `folder_history` (folder id → days, 0 = off). Off stops recording new versions; versions already kept age out on the vault's schedule (90 days when the vault is off too). Days run from 1 to 3650. Members of shared folders cannot read or change them and keep 90 days.

**Blocks.** Content-defined chunking (section 4) means a version shares every unchanged block with the current file and the other versions: only the changed blocks cost space, and they are stored once. The blocks of kept versions count as used exactly like the trash's (section 43): whole-folder data moves take them along, removing a storage copies first those that would lose their last copy (one copy required), `fsck` counts them as referenced, and the trash storage takes the blocks only versions or trash entries use (copied, checked, recorded, then dropped from this device's other warm storages). When the upkeep finds versions let go since its last run, their blocks are queued and dropped as in section 43: only if no current file, version still to download, trash entry, kept version or other folder uses the object; sealed and pushed before deletion; never before a cold storage's minimum duration. A device forgets its own let-go versions from its record seven days after it first saw them go.

**Restore.** `varsto history restore <folder> <path> <version|time> [--to DIR | --as NAME] [--confirm-cold-read]`. The version is named by its id (six or more characters) or a time in UTC (`YYYY-MM-DD` meaning the end of that day, `YYYY-MM-DD HH:MM[:SS]`): the version current then, i.e. the first one replaced after it (the nearest kept one when the history thinned it). Without `--to`/`--as` the version becomes the current file at its path: the folder is synced first (so a change on disk not yet seen is recorded and uploaded), the restore stops if the file changed after that, the content is fetched and checked against block names, chunk ids and the content hash, then written over the file (into the block cache in a folder kept encrypted here) and pushed as a new version, so the replaced current version becomes a history entry itself and nothing is lost; restoring the current content is refused. `--to DIR` writes it under that directory of the folder (as `name (restored).ext` when taken); `--as NAME` writes it beside the file under that name (or at that folder path) and is refused when the name exists. Blocks only a cold storage holds need `--confirm-cold-read` (`history_restore_plan` tells how much, where and what it costs). Activity kind `history_restore`.

**Commands, interface.** `varsto history <folder> <path>` lists the versions newest first (short id, time replaced, size, the bytes it does not share with the current file and newer versions, author and replacing device, whether restoring needs a cold read); `varsto history set [--days N | --off] [--folder F]` (with `--folder`, `--days 0` follows the vault again); `varsto history status` (settings, the schedule, versions and the bytes of blocks only versions use per folder and per storage); restore as above. The daily trash upkeep (`varsto trash upkeep`) does the history's upkeep and counts the versions let go. API: `GET /api/history?folder=&path=`, `GET /api/history/status`, `POST /api/history/restore-plan`, `/api/history/restore`, `/api/history/set`. The interface shows a Versions list in a file's details on the Files page (restore over the current file, or as a copy under a new name; a dialog with the fee before a cold read), and a Version history card beside the trash settings (on or off, days, one folder's own days or off, versions and extra bytes per folder and storage).

**Limits.**

- Only replacements made by a version with the history are recorded; a file changed several times between two scans keeps only the version the scan saw.
- A file replaced while its old version was never uploaded (changed again before the push) has no entry for that version.
- A device whose clock runs ahead lets versions go early on its own storages; other devices list them until their clocks pass the same point (as for trash expiry).
- Thinning follows the replacement times, not the versions' modification times.
- Renames and moves are not replacements; a file moved over another file's path replaces that file only when the scan sees new content there.
- Turning the history off does not delete the versions already kept; they age out.
- A device where the folder is receive-only (section 47) refuses to restore a version into it: the restored file would be a local change it does not publish. A send-only device restores as usual.
- The limits of section 43 for Strongroom conversions, folder removal and members apply to versions as to trash entries.

Tests: `varsto_core::engine::history::tests` (times in UTC, ties within a bucket, expiry, clocks ahead), `crates/varsto-core/tests/history.rs` (an overwrite on device A is listed on device B with author and replacing device; B restores it over the current file byte for byte, the replaced version is listed, A gets the restored file; restoring the current content is refused; a restore under a new name and into a directory, refused over an existing name; a version picked by date; a small edit of a large file stores few new blocks and the status counts only the replaced ones; the thinning schedule with a simulated clock keeps exactly every version of the last hour, one per hour, day and week, nothing past the days, and never brings back a version let go; with a simulated clock thinned versions' own blocks are dropped while kept versions', the current file's and shared blocks stay, then everything expires and only the current files' blocks are left, fsck clean; a cold trash storage takes the blocks only versions use, B's restore asks for the cold read and succeeds with it; a folder with the history off keeps no versions, its own days and back to the vault's, the vault off; a folder kept encrypted on a phone keeps the version an app write replaced and restores it).

## 51. QR invitations and varsto:// links

Code: `crates/varsto-core/src/qr.rs` (links, compact forms, parts, rendering, decoding); `varsto-cli` (`share qr`, `open-link`, the interface's QR and scanner); the native shells register the scheme.

Request codes (section 24), sealed tokens (`vst1`/`vst2`) and the nine-digit pairing codes (`crates/varsto-core/src/pair.rs`) were passed by copy and paste. They can now travel as `varsto://` links, shown as QR codes and scanned with a camera, or opened from a message.

**Links.**

| Link | From → to | Value |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `varsto://share/request?c=<value>` | recipient → owner | the request code: public KEM key and bound name, nothing secret |
| `varsto://share/token?t=<value>` | owner → recipient | a sealed token, which only the requesting device opens |
| `varsto://pair?c=<nine digits>[&a=<host:port>,…]` | your device → your new device | the pairing code (the SPAKE2 password) and the offer's addresses |

The scheme is matched without regard to case; the path must be one of the three; every parameter must be known and appear once; a fragment is refused; the whole link is at most 16 KiB. Addresses must parse as socket addresses. `c=`/`t=` take the compact value below or the text form (`vsr1.…`, `vst2.…`, percent-escaped), so a hand-made link works too. Everything that took a request code or a token takes the link instead: `share create --to`, `share accept [--token] <token or link>` (now also positional), `pair join --code <digits or link>` (the link's addresses best first, then discovery by the code; section 68), `POST /api/share/fingerprint`, `/api/share/create`, `/api/share/accept`. **Plain tokens** (`<vault>.<folder>.<key>.<name>`, the folder key itself) are never put in a link or a QR code.

**Compact values.** A request code is 2,437 characters of hex and a sealed token about 2,800; in a QR code's byte mode that needs a version-38 code at error correction L. The value is therefore binary, Base32 encoded (RFC 4648, upper case, no padding), so that the QR encoder puts it in alphanumeric mode (5.5 bits a character, 69 % of byte mode's cost for Base32; hex in alphanumeric mode would cost 23 % more):

- request: `format (1) | key length (u16 BE) | key (X25519 32 + ML-KEM-768 1,184 bytes) | name (UTF-8, the rest)`; decoding rebuilds exactly `vsr1.<hex>[.<name>]`, so the fingerprint is the same on both sides;
- token: `version (1 or 2) | vault id (u8 length) | folder id (u8) | KEM algorithm (u8) | KEM ciphertext (u16, 1,120 bytes) | sealed payload (u16) | folder name (UTF-8, the rest)`; the encoder checks that decoding gives back the very same text (the token's AEAD binds the text of its fields).

**Sizes** (measured by `tests/qr_invites.rs`; a `vst2` token with one owner device and no later epochs; every later folder key adds about 110 Base32 characters):

| Payload | Text form | Link | One QR code | As parts (560 Base32 characters each) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| request code, name "Robin" | 2,443 | 1,984 | version 27, 125 modules, ECC L | 4 codes, version ≤ 16, ECC M |
| sealed token `vst2` | 2,802 | 2,289 | version 29, 133 modules, ECC L | 5 codes, version 16, 81 modules, ECC M |
| pairing code with one address | 11 | 46 | version 4, 33 modules, ECC M | (never split) |

**Error correction and parts.** A code uses ECC M when that still fits version 25, else L: a screen is flat and sharp, and capacity matters more than damage tolerance. One code is shown by default so that a phone's own camera app can open the link (and Varsto with it). A dense code is read easily by a phone from a laptop screen; a fixed-focus laptop camera reading a phone screen may fail, so the interface offers "Camera cannot read it? Show smaller codes" and `varsto share qr --parts` shows the same link as a sequence of smaller codes, one every 0.9 s. A part is `varsto://share/<kind>?p=<index>.<count>.<group>&<c|t>=<slice>`: the slices concatenated give the value, `group` is the first four bytes of BLAKE3 of the whole value (hex), which tells sequences apart and checks the reassembly. 2 to 64 parts; the scanner collects them in any order, starts over when a part of another sequence appears and reports "2 of 5 parts". Only Varsto reads parts; a part opened by the system is refused with a hint. A value that does not fit one version-40 code is always shown in parts.

**Why not a rendezvous.** A short code with a locator and a key, the payload fetched elsewhere, was considered: the recipient has no access to the owner's storage before it accepts, and the LAN pairing listener serves one offer of one vault. Both payloads are public-safe as they are (the request code is a public key; the sealed token opens only with the requester's private key), so size was the only problem, and the compact form with optional parts solves it without a server, a listener or a lifetime to manage.

**Threat model.**

- *Whoever photographs a request code* learns a public key and a name. Swapping it on the way gives other fingerprint words; the owner still compares the six words with the requester over another channel before sealing, as before, and the interface shows the words under the code and in the confirmation (section 24).
- *Whoever photographs a sealed token* cannot open it: it is bound to the requester's KEM key, and the requester's device also checks the fingerprint the owner confirmed. This is why only sealed tokens get QR codes.
- *Whoever photographs a pairing code* within its ten minutes can pair one device into the vault, exactly as with the digits read off the screen: the offer serves one device, closes after three failed attempts, and the offering device names the device it sent the vault to. Show it only to the device being added.
- *A link opened from outside* (a web page, a message) only fills in a form or opens the share dialog: nothing is sealed, accepted or paired without the user's own confirmation, and a request link still goes through the fingerprint comparison. `POST /api/link` and every other endpoint need the session token as before.
- *Camera frames* are read in memory by `POST /api/qr/scan` (one grey byte a pixel, at most 4096×4096, or an image file such as a screenshot) and dropped; nothing is stored or logged. Where the browser has `BarcodeDetector` the page decodes frames itself. The decoder is `rqrr` (pure Rust); codes are drawn by `qrcode` as SVG.

**Interface.** The request code (Accept a shared folder), a newly sealed token (Shared page) and an open pairing offer (Devices, Add a device) are shown as QR codes next to the text, with the fingerprint words under a request code. "Scan QR code" (Pair with your other device, Accept a shared folder, and in the owner's Share dialog) opens the camera with `getUserMedia` (rear camera preferred), polls a frame every 0.3 s, and also takes a screenshot file or pasted text; a code of the wrong kind is named ("This is not a sealed share token."). Endpoints: `POST /api/qr {text, parts}` → kind, text, link, fingerprint/name (request), folder (token), code/addresses (pairing) and `codes` (SVG, version, modules, ECC); `POST /api/link/parse {text | texts}` → the same, or `{part, have, count}` while parts are missing; `POST /api/qr/scan?w=&h=` or `?image=1` → `{texts}`; `GET /api/pair/status` now includes `uri`.

**Opening links from the system.** `varsto open-link <link>` (hidden; registered by `varsto install` on Linux as `x-scheme-handler/varsto` in `varsto-link.desktop` and `mimeapps.list`, and on Windows as `HKCU\Software\Classes\varsto` with `URL Protocol`) checks the link, starts the service if needed, hands it over with `POST /api/link`, and opens the page; the page takes it with `GET /api/link` (once) when it loads, unlocks or regains focus. The macOS app declares `CFBundleURLTypes` and posts the link to the service the same way; Android (an intent filter on `MainActivity`, `singleTask`) and iOS (`CFBundleURLTypes` in `project.yml`, `onOpenURL`) pass it to the page as `#link=` on the first load or by calling `window.varstoOpenLink`. The page then routes by kind: a pairing link fills the pairing form and a token fills Accept a shared folder (both only on a device without a vault); a request link asks the owner which folder to share and continues with the fingerprint comparison. Camera access: `NSCameraUsageDescription` on macOS and iOS (`WKUIDelegate` grants capture to the local page only), the `CAMERA` permission on Android, asked for when the scanner opens (`WebChromeClient.onPermissionRequest`, local origin only). The native changes are not compiled in this change's tests.

**Command line.** `varsto share qr <code | token | link> [--parts] [--light]` prints the code with Unicode half blocks (two modules a character row; light modules as blocks for a dark terminal, `--light` for a light one), the caption (fingerprint of a request, folder of a token) and the link; `--parts` cycles through the parts until Ctrl-C; `--json` prints the links instead. `share request --qr`, `share create … --qr` and `pair offer --qr` add the code to their output.

Tests: `qr::tests` (round trips of request, token and pairing links; unknown paths, parameters, repeated parameters, fragments, bad addresses and malformed parts refused; parts reassembled in any order, a missing part and a foreign sequence; SVG, terminal and greyscale rendering decoded again, an inverted PNG screenshot), `crates/varsto-core/tests/qr_invites.rs` (a whole share made through links that went through rendered and decoded QR codes, the token both as parts in reverse order and as one code; another device cannot open the token; sizes), `crates/varsto-cli/tests/qr_cli.rs` (the CLI share flow with links only, `share qr` text and JSON, plain tokens refused, `/api/qr`, `/api/link/parse` with parts, `/api/qr/scan` with a camera frame, `open-link` through `/api/link`), `filemanager` tests (the Linux scheme handler and the Windows URL protocol), and `scripts/test/qr-scan-browser.py` (Chromium with a fake camera playing the codes: the token in parts and as one dense code fills Accept a shared folder, a pairing link fills the pairing form).

**Limits.** A dense single code needs a decent camera; parts need the Varsto scanner. A request link opened on a device without folders, or a token or pairing link on a device that already has a vault, is only explained, not acted on (accepting a share still needs a device directory of its own). Organization request codes and approval tokens (section 27) are not offered as QR codes yet. The iOS, Android and macOS changes have not been compiled or tried on devices.

## 52. Router port mapping for peer-to-peer (PCP, NAT-PMP, UPnP IGD)

**Why.** Hole punching (section 29 and before) fails behind symmetric NATs and relays cost a reachable device's upload. Most home routers will forward a port on request; a forwarded port makes the device directly reachable, which is the fastest route and lets it relay for the others.

**Order.** Before the NAT traversal of `p2p::stun` and `p2p::quic`, the service (`P2p::spawn_portmap_thread`, `p2p::portmap::PortMapper`) asks the router to forward the peer-to-peer port (the TCP and UDP listeners share the number), TCP and UDP, trying in order and stopping at the first protocol that maps anything:

1. **PCP** (RFC 6887): a `MAP` request (version 2, opcode 1) to the default gateway's UDP port 5351, with the client address as an IPv4-mapped IPv6 address, a random 12-byte nonce kept for renewals and removal, the protocol (6 or 17), the internal port, the same number as the suggested external port, and `::ffff:0.0.0.0` as the suggested external address. A version-0 answer (a NAT-PMP-only server) or no answer moves on.
2. **NAT-PMP** (RFC 6886): the public address request (opcode 0), then a mapping request (opcode 1 UDP, 2 TCP) to the same port 5351.
3. **UPnP IGD**: an SSDP `M-SEARCH` to 239.255.255.250:1900 for `InternetGatewayDevice:2` and `:1`; only answers whose `LOCATION` is an IP literal of the answering host on the LAN are followed. From the device description the `WANIPConnection:2`, else `:1`, else `WANPPPConnection:1` service on that same host is used, by SOAP over HTTP: `GetExternalIPAddress`, `GetSpecificPortMappingEntry`, `AddPortMapping` (description `Varsto P2P`), `AddAnyPortMapping` (version 2, when the port is taken by another computer) and `DeletePortMapping`.

Default gateways come from the routing table (`/proc/net/route` on Linux and Android, `route -n get default` on macOS, `route print -4 0.0.0.0` on Windows), else the `.1` of each private /24 the device has an address in. The three clients are written in `portmap.rs` rather than taken from crates (the UPnP crates bring a URL and XML stack of some thirty crates and no `GetSpecificPortMappingEntry`).

**Leases.** One hour is asked for (PCP, NAT-PMP and UPnP alike); a mapping is renewed at half of what the router granted, with the same nonce or parameters. A failed renewal is retried every minute until the lease runs out; then everything is mapped again from the start. A UPnP router that only takes permanent mappings (error 725) gets one, removed like the others. When nothing could be mapped, the protocols are tried again after 30 minutes (another network, UPnP switched on), and at once when the device's local address changes (checked every minute; the old mappings are removed first). Mappings are removed when the setting is turned off, when peer-to-peer stops and when the service stops (Quit, and now also SIGTERM and SIGINT, which end the service loop like Quit; should a sync hold it, the process exits ten seconds later as before).

**Respecting others.** Only the peer-to-peer port is ever mapped. An existing UPnP entry for the external port that points at another computer is never replaced: version 2 asks the router for any free port (`AddAnyPortMapping`), version 1 tries three random external ports (20000-59999), each checked first; a router's own conflict error (718) moves on as well. An entry that points at this device and port but carries another description was made by hand: it is used as it is, never renewed or removed (status "already open"). An entry with our description is a leftover of an earlier run and is taken over. PCP and NAT-PMP servers never replace another client's mapping by design. A router whose own external address is not public (private, CGNAT 100.64/10, unspecified) gains nothing: its mappings are removed again and the protocol counts as failed.

**Publishing.** The rendezvous record (`PeerRecord`, now version 2) has `mapped_tcp` and `mapped_udp`: the external addresses. Other devices try them right after the LAN addresses over TCP and first over QUIC (`PeerRecord::addrs`, `udp_addrs`); the record is republished when they change. After a mapping the service probes its own mapped UDP address (`Node::probe_self`); when that leads back (the router loops it back), the record says `reachable` and the device may relay for the others. There is no DHT record on this version.

**Fallback.** Every failure is silent: no router answering, refusals and timeouts end in the existing ways (STUN, punching, relays through own devices). Details go to the log only with `VARSTO_P2P_DEBUG`. A whole failed round costs about four seconds on the mapping thread, never the sync.

**Setting and status.** `P2pConfig::port_mapping` (`config.json`, default on): `varsto p2p port-mapping on|off`, `POST /api/p2p {"port_mapping": bool}` (applied within a second), the checkbox "Open the port on the router automatically" on the Peers page. `varsto p2p status`, `GET /api/p2p` (`portmap`: `state` off, trying, mapped or unavailable; `method` pcp, nat-pmp or upnp; `tcp`, `udp`, `lease_secs`, `existing`, `verified`, `detail`; and `portmap_text`) and the Peers page say "router port opened via UPnP: 203.0.113.5:17893" or "not available". `VARSTO_NO_PORT_MAPPING` turns it off for one process: the tests and any throwaway service set it, so that they never touch the router of the network they run in.

Tests: `p2p::portmap::tests`: encoders and parsers, route tables, an in-process PCP server and a NAT-PMP server on loopback (mapping of TCP and UDP, the fallback from PCP to NAT-PMP, renewal at half the lease with the same nonce, a short lease renewed in time, a failed renewal retried and a lapsed mapping mapped again, removal with lifetime 0, a router without a public address refused), a fake IGD (SSDP responder plus an HTTP server with the description and SOAP control: mapping after PCP and NAT-PMP fail, renewal, removal, another computer's entry left alone, `AddAnyPortMapping` on version 2, a hand-made mapping used and never touched, our leftover taken over on a permanent-only router), SSDP answers pointing elsewhere ignored, and nothing answering at all; `p2p::tests` (record order with mapped addresses). The lab has no router VM that offers UPnP yet.

A mapping is published only after the probe through it succeeded: section 60.

## 53. Minimum free disk space

**Why.** A sync that fills a disk breaks the device for everything else, and a full disk is the worst moment to write a ledger or a database. Like Syncthing's `minDiskFree`, Varsto stops writing new data before a disk runs full.

**Limits.** A limit is a share of the disk (`"5%"`, `"2.5%"`) or an amount (`"10GB"`, `"500MB"`, `"1.5GiB"`: decimal units are powers of 1000, binary ones of 1024; a plain number is bytes; `"0"` is no limit), stored as that string (`freespace::MinFree`). The default is 5 %. Where it applies:

- the disk holding a synced folder: `FolderMount::min_free` in `config.json` (this device only): pulls, fetches of placeholders, conflict copies, imports with a full local copy, the restic mirror (whose folders are folders), and the block cache of a folder kept encrypted on this device (the folder's limit, measured at the device directory's disk);
- a local-directory storage's disk: `StorageSpec::LocalDir::min_free`: block objects (`chunks/`, `packs/`); records, ledger batches and manifests are small and still written;
- the device directory's disk for blocks staged from pool disks (`disk_fetch`), and a restore target outside the folders: the default.

Pool disks keep their own reserve (section 34), 5 % or at least 2 GiB, unchanged; S3 and rclone storages have no disk here.

**Behaviour.** A write of n bytes is refused when the free space minus n would be below the limit (`freespace::check`), with `freespace::LowSpace`: "disk of folder Photos has 3 % free, below the 5 % limit; downloads paused", or, when the write itself would cross it, "disk of folder Photos has 6 % free; 2.0 GB more would leave less than the 5 % limit; downloads paused" (storages: "uploads paused"). A pull keeps such files pending (`pending_remote`) and counts them (`PullReport::files_waiting_for_space`, `low_space`), not as unavailable; smaller files that still fit are fetched. A push leaves a full storage out for the rest of the push (`PushReport::storages_unavailable`, as a pool without room) while the other storages get the blocks. A fetch, a restore or a staging step fails with the message; an import stops and continues when run again. Reading, uploading, deleting and moving to the trash go on. Places refused are remembered in the process (`freespace::short_places`); the service measures them again after every sync and every 30 seconds (`recheck`), and one that has room for the refused write again is dropped, logged ("disk of folder Photos has room again; downloads resume") and a sync starts at once. The service notifies once per place while it stays short. A disk whose space cannot be read is never refused.

**Surfaces.** `varsto folder set <folder> --min-free 10%|10GB|default` and `varsto storage set <name> --min-free ...` (through the running service when there is one); `POST /api/folder/min-free {folder, min_free}` and `POST /api/storage/min-free {name, min_free}` (empty or "default" resets); `GET /api/status` folders carry `min_free` (as set, or null) and `min_free_text`; `storage list` shows "(keeps 5 % free)" for directories; `GET /api/service` has `low_space` (the places short now, with `text`). The interface: "Free space…" in a folder row's menu and on directory storages (a dialog with the limit, Default resets), the storage line says "keeps 5 % free", and a banner on the Overview lists the places short of space, one line each. English, Finnish and Swedish.

Tests: `freespace::tests` (parsing and wording, refusal, recheck and resumption with a byte limit), `crates/varsto-core/tests/free_space.rs` with the fake provider (`pool::set_fake_space`): a pull below the limit writes nothing and keeps the files pending, resumes when space is back, a folder's own limit and the default again, a placeholder fetch refused and then done; a full directory storage left out of a push while another storage gets every block, filled in after its limit is lowered; a folder kept encrypted here waiting for space in the device directory.

## 54. Event stream for the interface

**Why.** The interface asked the service every few seconds (the service summary every 5 s, the Now card every 2 s, the progress bar every 1-5 s, the traffic view every second), most of the time for nothing new. Now the service tells.

**`GET /api/events`.** Server-Sent Events (`text/event-stream`), answered on a thread of its own without the state lock (`sse.rs`; at most 16 at a time, more get 503 with `Retry-After`). The token comes in `X-Varsto-Token` or `Authorization: Bearer`, never in the address (section 60); the page reads the stream with `fetch`. The response has no length and no chunking; every event is written and flushed as it happens, and the stream ends when the client goes away or the service stops. It starts with `retry: 3000` and an unnumbered `hello` event (`last_id`, this device's activity as in `/api/activity?mine=1`, the transfers as in `/api/progress`); a comment line goes out after 15 seconds of silence.

Each event is `id: <n>`, `event: <kind>`, `data: {"id", "kind", "utc", "data"}`. Numbers count up per service process (`varsto_core::events`); the last 1024 events are kept. A client that reconnects sends `Last-Event-ID` (the browser does by itself; `?last_id=` works too) and gets what it missed, or an unnumbered `reset` (`last_id`) when its number is older than the ring or larger than the newest (the service started again): the client loads everything again. Nothing is written to disk.

**Events.** Counts, names and states only; never file names or contents.

| Event | Data |
| --- | --- |
| `sync.started` | `reason` (files changed, requested, interval), `folder` for a requested one |
| `sync.finished` | `ok`; on success `folders`, `updated`, `deleted`, `conflicts`, `uploaded`, `unavailable`, `waiting_for_space`, `syncs`; on failure `error` |
| `files.new` | `folder`, `count` (files that came from other devices) |
| `trash` | `folder`, `count` (files deleted by other devices, moved to the trash) |
| `activity.started`, `activity.progress` | the task (`activity::TaskView`); progress at most once a second per task, only when done, total, step, file or percentage changed |
| `activity.finished` | `id`, `kind`, `subject` |
| `transfers` | `download`, `transfers` (as `/api/progress`), at most once a second while anything moves |
| `peer.connected`, `peer.disconnected` | `device`, `name`, `path`, `addr`: a peer that exchanged anything within the last minute, or no longer |
| `storage.problem`, `storage.ok` | `storage`, `text`: a storage a push had to leave out (no disk, full, below its free-space limit), and when it is back |
| `space.low`, `space.ok` | `place` (`folder:<name>`, `storage:<name>`, `device`, `path:<dir>`), `text` (section 53) |
| `folder.paused` | `folder`, `text`: a folder paused on this device (its marker is missing, or deletions are held; section 47), once per new reason |
| `notification` | `title`, `body`: every desktop notification the service raises |
| `config.changed` | `source` (`api` with the `path` of a successful POST, or `file` when `config.json` changed under the service) |
| `policy.changed` | `folder`, `state` (ok, at_risk, violated, unknown) |
| `ledger.health` | `state`, `text`, `warnings` (also published when the warnings change, section 74) |
| `backup` | `name` (or `restic`), `ok`, `text` |
| `move.waiting`, `move.done` | `id`, `folder`, `to`, and for a waiting job `kind`, `disk`, `bytes_left`, `text`; for a finished one `from`, `bytes`, `disks` (section 59) |

Two unnumbered events only for a connection that asks: `?traffic=1` adds `traffic` (the report of `/api/p2p/traffic`) every second; `?peers=1` adds `activity.peers` (`peers`, as in `/api/activity`) whenever it changes, and the service keeps asking the other devices while such a connection is open. Tasks, transfers and peers are sampled once a second by one thread while any stream is open.

**The interface.** One `EventSource` while the page is shown (closed while it is hidden, reopened with the last number when it shows again; reopened with other flags when a page that needs the peers' activity or the traffic is entered or left). The progress bar at the top and the Now card follow the `activity.*` events, the Peers page's traffic the `traffic` events, a download button's note the `transfers` events; syncs, changes, problems and notifications make the page load the service summary once (several events in a row ask once), configuration changes and resets also the status. The polls of section 41 and before run only while the stream is down; the service summary is still asked once a minute as a safety net. When the browser gives up reconnecting, the page tries again after 1 s, doubling up to a minute.

**Measured** (Chromium 2026-10, a throwaway vault and service of this build, 60 s per page after the page settled, one sync started from outside in the middle; old interface served by intercepting `app.js`): requests per minute on Overview 38 before, 13 after (the 11 requests reloading the page's data after the sync are in both); Peers 76 before, 5 after; Files 27 before, 4 after. Idle, without the sync: Overview 37 before, 2 after; Peers 66 before, 4 after; Files 16 before, 3 after (what is left after: the once-a-minute summary, the update check at start, reopening the stream with other flags when the page changes).

Tests: `events::tests` (numbering, ring, reset after a restart or a gap, waiting), `sse::tests` (hello, live events, resume after the last seen, reset, the traffic event, the sampler turning a task into started, progress and finished), and the measurement above with Playwright.

## 55. Read-only members of shared folders

Code: `crates/varsto-core/src/share.rs` (`ShareRole`, `ShareRoles`, `SignedShareRoles`, `JoinProof`) and `engine/share_roles.rs`.

**Why the others enforce it.** A member of a shared folder holds the folder key, and the folder key opens *and* encrypts: chunk keys, manifests and ledger batches are all derived from it. Nothing cryptographic can stop a key holder from writing objects to the storage. A **viewer** is therefore read-only because nobody takes what it writes: the owner's devices and the editors do not merge its manifests, do not count its ledger batches and ignore its records for the folder (file analyses, section 36; last-accessed times, section 13). Its own client refuses to write as well. An **editor** reads and writes, as every member did before roles existed; the owner's devices always write.

**Role record.** `vault/share-roles/<folder>/<seq, 10 digits>-<issuer device>.json`, written by one of the owner's devices: an envelope `{format_version, folder, seq, issuer, epoch, body_hex, sig_alg, sig_hex}`. The body is `{folder, seq, issuer, issued_utc, share_epoch, roles}` with `roles` mapping member device ids to `{role: "editor"|"viewer", name, since_utc, editor_until_clock, editor_until_seq}`, encrypted with XChaCha20-Poly1305 under `derive(K_e, "share-roles", folder)` where `K_e` is the folder key of share epoch `epoch` (section 24; 0 is the folder key itself), associated data `("share-roles", vault, folder, seq, issuer, epoch)`. The signature is the issuer's device signature over `("share-roles-signature", vault, folder, seq, issuer, epoch, BLAKE3(body ciphertext))`. A record is a snapshot: every reader adopts the valid record with the highest `(seq, issued_utc, issuer)` and never goes back to a lower one. Valid means: the issuer is one of the owner's devices as the reader knows them (on the owner's devices a trusted device of the vault, section 21 and 27; on a member the `owners` its token and the share epoch records named), the issuer is not revoked, the signature verifies with the issuer's key (the vault registry, or on a member the issuer's member record), the body opens and agrees with the envelope. A member cannot give itself or anyone a role: its signature does not count.

**Who is what.** Without any role record every member is an editor (folders shared before roles, and folders that never had a viewer). Once a folder has one, a member device the record does not list is a **viewer**: a new member writes only once an owner device has listed it. The first role record is written when the owner makes the first viewer token or changes the first role; it lists every member there at that moment as an editor.

**Tokens carry the role.** `share create --to <code> --role viewer|editor` puts the role, the invitation id (`invite_id` of the request key) and a random 256-bit **join secret** into the sealed token (`vst2` payload `{…, role, invite, join_secret_hex}`); the invitation record (`vault/share-invites/…`, section 24, readable by the owner's devices only) keeps the role and the secret. The member keeps both in its local folder record (`share_join`, never published) and publishes a **join proof** `vault/share-join/<folder>/<device>.json` = `{folder, device, invite, proof_hex}` with `proof = derive(join secret, "share-join-proof", folder, device)`. An owner device that sees a member without an entry looks up the invitation, checks the proof (constant-time comparison) and writes the next role record listing the member under the invitation's role. The proof binds the role to the device that opened the token: a viewer that copies another member's KEM key or request code does not know that invitation's secret. A plain token (`<vault>.<folder>.<key>.<name>`) has no room for a role; once a folder has a role record, plain tokens are refused, and `--role viewer` requires `--to`.

**Changing a role.** `varsto share role <folder> <member> viewer|editor`, `POST /api/share/role {folder, member, role}` and the role selector in the Shared page's member list write the next role record (owner devices only; in an organization only where the policy lets members share, or an administrator). Making an editor a viewer records `editor_until_clock`, the largest Lamport clock of the member's changes in the owner device's merged state, and `editor_until_seq`, its newest ledger batch held there: what it wrote while it was an editor stays valid. Making a viewer an editor takes its newest manifest from then on.

**Enforcement on the owner's devices and the editors.**

- Manifests: a pull does not open the manifest of a viewer (and does not advance `last_seen` for it, so a later editor role takes its newest one). `PullReport.writes_refused` names the viewers whose newer manifests were not taken.
- File versions relayed by others: a version whose `modified_by` is a viewer and whose `modified_clock` is above its `editor_until_clock` is not merged, whichever device's manifest carries it, so an editor that has not seen the role record yet cannot launder a viewer's change.
- Ledger: a viewer's batches after `editor_until_seq` are neither pulled nor counted (`devices.json` `share_viewers`, part of the ledger view's context like revocation cut-offs, so the view is rebuilt when roles change). Its claims and verifications of blocks therefore do not count toward policies either.
- Records: analyses and last-accessed records of viewers are skipped.

**The viewer's own client.** `share_read_only` is true on a member device whose own role is viewer (the newest role record listing it, else the role its token gave; unlisted in a folder with roles: viewer). Then a scan publishes nothing: a file changed or created in the folder is moved to `<home>/kept-aside/<folder name>/<path>.<utc>` (outside the folder, never synced), a changed or deleted file is put back from the shared version on the next pull, and files written by a pull lose their write permission (`set_readonly`, where the platform has it). Push publishes no manifest and uploads nothing; `PushReport.read_only` and `kept_aside`, `PullReport.kept_aside`, the command line ("read-only: your changes were not published") and the service's last result report it. Writes through the interface and the API (`write_file`, uploads, `mkdir`, moves, the block cache of an encrypted folder) are refused. `share members` shows `this_role`, the Files and Shared pages a "read only" mark.

**Interfaces.** CLI: `varsto share create <folder> --to <code> --role viewer|editor`, `varsto share role <folder> <member> viewer|editor`, `varsto share members` (each member's role, and a token's role). API: `POST /api/share/create {…, role}`, `POST /api/share/role`, `GET /api/share/members` (members carry `role`; the list carries `this_role` and `roles_in_use`); `FolderStatus.read_only`. Interface: a Role choice in the share dialog, a role selector and an Editor/Viewer mark per member in the Shared page's member list; English, Finnish and Swedish.

**Threat model.** A viewer keeps everything it can read and can copy it anywhere: no key can prevent that, as with any member (section 24). What the role gives is integrity for everyone else: nothing a viewer's devices publish for the folder is accepted, so they cannot add, change, delete or rename files of the others, even with a modified client. A viewer can still write objects to a storage it can reach (and delete or occupy names there, as section 24 says of a removed member), and can plant a thumbnail for content that has none yet (thumbnails are written once per content hash and are not attributed). Those are storage-level powers the storage credentials grant, not changes anyone merges.

**Limits.** Changes a modified client published while it was a viewer are taken if the owner later makes it an editor (its newest manifest is read then). Two owner devices writing role records at the same moment: the later `(seq, issued_utc, issuer)` wins and the other change is lost until it is made again. A member that joined with a plain token or before tokens named the owner's devices cannot verify role records and treats everyone as an editor; the owner's devices still refuse what a viewer writes, also when that member relays it. A new editor's first changes wait until an owner device has seen its join proof. Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/share_roles.rs` (a viewer reads, its changes are kept aside and undone, deletions undone, interface writes refused; a modified client that publishes anyway is refused by the owner and the editor; an editor writes; making the viewer an editor and a viewer again takes effect, with its editor-time change kept and readable by a new device; a forged role record is ignored; members cannot set roles; plain tokens refused once a folder has viewers).

## 56. Organizations: which device platforms may belong

Code: `crates/varsto-core/src/org.rs` (`OrgPolicy::allowed_platforms`, `min_app_version`, `refuses`, request codes) and `engine/org_ops.rs`, `engine/devinfo.rs`, `engine/membership.rs` (`trusted`, `belongs`).

**Policy.** Only in a vault with an organization (section 27). The roster's policy gains `allowed_platforms` (any of `linux`, `macos`, `windows`, `android`, `ios`; empty: every platform) and `min_app_version` (the oldest Varsto version a device may run, compared as `major.minor.patch[-pre.N]`; empty: any). Like the rest of the policy they are part of the roster signed by an administrator device, and every change is logged as `policy_changed` with the whole policy. Rosters that do not verify (section 27) never change it, so neither does a policy forged by a member or written by anyone with the storage credentials. Device kinds (desktop, phone, …) and hardware-backed keys are not in the policy: the code does not know a device's kind reliably. An administrator cannot set a policy that refuses its own device ("this policy would lock out this device"); unknown platform names are refused; listing every platform is stored as no list.

**What a device's platform is.** A device says it: (1) its request code `vor2.<KEM key>.<device id>.<platform>[.<name>]` names its platform, and the six-word fingerprint covers it (`derive_key("varsto share-request fingerprint v1", key, "<device> <platform> <name>")`, the context of section 24, outside `e2ee-sync-format/0`), so a platform changed on the way changes the words; `vor1` codes (no platform) are still read; the roster entry of an approved device keeps `platform`; (2) every full device publishes its details (`vault/devinfo/<device>/<utc>.enc`, section "Device details") with `platform` and `app_version`, now **signed** by the device's own key over `("device-details-signature", vault, the details as JSON without the signature)`. Readers keep only details whose signature verifies with that device's registry key, reading past newer records someone else planted under its name (at most 16), so a holder of the vault key cannot make another device look like another platform. A sync republishes them when they change (and at least every six hours, as before). The platform used is the signed details' one, else the roster's. Mobile shells set `VARSTO_OS`.

**Enforcement.** With a restricting policy:

- **Approval.** `org approve` refuses a request whose platform the policy does not allow ("the organization does not allow Android devices"), and a `vor1` code, which does not say ("update Varsto on the device and make a new request"). The version is checked later, from the signed details.
- **Devices already in the organization.** `trusted(device)` (section 21, 27) is false for a device whose platform is refused or unknown, or whose version is older than the minimum or unknown. Hence the other devices do not pull or count its ledger batches, do not merge its manifests (a pull now skips the manifests of every full device that is not trusted and not revoked; they stay unread and are taken once it is trusted again), refuse its peer connections, accept no revocation, grant or roster from it, and seal it no new vault key: a key epoch started meanwhile keeps it as a member (`belongs`: listed and not revoked, platform aside), and the key is sealed to it once it is allowed again. An administrator can still remove it.
- **The device itself.** When the signed policy it holds refuses its own platform or version, `ensure_active` fails with "this organization (Acme) does not allow Windows devices: this device does not sync or open the vault until an administrator allows it": sync, pull, push, fetching and reading files, the viewer and every action that changes the vault stop. It still reads the registry, revocations, key epochs and the organization's objects at the start of each sync, so a policy that allows it again (sealed under a key epoch started meanwhile, after its grant arrives) is seen and it carries on. `StatusReport.org.blocked`, `OrgSummary.this_device_blocked` and the banner of the interface say why.

**Interfaces.** CLI: `varsto org policy platforms --allow linux,macos,windows | --allow-all [--min-version 0.0.1-alpha.14|any]` (prints the policy and the devices it locks out), `varsto org policy` shows the platforms and minimum version, `varsto org status` shows each device's platform and "LOCKED OUT", `varsto org approve` shows the code's platform. API: `POST /api/org/policy` takes the two fields, `GET /api/org` gives `policy`, per device `platform` and `refused`, and `this_device_blocked`, `this_platform`; `POST /api/org/fingerprint` gives the code's `platform`. Interface: platform checkboxes and the minimum version in the Organization page's policy card, a "Locked out" mark per device, the platform in the approval question, and a banner on a locked-out device; English, Finnish and Swedish.

**Limits.** The platform and version are what the device's own app says: a modified app can claim another platform, so this binds honest apps (and makes a policy visible and enforced by every honest device), not an attacker who holds the vault key, who can read everything the key opens anyway (section 27). A device that has not published signed details yet (one that never synced with this version) counts as an unknown platform and is locked out once the policy restricts platforms, until it syncs once; a locked-out device does publish them. Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/org_platforms.rs` (an approval for a refused platform and a code without one refused, the platform in the fingerprint and the roster; a Windows device locked out after the policy changes: its files not taken, its own sync and reads refused, no key from a removal meanwhile; allowed again: its files taken, the key sealed to it, syncing again; a roster forged by a member and device details forged in another device's name ignored; a policy that would lock out the administrator refused), `crates/varsto-cli/tests/org_cli.rs` (`org policy platforms`).

## 57. Change history of files

Code: `crates/varsto-core/src/changes.rs` (events, the index, limits) and `engine/changes_ops.rs`.

**Sources.** Who changed what and when is read from manifests only, never from a local log of what this device did: a version is a `FileState` that a manifest names, identified by `(path, modified_by, modified_clock)`. A pull records the versions of every manifest it takes (the same manifests it merges: not those of read-only members, section 55, nor of untrusted devices, section 56, and not versions a viewer made), whether or not they win the merge; a device records its own versions when it publishes its manifest. Each event keeps the time of the first manifest that named it (`created_utc`, the publisher's clock), the device that made it, the device whose manifest carried it (`source`), and `verified`: the manifest's BLAKE3 hash matched the hash the publisher's signed ledger batch announced for it (`manifest_published`; the ledger view keeps the hash of each device's newest announced manifest, view cache format 5). A device's own versions are always verified; a member of a shared folder cannot read the owner's vault ledger, so the owner's manifests are unverified there.

**Actions.** For each path, with the versions recorded before: deleted (the version is a deletion; the event names the content that was there), created (no earlier version, or a deleted path coming back with content it never had), restored (a deleted path coming back with content it had before), modified. Among the versions recorded together (one manifest), a deletion and a creation of the same content by the same device are a **rename**: the old path's event says `renamed` with `to`, the new path's `renamed` with `from`. A move that also changes the content is a deletion and a creation. A file's history follows renames back: asking for the new path also lists the old path's versions before the move (up to 16 moves).

**Storage and bounds.** `state/changes/<folder>.jsonl`: one event per line, `{path, action, from?, to?, device, clock, time_utc, size, hash (first 16 hex digits of the keyed content hash), source, verified, backfilled?}`, appended. `state/changes/<folder>.json`: the index, per path the versions recorded (device, clock, deleted, content hash), so each version is recorded once, and whether the history was started. `config.json` `changes: {per_file, per_folder}` (defaults 100 and 20 000) bounds both: a path keeps its newest `per_file` versions (older ones offered later are ignored), and the events file is rewritten with the newest `per_folder` events once it grows a quarter past that. Both files survive restarts; they are local and never published.

**Starting.** The first pull of a folder on a device with this version (or `varsto changes` on a device that has not pulled since) reads the newest manifest of every device on the storages whose writes it takes, then adds the other devices' versions it holds that no manifest names any more, at the file's modification time (`backfilled`, unverified). Manifests are pruned to each device's newest (section 25), so older versions made before the history began on a device are not known to it: a device that joins later starts with the newest version of every file.

**Queries.** `varsto changes <folder> [<path>] [--device <name|id>] [--user <person>] [--since <YYYY-MM-DD[THH:MM]|seconds|7d|12h>] [--limit N] [--json]` (the name `varsto log` is left to logging): with a path that has versions, that file's versions (`file: true`); with any other path, the changes under it as a directory; without, the folder's recent changes; newest first, default 100 (0: all), `truncated` when more matched. `--user` matches the organization's person of the device (section 27). `GET /api/changes?folder=&path=&device=&user=&since=&limit=` gives `{folder, path, file, changes: [{…event, device_name, user?, source_name}], truncated}`. Interface: a Changes list in the file details and a Recent changes panel on the Files page with filters for a directory, a device, a person and a time; English, Finnish and Swedish.

**Limits.** Times are the publishers' clocks, and a change seen first through another device's manifest gets that manifest's time. A version made and replaced before any manifest named it (two edits between syncs) is not seen. Content events only: moving data between storages, placement and pool disks are not in it. Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/changes.rs` (edits on two devices with the right devices and order, verified against the ledger; a rename shown as a rename and followed back; a deletion and a restore; the folder feed by device, directory, time and limit; by person in an organization; the history surviving a restart; a device joining later starting from the manifests), `changes::tests` (actions, renames, limits, dates) and `crates/varsto-cli/tests/cli_smoke.rs`.

## 58. Ledger authorization and gap handling

A security review (2026-10-13) found that the ledger took every event from every signer whose batch opened, and decided fencing, gaps and revocations from object names and timestamps. Replicas and members of shared folders hold storage credentials and sign batches; the storages can withhold, reorder, replay and write junk. These rules follow. The storage format does not change: batch envelopes, checkpoints and epoch records keep their fields (the epoch record is now always the signed form of section 28).

**Who may say what.** The key id of a batch names the role it claims, and the signer must have that role in the device's registry (`EventScope`, `Engine::event_scope`):

| Key id | Scope | Signer | Events that count |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `ledger`, `ledger@<epoch>` | full | a trusted full device, or a removed one up to its cut-off | every event |
| `replica` | replica | a registered replica | `chunk_stored` and `chunk_verified` with an empty folder (object claims) |
| `share:<folder>[@<epoch>]` | member of `<folder>` | a known member device (or this device) | `chunk_stored`, `chunk_verified`, `chunk_on_device`, `manifest_published`, `chunk_dropped` of that folder |
