`device_enrolled` (the signer's own name) and unknown event types count in every scope; everything else outside the scope is left out when the batch is applied and counted (`LedgerView::events_refused`, `ledger_health.events_refused`). A member never retires a storage, adds a folder, takes or expires a snapshot or stops a backup; a replica only claims objects. `snapshot_expired` and `backup_stopped` therefore count only from full devices: the backup's source device or another trusted full device of the vault (section 31). Acknowledgements (`seen`) count only from full devices. A batch whose signer has no scope (a key id it has no role for) does not count at all. The scope of every device is part of the cached view's context, so a change of role rebuilds the view (view format 6).

A manifest that does not open with any key this device holds stops the folder's pull only when a trusted full device wrote it (a newer vault key has not arrived yet); from anyone else (a member, a replica, a removed device, or a name planted on the storage) it is skipped.

**Checkpoints speak for their signer.** Checkpoints are read only from full devices. When one is merged (`LedgerView::restricted_to`), every claim, verification, block held, manifest announcement, device name, snapshot and acknowledgement that names another device as its author is dropped; object and size stamps of other devices are dropped with them. Retirements, drops, folders, stopped backups and expiries of other devices' snapshots stay (a full device may make those), but of another device's snapshot only that it expired: its hash, which a restore trusts, and its times and sizes do not. A checkpoint more than 65 536 beyond the newest batch held of its device is refused unless the batch it ends at is beside it on the storage, signed by the device with the checkpoint's `head_hash`.

**Gaps.** The head of a device is the end of the unbroken chain of its batches held here (after its checkpoint). A batch whose predecessor is missing is kept in `ledger/<device>/pending/<seq>.json` (the signed batch, and the predecessor hash its body names when this device could open it) without moving the head. Every pull lists every storage after the head, so the missing batch is asked for again everywhere; when it arrives, the waiting batches follow it in order, each checked against the hash of the one before (one that names another predecessor marks the device forked). A checkpoint that covers waiting batches drops them (one at its own number must agree with it). A device restoring its own history from the storages (`varsto ledger rebuild`, section 38) still moves past a gap no storage can fill, so it never signs a number it used.

**Freshness.** After every pull, a device compares what the full devices acknowledged (`seen` in their signed batches, section 22) with its own heads: when another device says it holds batches of device A up to 50 while this device's head for A is 30 (never beyond a removed device's cut-off), or batches of A wait for a predecessor, it records `ledger_health.behind` = `{device, name, held: 30, seen: 50, pulls}`; the pull just asked every storage for 31..50. When the same gap is still there after three pulls in a row, `ledger_health.warnings` says "a storage may be withholding ledger batches of <A>", `varsto status` and `varsto ledger status` show it after "ok", and the Help page shows a warning (English, Finnish, Swedish). The record is kept in `ledger/watch.json`.

**Damaged and absurd batches.** A batch that does not parse, names another device or number, or does not verify on one storage is skipped and counted (`ledger_health.batches_malformed`, last pull) instead of stopping the pull: another storage may hold the real one. A batch whose Lamport time is more than 2^40 ahead of the reader's clock is refused (`Ingest::Refused`, `ledger_health.batches_refused`) and not applied, so one value cannot win every later ordering. A batch under a key this device does not hold yet is checked when it is applied, not when it is stored: see Limits.

**Fencing only on verified objects.** A device fences itself (section 6) only when an object under its own name verifies with its own key and is not its own (another copy of the identity signed it). Anything else under `ledger/<device>/` (a file that does not parse, another device's batch copied there, a checkpoint that does not verify) is junk: it is noted once per storage and object (`ledger/watch.json`), never read again, reported as `ledger_health.junk_objects` with a warning, and deleted by `varsto ledger clean-junk --yes` (without `--yes` it lists them; the device's genuine objects are never touched). The same rule holds for the check before every signature (`own_objects_ahead`), for pushes (a name occupied by junk is skipped on that storage) and for `varsto ledger rebuild` (the newest checkpoint and batches that verify are used).

**Key epochs.** Every vault writes epoch records as signed envelopes (`SignedEpoch`); a number taken by anything that does not parse or verify against a trusted device (an administrator, in an organization) is skipped by a removal, which takes the next free number. A name under `vault/share-epochs/<folder>/` makes a member see itself removed (`access_lost`) only when an owner device (a participant, for members from before tokens named the owners) signed a grant of that epoch to someone.

**Revocations in epoch order.** A revocation counts only when `vault/epochs/<new_epoch>` exists as a signed record of the revocation's issuer and, once this device holds that epoch's key (adopted through a chain of epoch records from a key it held), opens and lists the revoked device among those it removed; the revoked device itself, which never gets the key, checks the issuer's signed envelope. Revocations are applied in the order `(new_epoch, issuer)` and key epochs are adopted one at a time from the lowest, the revocations of each applied before the next: a device removed in epoch 1 can neither revoke anyone in epoch 2 nor hand out its key. `issued_utc` is shown, never used for ordering.

**Two epochs under one number.** Two devices removing at the same instant through different first storages can each write epoch `n`. A device that finds two different signed records under one number at or above its current epoch (verified with their issuers' device keys) records both issuers in `epoch-fork.json` in its device directory, applies neither record's revocations, adopts neither key, reports ledger health `epoch_fork` and signs nothing (ledger batches, removals) until the user runs `varsto device resolve-epoch <n> --keep <device>`; the kept device must be trusted. From then on only the kept issuer's record, grants and revocations of epoch `n` count. A device that already adopted the other record's key refuses the choice: pair it again.

**Limits.**

- A storage that withholds a batch from every reader and the acknowledgements that would reveal it (all of them) is not caught by the storage alone: there is nothing signed there to compare with. Since the release after 0.0.1-alpha.15 devices also compare their heads with what trusted full devices tell them over peer-to-peer (section 74), so a storage that is the only channel between the devices, and withholds everything including the rendezvous records, is still not caught: the devices must meet. Batches of devices that never acknowledge or send statements (members, replicas) are only caught by a later batch of theirs that waits for the missing one.
- The Lamport check needs the batch opened: a batch kept unread (a key this device does not hold yet) is applied as it is once the key arrives.
- A trusted full device can still sign a checkpoint and the batch it ends at far ahead and make readers jump; full devices are trusted with the vault anyway.
- `key_id` is not covered by the batch signature yet (a storage can relabel it); a relabelled batch then does not open, or opens under a scope its signer does not have and does not count. Signed key ids are a format change (planned).
- Manifests, trash records and snapshots are still accepted on the folder key alone; signing them is a format change (planned).
- The removed device trusts the signed envelope of its own revocation's epoch: a thief who acts first still removes the others (section 21).

Tests: `ledger::tests::checkpoint_cannot_speak_for_other_devices`, `ledger::tests::checkpoint_far_ahead_is_refused`, `crates/varsto-core/tests/sharing.rs` (`a_members_batch_cannot_reach_outside_its_folder`, `junk_share_epoch_does_not_cut_a_member_off`), `crates/varsto-core/tests/backups.rs` (`a_replica_cannot_expire_snapshots`), `crates/varsto-core/tests/ledger_gaps.rs` (a withheld batch, acknowledgements that reveal one and the warning after three pulls, a malformed batch on one storage, an absurd Lamport time), `crates/varsto-core/tests/revocation.rs` (`junk_under_a_devices_ledger_name_does_not_fence_it`, `junk_epoch_record_does_not_block_revocation`, `a_backdated_revocation_does_not_win`, `two_epochs_under_one_number_wait_for_a_choice`), and the lab scenario `ledger-withhold` (section 74 adds `ledger-rollback`).

## 59. Staged moves to disk pools

Code: `crates/varsto-core/src/staged_move.rs` (the job file), `engine/staged_moves.rs` (running jobs) and the `move` commands in `crates/varsto-cli/src/placing.rs`.

**Why.** A move (section 23) copies a folder's blocks from one storage to another in one run. A pool of removable disks (section 19) is filled while the disks are docked one at a time, so a two-terabyte download in a cloud storage cannot be moved onto a stack of 500 GB disks in one run: a pool without an attached disk, or without room, is simply left out. A *move job* is a move that goes on over as many runs as it takes: it moves what fits on the disks attached now, stops, says which disk to attach next, and carries on by itself when one is attached. Optionally the data then leaves the cloud storage, once the pool holds the copies the policy needs.

**Commands.**

- `varsto move <folder> --from <storage> --to <pool|storage> [--path <dir-or-file>]... [--idle-days N] [--keep-source] [--job] [--dry-run] [--confirm-cold-read] [--yes]`. A move to a pool, or with `--path`, `--keep-source` or `--job`, is recorded as a job and run once with the disks attached now; other moves behave as in section 23. `--path` (repeatable) limits the job to the files at or under those paths, which are relative to the folder or absolute inside its directory on this device; a path that holds no current file is refused. `--keep-source` copies and checks but never drops from `--from`. The same request while its job is open continues that job instead of starting another. The CLI asks (with the plan's summary) unless `--yes`, and exits with code 1 when blocks of a finished job stay on the source.
- `varsto move status [id]` lists open and paused jobs (or one job, finished or not) with bytes done, bytes left and the disk each waits for; `varsto move pause <id>`; `varsto move resume <id>` (opens a paused job and runs it now); `varsto move cancel <id>`. These need no passphrase except `resume`. Cancelling never deletes anything: what was moved stays moved and what was copied stays copied, so the ledger and the storages stay consistent; a new job for the same folder picks up the rest.
- `varsto status` lists the open jobs under the folders, one line each (`status --json` adds `move_jobs` when the service answers). `GET /api/moves` lists the jobs; `POST /api/move/state {id, action: pause|resume|cancel}` changes one; the service summary has `move_jobs`.

**The job file.** `<home>/state/moves/<id>.json`, `id` = `mv-` and eight hex digits, written atomically after every group of blocks and at the end of every run: `folder` (name) and `folder_id`, `paths`, `from`, `to`, `idle_days`, `keep_source`, `confirm_cold_read`, `policy` (the folder's copy rules when it started, in words), `state` (`open`, `paused`, `done`, `cancelled`), `bytes_total`, `bytes_done`, `bytes_remaining`, `blocks_total`, `blocks_done`, `need_second_place_bytes`, `disks_used` (labels), `started_utc`, `last_progress_utc`, `finished_utc`, `runs`, `waiting` (`kind` disk, place or room; `disk`, the known disk with room that continues the job; `text`), `room_sig`, `room_needed`, `kept_blocks` and `errors` (the last 20). The file holds no block names. What is left to move is worked out from the ledger at every run, so the counters are informative: a run recomputes `bytes_remaining` and corrects `bytes_done` (done = total - left), and a crash, a restart or another device finishing part of the move changes nothing but the numbers. A process that runs a job holds `<id>.lock` (created exclusively, refreshed after every group, taken over when older than ten minutes), so the service and the command line never move the same blocks at once.

**A run.** It pulls the ledger, plans the move as in section 23 (limited to `paths`; with `keep_source` the folder's copy rules do not keep blocks, nothing is dropped), then moves the blocks in groups of 64: write to the target unless it has the block, read back and hash-check, record `chunk_stored` and `chunk_verified` for the target, and (unless `keep_source`) record `chunk_dropped` for the source, seal and push the batch, then delete from the source, exactly as in section 23. The source copy of a group's block is dropped only when the target copy is checked and the policy still holds. A pool target is written through the pool itself (section 19): a block that finds no attached disk with room within the reserve, or no disk at all, is neither copied nor kept; the run notes the size and skips every block at least that large without reading it, and goes on with smaller blocks. A storage at its quota (section 44) waits the same way. When a run has moved what fits, the job stays `open` with `waiting`: `kind: "disk"` and the label of the known disk with the most room (its last measurement, section 40) or "attach a new disk" when no known disk has room. The service limits a run to 16 groups and goes on at its next turn.

**Disk groups.** For a pool with a `copies` rule (section 23) a block counts as moved only when the pool holds it in that many different places. After writing, the run adds copies on attached disks of other places; while a block is held in fewer places its source copy stays and the block is counted in `need_second_place_bytes` (`waiting.kind` is `place`: "N GB need a copy on a disk kept in another place"). With the second disk attached the next run adds the copies and drops the sources. With `keep_source` a block counts as done under the same rule.

**Done.** A job is done when nothing in its scope is left waiting. Blocks that stay on the source because the folder's policy needs the copy there, a cold read is not confirmed, or no readable copy was found are listed in `errors` and `kept_blocks`; they do not hold the job open. A finished whole-folder job (no `paths`, no `idle_days`, no `keep_source`, nothing kept) also replaces the source with the target in the folder's placement, as a whole-folder move does (section 23), so new blocks go to the pool too. Until then the folder's placement should name the source, otherwise `disk add` and pushes would fill the pool with the whole folder by themselves.

**Automatic continuation.** The background service (every 30 seconds, with its disk listing, and after every sync) asks which open jobs are ready and runs them within its share. A job to a pool is ready when an attached disk has at least `room_needed` bytes (the smallest block that found no room) and the attached disks with their room differ from what the job saw when it last waited (`room_sig`): a disk was attached (`disk add`, `disk insert`, `disk check`, a mount appearing) or a disk's space changed (blocks freed, files deleted). A job to an ordinary storage is ready after every sync. A paused service, a locked vault and paused or finished jobs run nothing. `disk add`, `disk init`, `disk insert` and `disk check` say which jobs can continue with the disks attached (`varsto move resume <id>` does it at once).

**Next disk to dock.** `Engine::disk_suggestions` (section 40) takes the open jobs that wait for room on a pool (`moves`: id, folder, pool, `bytes_left`, `need_second_place_bytes`, `room_needed`). A disk of that pool whose free space within its reserve is at least `room_needed` gets a reason of kind `move`: "continue the move of <folder>: N GB left; this disk has M GB free", worth `65 × min(room / bytes left, 1)` points (at least the suggestion threshold), so a known disk that can continue a job is suggested before one that is merely overdue for a check; attached disks with room are listed apart as before. When no attached or known disk of the pool has room, `new_disk_needed` lists the job with "a new disk is needed" and the commands `varsto disk init <device> --pool <pool> --label <name>` or `varsto disk add <mount> --pool <pool> --label <name>`; `varsto disk suggest` prints them.

**Interface and events.** The Storages page shows, in each pool card, a line per waiting job with the bytes left, the bytes that need a second place and "Attach disk «X»" or "A new disk is needed"; a banner under the page header lists the waiting jobs from the service summary. The Help page (section 41) has a step and an attention line per waiting job. The event stream (section 54) has `move.waiting` (`id`, `folder`, `to`, `kind`, `disk`, `bytes_left`, `need_second_place_bytes`, `text`), sent when a run ends waiting with a different reason than the run before, and `move.done` (`id`, `folder`, `from`, `to`, `bytes`, `disks`). The service raises a desktop notification for both.

**Limits.** Jobs are per device (`<home>/state`): another device does not see them, though it sees the moved blocks in the ledger. The room of a pool is the free space the file system reports; a disk whose reserve leaves less than the smallest remaining block counts as full. A group rule needs disks in enough different places to exist; a job cannot finish with fewer. Moving a block to a pool does not change which disks a later `disk check` fills.

Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/staged_move.rs` (a folder moved to a pool disk by disk with the suggestion and the service trigger, `--keep-source`, `--path`, a disk group rule needing two places, pause and cancel, a job resumed after the engine is dropped between groups, an ordinary storage target and the run lock), `crates/varsto-core/src/disksuggest.rs` (ranking for waiting jobs), `crates/varsto-cli/tests/cli_smoke.rs` (`staged_move_jobs_through_the_cli`) and the scenario `staged-move-to-pool` of the lab (`scripts/test/lab/scenarios.py`).
## 60. Service and transport hardening

Changes that tighten the local service, the peer transport and the reads from storages. None of them changes an object or record on a storage.

**STUN side channel (section 52).** The shared UDP socket queues a datagram that looks like STUN only while `Node::stun` has a request open, only when bytes 8 to 20 equal the transaction id of that request, and into a bounded queue of 64 entries (`try_send`). Everything else is dropped without allocating and counted. Before, any host on the internet could grow the queue of an unbounded channel without limit by sending STUN-shaped datagrams to the UDP port. Test: `p2p::quic::tests::unsolicited_stun_flood_is_not_queued`.

**Session token out of addresses and argv (S3).** The service no longer hands the browser an address with the session token. `varsto desktop`, the tray, `varsto open-link`, the service's own browser start and the FFI `varsto_url` open `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/?boot=<code>`: a code of 32 random bytes (64 hex digits) that is valid for 60 seconds and works once (`POST /api/session/boot`, authenticated by the token header, mints it; at most 256 codes are kept). The page trades it with `POST /api/session` `{"boot": "<code>"}` for `{"token": …}`, keeps the token in `sessionStorage`, and removes the parameter from the address at once; a second use or an expired code answers 401. The token stays what it was: random per service start, written to `service.json` (0600), read by the CLI, the tray and the MCP server from there. The `?token=` parameter is not accepted on any route. The token is read from `X-Varsto-Token` or `Authorization: Bearer`. Thumbnails are fetched by the page with the header and shown from blob URLs. A download (`/api/open`) or viewer (`/api/view`) link, which a browser opens without headers, carries `ticket=<random>` from `POST /api/ticket` `{route, folder, path}`: the ticket opens exactly that route, folder and path, for ten minutes, any number of times (a video asks for ranges). Every answer under `/api/` has `Cache-Control: no-store`. The FFI adds `varsto_token()` for the mobile shells' own API calls. Tests: `desktop::tests::boot_code_opens_the_session_once`, `expired_boot_code_is_refused`, `the_token_is_never_accepted_in_the_address`, `tickets_open_exactly_one_file`.

**Request handling (S2).** Query values are decoded on bytes (`%XX` with two ASCII hex digits, else the byte as it is), so a malformed escape such as `%aé` cannot split a character; a panic in a request handler answers 500 and the API worker carries on (before, the one worker thread died and every stateful route hung). Test: `desktop::tests::percent_decode_survives_malformed_escapes`.

**Strongroom key commitment (S4, section 17).** See section 17: `check_hex`, `-t pin=true`, `--uv`. Test: `crates/varsto-core/tests/strongroom.rs::a_wrong_folder_key_is_refused_by_unlock_convert_and_rekey`.

**Passphrases and secrets off the command line (S5, section 28).** A device passphrase must be at least 12 characters after NFKC normalisation (`crypto::check_passphrase`, checked by `Keys::save` and before `init`, `join`, `pair`, `share accept` and `org join` create anything; the interface's forms say "At least 12 characters"). Keys already written with a shorter passphrase still open. Nothing secret is taken from the command line any more, because every process of the user can read it (`ps`, `/proc/<pid>/cmdline`) and the shell keeps it in its history. The options `join --vault-key` and `--words`, `--s3-secret-access-key` (`join`, `share accept`), `storage add-s3 --secret-access-key`, `mcp --key`, the positional shares of `recovery combine` and `org admin add|remove --root-words` are gone. Each secret now comes from, in order: a file named by `--key-file` (`join`; the file holds 64 hex digits or the 24 words), `--s3-secret-file`, `--file` (`recovery combine`, one share per line), `--root-words-file`, `--key-file` (`mcp`); the environment variable `VARSTO_VAULT_KEY`, `VARSTO_S3_SECRET`, `VARSTO_ORG_ROOT_WORDS`, `VARSTO_MCP_KEY`; standard input when it is not a terminal (`join`, `recovery combine`, `org admin`, `share accept`, `storage add-s3`); otherwise a prompt without echo. On macOS the passphrase is stored in the login keychain by feeding `add-generic-password` to `security -i` on standard input instead of `-w <passphrase>` in the arguments. Tests: `crates/varsto-cli/tests/cli_smoke.rs::secrets_stay_off_the_command_line` (a short passphrase refused with nothing written, the removed options refused, `join` reading the key from standard input), `main::tests` (key text parsing). The recovery guide, the kit text and the lab scenarios use the new forms.

**Updater freeze and rollback protection (S6, section 14).** A download site that serves an old signed release, or withholds a newer one, cannot make a client install the old one. (a) `manifest.json` must carry a valid release signature (`manifest.json.minisig`, trusted comment `file:manifest.json`, the version it names must equal `_version`); an unsigned manifest is refused. (b) The signing time in the trusted comment of `SHA256SUMS.sig` must not be older than the newest one accepted before, kept in `update-state.json` in the device directory (`{last_timestamp, last_version}`, moved forward only after the archive passed every check); an older one is refused as a rollback. A signed list more than 90 days old is accepted with a warning, since it may simply be the newest release. (c) `MIN_ACCEPTED_VERSION` is the version compiled into the binary: a version equal to or older than it is never installed. (d) The GitHub cross-check is required: when the release list there cannot be fetched, the update is postponed with the reason, and `varsto update --allow-single-source` goes on with the signature alone (the interface never does). `scripts/sign-release.sh` signs `manifest.json` next to `SHA256SUMS` and `--verify` checks both; `website/deploy.sh` refuses to publish a manifest without a valid signature. Tests: `update::tests` (`update_refuses_a_checksum_list_older_than_the_last_accepted`, `the_manifest_must_carry_the_release_signature`, `the_same_or_an_older_version_is_never_installed`, `update_waits_when_github_does_not_answer`, `a_signed_list_that_is_months_old_is_reported`).

**Bounded storage reads and pack index validation (S7, sections 28 and 30).** No read from a storage can make the program allocate more than the object kind allows. `Storage::get_bounded(key, max)` refuses an object larger than `max`; `get` is `get_bounded` with `storage::read_limit(key)`: `MAX_CHUNK_SIZE + 64` (16 MiB and 64 bytes) for `chunks/` and `packs/`, 256 MiB for records (`vault/`, `manifests/`, `ledger/`, `org/` and the rest). The local directory and pool disks check the file size before reading and read at most one byte past the limit; S3 refuses a `Content-Length` over the limit before reading the body, caps the body it reads (it was 2 GiB for every request) and uses checked arithmetic for ranges; rclone is run with `--count max+1` and its output is read through a cap (a listing may be up to 1 GiB), and the process is killed when it runs past it. A block read in the engine (fetch, scrub, verify, repair) is bounded by `MAX_CHUNK_SIZE + 64`. A claimed range (`offset`, `len` of a pack member) is clamped to a block object, with overflow-checked addition. `PackIndex::validate(key)` checks an index read from a storage: its pack id is 64 lowercase hex digits and equals the id in the index's own key, at most 4096 members, every member name is 64 lowercase hex digits (names were sliced with `[..2]`, which panics in the middle of a multi-byte character), `0 < len <= MAX_CHUNK_SIZE + 64`, and `offset + len` does not overflow and is at most 64 MiB. An index that fails is skipped, logged and counted (`PackAware::invalid_indexes`): not trusted, not a panic. A panic while reading one object during scrub, verify, repair or a fetch (`storage::guarded`) is an error for that object, not the end of the pass. Tests: `packs::tests::an_index_that_lies_is_rejected_not_trusted`, `hostile_indexes_in_a_storage_do_not_panic_a_listing`, `s3::tests::a_response_that_claims_two_gib_is_refused_without_reading_it`, `a_block_read_is_capped_at_a_chunk_not_a_record`, `storage::bounded_read_tests`. Format 2 (section 62) replaces the limits of `packs/` objects and the JSON pack indexes; see "Read limits" there.

**restic details out of logs and support bundles (S8, sections 35 and 45).** The log scrubber cuts the login of every URL: `scheme://user:password@host/...` becomes `scheme://[redacted]@host/...` (`redact::scrub`), so `rest:https://u:p@h/r` and `s3:https://K:S@h/b` never reach a log line, wherever they came from. The repository is not logged at all: a debug line for a restic call names the repository's kind and host only (`restic::repository_summary`: `rest:https://h`, `sftp:nas`). Messages, `varsto restic status` and the recovery kit's note show the repository without its login (`restic::display_repository`), and the status shows only the first word of a password command (the rest of a command line may hold the password). The `config.json` copy in a support bundle (`bundle_extras`, `redact::redact_config_json`) has `restic.password`, `restic.env_file` and every `access_key_id` and `secret_access_key` replaced by `[redacted]`, and the login of every URL cut; it is no longer copied as it is. Tests: `redact::tests::url_userinfo_is_cut_everywhere`, `a_config_copy_for_a_bundle_has_no_credentials`, `restic::tests::repositories_are_shown_without_their_login`, `restic` (CLI) `tests::status_and_kit_show_no_login_and_no_password_command_arguments`.

**MCP keys (S9, section 37).** The time a key was last used is kept in `mcp-keys-used.json` (`{key id: seconds}`, at most one write a minute), not in `mcp-keys.json`: checking a key reads the key file and never writes it, so a check running while `revoke` writes the file can no longer put the revoked key back (it used to read the whole file, set the time and write it back). A revoked key's use record goes with it. `varsto mcp` without a key is refused while the service runs ("the MCP server then needs an MCP key"): the session token would give the assistant everything; with no service it still opens the vault itself with `VARSTO_PASSPHRASE`. `GET /api/status` with a scoped key answers `{"folders": [...]}` with the folders the key may read and nothing else: no devices, no storages, no vault identity. Tests: `mcp_keys::tests::a_check_never_rewrites_the_keys_and_cannot_resurrect_a_revoked_key`, `crates/varsto-cli/tests/mcp.rs` (`a_scoped_key_reaches_only_its_folders_through_the_service`).

**Names that cannot redraw the terminal (S10, sections 27, 46 and 51).** `names::check_display_name` refuses a name of a device or a person with a control character (`ESC[2A` moves the cursor and redraws the fingerprint line), a zero-width character (U+200B to U+200F, U+2060 to U+2064, U+FEFF), a line or paragraph separator, a bidirectional control (U+202A to U+202E, U+2066 to U+2069, U+061C), or more than 64 characters. It runs when a name is made (device names at `init`, `join`, `pair`, `share accept` and `org join`; the names bound into a share request or an organization request) and again when a name is read from a code (`ShareRequest::parse`, `OrgRequest::parse`, a QR link), because a code is made by someone else. The command line shows every name through `names::display` (the same characters, if one gets in anyway, as `\u{..}` escapes): the QR caption, the approval questions and the member table. The page puts names in with `textContent`. Tests: `names::tests::display_names_refuse_control_invisible_and_direction_characters`, `vault::tests::request_codes_with_control_or_direction_characters_are_refused`, `org::tests::request_names_with_control_or_direction_characters_are_refused`.

**Disk markers carry a MAC (S11, section 34).** `.varsto-disk.json` holds the pool id and the vault tag in plain text on every disk of a pool, so anyone who has held one disk could copy the two fields under a new disk id and make a disk that every device of the vault adopted (and, with the most free space, chose as the favourite write target). A marker now also carries `mac_hex = keyed_hash(derive(root, "disk-pool-marker"), aad("disk-pool-marker", pool_id, disk_id, created_utc))` (length-prefixed fields; `aad` is the same framing as elsewhere). A disk that is not in the pool's registry is adopted on sight only when its MAC is valid (constant-time comparison); a marker with the pool id and vault tag but no valid MAC makes it an unknown disk: it gets no writes and is not in the registry, `varsto disk list` names it ("unknown disk ... carries no valid marker MAC ... run `varsto disk add <mount> --pool <pool> --label <label>`"), and `varsto disk add` (also `init` and `insert`, which register or re-attach drives) takes it in on purpose and writes the MAC; `disk list` and a re-attach add the MAC to the marker of a known disk that predates it. A copy of a real marker with another disk id fails the MAC (the disk id is in the message), as does the real MAC copied along with it. A disk already in the registry is recognised by its id as before. Tests: `pool::tests::a_copied_marker_without_a_valid_mac_is_never_adopted`, `crates/varsto-core/tests/disk_pool.rs::a_disk_with_a_copied_marker_is_not_adopted_until_the_user_adds_it`.

**Small network and file fixes (S12, sections 15, 28, 52).**

- *Port mappings.* A router mapping (`mapped_tcp`, `mapped_udp` of the peer record, section 52) is published only after the probe through the mapped UDP address came back to us (`PortMapStatus.verified`); before that the record carries none and a mapping alone does not make the device "reachable". Test: `service::tests::a_mapping_is_published_only_after_the_probe_reached_us`.
- *TCP peer server (section 15).* A request head must arrive within 5 seconds of its first byte; a new connection that starts none is closed after 5 seconds (a connection kept open between requests still has 30 seconds). At most 8 connections of one address may hold a request head that has begun and is not whole; the next one to begin a head is closed at once. A head normally arrives in one piece and then takes no slot, so a peer with many parallel requests (the fetch window is 32) is not held back, while a slowloris fills its eight slots and loses them at the deadline. Test: `p2p::tests::unfinished_request_heads_are_limited_and_time_out`.
- *QUIC.* `ServerConfig::max_incoming` is 256, a connection may have 64 bidirectional streams open (the fetch window is 32) and no unidirectional ones.
- *Temporary files.* The files Varsto writes beside a folder's files while downloading (`.varsto-tmp-<pid>-<random>`), writing through the engine (`.varsto-upload-…`) and importing (`.varsto-import-…`) are created with `O_EXCL` under a random name and mode 0600, so a link or file another local user plants under the old name `.varsto-tmp-<pid>` is never written through. The mode the folder records for the file (default: what a new file gets here) is set with `fchmod` on the open file before it is renamed into place; before, the file had the default mode from the rename until the metadata step. Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/temp_files.rs`.
- *Paths.* A manifest, a trash record or a note that names a path with a component beginning `.varsto` (except `.varstoignore`) or ending `.varsto-placeholder` or `.varsto-link` is refused (`names::check_synced_path`): those are Varsto's own files and are never synced, so an honest device never writes such a path, and one that did would overwrite the folder marker or a placeholder of another device. Tests: `names::tests::synced_paths_never_name_a_file_of_varsto_itself`, `manifest::tests::a_manifest_cannot_name_files_of_varsto_itself`.

## 62. Format 2: blocks, packs and the keyed chunker

A security review (2026-10-13, findings H1 and H8) found that the nonce of a chunk object was derived from the chunk id while the encrypted input was the zstd output, which is not guaranteed to be the same across zstd versions, and that a storage could recognise a known file from the sizes of its block objects, because cut points were a public function of the content and every block was an object of its own. Format 2 changes the blocks; the records (signed envelopes, grants, recovery) change alongside it in their own section. Vaults are created fresh; nothing older is read. `FORMAT_VERSION` is 2 (`varsto_core::FORMAT_VERSION`, bound into every associated-data string and written to `vault/meta.json` and `vault.json`): a build refuses a vault of any other value before it reads a block, and the message says what to do (`varsto_core::format_mismatch`): for format 1, the vault of 0.0.1-alpha.14 and earlier, that it has to be recreated (`varsto reset --yes`, `varsto init`, sync the files into the new vault); for a newer format, to update. Measured in the lab (scenario `upgrade-from-alpha-14`): a build with format 1 still in `FORMAT_VERSION` opened an alpha.14 vault (`status` worked) but found none of its 3135 blocks, since they are objects of their own under `chunks/` and no longer read; the bump turns that into a clear refusal. Alpha.14 itself refuses a format 2 vault with its own text, "vault format version 2 is not readable by this version (1)".

**Nonce and associated data.** A chunk object is `nonce || ciphertext || tag` (XChaCha20-Poly1305) as before, made by one function (`chunk_seal::seal_chunk`) for every writer: a push, the block cache, an import, a data move, a Strongroom conversion and a peer serving a block again. The key is `derive(K9 of the epoch, "chunk-key", folder, chunk id)`; the nonce is the first 24 bytes of `keyed_hash(derive(K9 of the epoch, "chunk-nonce", folder), packed)`, where `packed` is the exact AEAD input (section 16); the associated data is (format, AEAD, `chunk`, vault, folder, epoch, chunk id, plaintext length, packed length). A reader (`chunk_seal::open_chunk`) takes the packed length from the object's length, opens it, checks that the nonce is the one the packed bytes give (an object with a nonce of its own choosing is refused), unpacks it and checks the plaintext against the chunk id. The same bytes therefore always give the same object (deduplication and content addressing hold); two encodings of one chunk give two objects and two nonces, never one nonce over two inputs.

**Compression.** zstd level 3 after a 64 KiB level-1 probe, as section 16 describes. Measured on one core of the build machine (release build, 64 MiB in 1 MiB chunks): log-like text 51 MB/s at level 12 against 560 MB/s now, stored as 12.2 % and 14.7 % of the input; random data 1.7 GB/s against 19.5 GB/s (stored raw either way).

**Keyed chunker and size tiers.** The Gear table is 256 words of the BLAKE3 output keyed by the folder's chunker seed `derive(K11, "chunker-seed", folder)` (`chunking::gear_table`). K11 is fixed at the folder's first epoch, so a key rotation moves no cut point and deduplication survives it. Block sizes follow the file size (`ChunkerParams::for_size(size, seed)`): under 1 MiB one block; 1 to 32 MiB 256 KiB to 4 MiB, average 1 MiB; 32 MiB to 1 GiB 1 to 16 MiB, average 4 MiB; from 1 GiB 2 to 16 MiB, average 8 MiB. Content of unknown size (an import from a stream, the block cache without a size) is cut with the 1 MiB tier. A file entry cut with the tiers of its size says `chunking: 2` (absent otherwise), so a device can cut the file again the same way.

**Packs.** Every block a device writes to a storage is appended to a pack: `packs/<xx>/<pack id>`, the block objects one after another, then random padding (below), where the pack id is the BLAKE3 hash of the pack's bytes, padding included and `<xx>` its first two hex digits. A push collects, per storage, the blocks that storage needs until a pack's worth is together: 64 MiB on hot, local, pool and transferrer storages, 256 MiB on cold ones. Packs are written with put-if-absent; on S3 an object above 64 MiB is written as a multipart upload in 16 MiB parts (each part's ETag is the MD5 of its bytes; a server that refuses the upload gets one PUT, and a HEAD first finds a pack that is there, since the name is the hash of the bytes). The bytes a push holds are bounded by the pack sizes of the storages it writes (128 to 768 MiB); when the bound is reached, what each storage collected is written as it is, so a pack may be smaller than the target. Data moves, drains and moves to the trash storage write each group of blocks (up to 64) as one call, so as packs too; a staged move to a pool whose disk has no room for the whole group splits it in halves until the parts fit, so a disk fills to its edge. A repair collects the good copies per storage, a storage removal the copies per target, and a Strongroom conversion the re-encrypted blocks of many files, each up to a pack's worth, before writing; an import collects the blocks of many contents into packs of up to 64 MiB exactly like a push (two packs written at a time, one queued, one filling), so that a small file is no pack of its own; a content counts as imported, and its blocks are recorded in the ledger, once every pack with one of its blocks is written, and the packs collected so far are written, however small, before each checkpoint of the run (every 500 contents or 20 seconds) and at its end.

**Size classes.** A pack is not its members' size: after the last member the writer appends random bytes (the operating system's CSPRNG), and the length of a pack is exactly one size class: 256 KiB, 1, 4, 16 or 64 MiB on hot, local, pool and transferrer storages, and the same plus 256 MiB on cold ones. A pack closes when the next member would pass the largest class of its storage (a member is at most 16 MiB and 41 bytes, so it always fits), the members of a push are placed first-fit decreasing so that full packs are nearly all members, and the pack of what is left takes the smallest class its members fit in. Every pack written is padded: a push, a move, a repair, a conversion, an import, a disk fill, and the rewrite of a pack in garbage collection. Nothing addresses the padding. Members are found by `(offset, len)` from the ledger place or the pack index; the pack id is the hash of the whole padded object; `pack_len` in claims, indexes and tombstones is the padded length; verification of a whole pack (disk check, scrub) hashes the whole object; replicas copy the pack whole. Format 2 reads a pack, place, index or tombstone entry of any length up to `storage::MAX_PACK` (exactly 256 MiB): packs of older builds, which are not padded, are still read, verified and collected; only writes produce size classes. A format 3 reader (feature `format3`) refuses a length that is no class (`pack <id> has length <n>, not a size class`). The padding is bounded by the next class: a pack is never larger than the next class above its members (a 1-byte push is a 256 KiB object, 256 KiB + 1 byte is 1 MiB, 5 MiB is 16 MiB, 70 MiB on a hot storage is a full 64 MiB pack and a second one of 8 MiB, which takes the 16 MiB class). The change needs no new format version: the padding is trailing bytes nobody addresses. A device on a build before it reads padded packs but, in garbage collection, leaves a padded pack alone, and an emptied one stays until a newer device removes it. Format 3 keeps packs as the unit and the size classes; the padding carries over. Not yet done: packs are built per storage, so two storages of one push hold packs of the same members with different padding and ids (format 3 builds one pack per target set).

No block is an object of its own any more: `chunks/` is not written. A block keeps its object name, the hash of its bytes, which the ledger, verification and peers use, and every reader still checks it.

**Where a block is.** The `chunk_stored` event carries `pack: {pack, offset, len, pack_len}` beside the object name: the place the writer's storage holds the block at. In the location view (section 6) each storage location of a chunk keeps the place from the newest claim that names one (Lamport time, then the larger place, so applying batches in any order gives the same view; view format 7). Whenever the view changes, the engine hands every place in it to the pack layer it shares with every storage it opens (`packs::PackContext`), with the places of packs it wrote itself; a `chunks/<xx>/<object>` read is a ranged read of the pack at that place, on a storage that lists the pack (`packs/` is listed once per process and again at most every 30 seconds on a miss). Readers therefore need no index: the ledger they hold says where every block is. An engine hands the view over before it opens its first storage, so a fresh process (a single CLI command) finds blocks too; a place the view drops (a block whose drop was just sealed) stays known to the process, so the deletion that follows finds the block. A read that misses the listed packs tries every live place it knows with a ranged read, also in packs another thread listed meanwhile. Events recorded before their storage's pack existed get their place when they are sealed or kept for the next batch.

**Pack index.** At the same moment the packs written since are listed in `packs/index/<device>/<seq>.enc`, one record per storage and folder: `(pack, offset, length, pack length, object name, chunk id)` for every block whose claim is in the batch, as fixed-width binary entries (format byte 2, kind, count, then 32 + 8 + 8 + 8 + 32 + 32 bytes per entry), zstd-compressed, encrypted under the folder's metadata key K10 with associated data (format, `pack-index`, vault, folder, device, seq). `seq` is the next free number of the device's indexes on that storage (tombstone lists are numbered the same way). The index is for `fsck`, `ledger rebuild` (which reads every index it can open before it lists blocks, section 38), devices that join later, and members of shared folders: they do not hold the vault ledger, so before each pull of a shared folder a member reads the indexes it has not read yet that open under the folder's metadata keys and learns the places from them (an index no key opens is tried again when the folder keys change); the record envelope of the records work replaces the AEAD wrapper through `record_shim`.

**Deletion and garbage collection.** Deleting a block marks it dead in its pack and adds it to this device's tombstone list for the storage; the list is written as `packs/tombstones/<device>/<seq>.enc` (same binary entries without the chunk id, kind `tombstones`) at the end of each operation that deleted blocks, one list per folder: under the folder's metadata key K10 with associated data (format, `tombstones`, vault, folder, device, seq) for the blocks the view places in a folder whose key the device holds, so every device of the folder (members of a shared folder too) reads what the others deleted; under a key derived from the vault key of epoch 0 (`pack-tombstones`, empty scope) for the rest. Each device reads the lists it has not read when it lists a storage's packs, trying the vault key and then the metadata keys of its folders; a list no key opens is tried again once the device's folder keys change. A tombstone of another device kills a member only where no live claim of the view places a block on that storage at that pack and offset: a member of a shared folder, or a device whose ledger is behind, cannot make another device drop or rewrite away a block the ledger still holds (a hostile tombstone only makes reads of that copy go elsewhere). A device's own deletions count at once. A pack whose every byte is dead is deleted by whoever kills its last block; when that deletion fails (a read-only directory, a storage that refuses), the block stays dead and the pack is left to garbage collection. A hot pack more than half dead is rewritten by `pack_upkeep` (run at the end of the trash upkeep): its live blocks are read from one GET of the pack, checked against their names, written into a new pack and claimed there in one batch, then the old pack is deleted. A device rewrites only packs whose every live block belongs to a folder whose key it holds and whose bytes it can account for completely (live blocks and tombstones cover the pack); cold packs are never rewritten, their dead space goes with the pack.

**Verification and repair.** Automatic verification groups the blocks due by pack and reads each group with one ranged request spanning them (`packs::read_members`); a pool disk check and a scrub of a disk hash each pack against its id and count it for every block the ledger places in it. The scrub of other storages reads each block at its place. A repair of a corrupt block writes a tombstone for it and a new pack with the good copy.

**Pools and transferrers.** Pools and transferrers keep packs like any storage (section 19). A pool reads a block with a ranged read of the pack file on the disk that holds it. A pool holds blocks only: it gets no pack indexes and no tombstone lists. Its packs are known from the ledger (each claim names its pack), and a disk insert tidies away packs no claim names any more (section 34), so neither record is needed to find or remove them. A staged move (section 59) writes packs to the pool and counts a disk group rule by the places that hold the pack. A disk's tidy, check and fake-capacity measurement list `packs/` beside `chunks/`; a fill counts blocks, also for a pack it copies whole to another place.

**Read limits.** Section 60 (S7) bounds every storage read by the kind of object; format 2 keeps the bounds and changes the kinds. A block read (`chunks/<xx>/<object>`) is at most 16 MiB and 64 bytes; a pack (`packs/<xx>/<id>`) at most `storage::MAX_PACK`, the largest size class (256 MiB, cold); pack indexes and tombstone lists are records (256 MiB). A pack store reads a whole pack (garbage collection) up to its own top size class, so a 64 MiB storage never reads a 256 MiB object. A place from the ledger is used only when it fits in a pack of at most `MAX_PACK` bytes (`PackLoc::valid`), and a ranged read, of one block or of the span of several in verification, is clamped to `MAX_PACK`. Pack ids and object names are sliced without splitting a character.

**Replicas.** A replica copies packs whole, each checked against its id, and copies pack indexes and tombstone lists as they are. It claims a pack by its id (object and chunk id in an object claim, section 58); the owner's view gives every block placed in that pack on any storage a copy on the replica at the same place.

**Peers.** Peers still ask for single block objects. A device serves a block from a pack on a local-directory storage at the place the ledger gives, from the block cache, or encrypts it again from the local file, which gives the stored object because key, nonce and packing are deterministic for one zstd version (otherwise the name does not match and the peer asks elsewhere).

**What a storage learns.** Pack size classes (256 KiB, 1, 4, 16, 64 or 256 MiB; the data's size only to within the class), at most one push and folder per pack, the number and timing of pack indexes and tombstone lists, and which packs go away; not the size or number of blocks in a pack, nor where one ends. The same file pushed to two folders is cut at other places and packed into other packs. A pack index and a tombstone list are opaque.

**Limits.**

- Pack places come from the ledger: a device whose ledger lacks a claim (not pulled yet, or lost) finds the block only after the next pull or by reading the pack indexes (`ledger rebuild`). A reader with an old place for a block that garbage collection moved misses it until it pulls the claim of the new place.
- A device lists the tombstones of a block under the vault key when its view places the block in no folder whose key it holds; devices without the vault key (members of shared folders) cannot read those.
- A block deleted on one device while another device skips writing it because it is still in the pack survives a rewrite of the pack only if the rewriting device's view already holds the second device's claim; otherwise verification reports the missing copy and repair writes it again.
- The last pack of a push, move, repair, conversion or import, and a pack written because a checkpoint or the buffer bound forced it, is smaller than the target; padding hides its size inside a class. The class still tells a storage the order of magnitude of the data, and the number of full packs tells the size of a large push to within a pack. Cost: a 1-byte push stores a 256 KiB object, anything above 256 KiB less than four times its size, and a large push one padded last pack; a hot pack more than half dead counts padding as free space, so a pack is rewritten when more than half of its members' bytes are dead.
- Garbage collection and the removal of an emptied pack account for the members from offset 0; what follows the last known member must be the padding of the pack's class. A member this device does not know that lies after the last known one hides in the padding (one in the middle still shows as a gap); a pack has one writer and one push, so this takes a device whose ledger lacks the claims of that push's last blocks and no others of the pack.
- Measured on the benchmark data (`scripts/bench/gen-data.py`, release build, one local directory storage): a push of 50 000 small files (1 456 959 306 bytes) stores 22 packs of 1 476 395 008 bytes, 1.3 % over the data; a push of one 1 GiB incompressible file (blocks of 1 to 16 MiB) stores 17 packs, 1 090 519 040 bytes (16 of 64 MiB and a last one of 16 MiB), 1.6 % over the data. A push collects per storage one pack's worth plus one block, fills a pack from that buffer first fit decreasing and keeps the rest for the next one, so only the last pack is not full. Moves, repairs, drains, forwarding and conversions fill a buffer to the target first and let the writer split what passes it, so each of their batches can end in a padded tail pack.
- An import that is stopped keeps the contents already recorded; the blocks of a content found damaged after some of them were written stay in their pack, claimed by no one.
- A pool pack keeps its dead bytes until every block in it is gone: packs on removable disks are not rewritten.
- `.idx` files and `.del.` markers of format 1 are not read.

Tests: `chunk_seal::tests` (no two encodings of a chunk share a nonce over 200 chunks and six encodings each, the same bytes give the same nonce, a chosen nonce is refused), `pack::tests` (round trips of 1, 63, 64, 4095, 4096, 300 000 and 16 MiB bytes; the probe), `chunking::tests` (different seeds cut differently, the same seed alike; size tiers), `packs::tests` (blocks of those sizes through a pack, packs cut at the target, tombstones and an emptied pack, rewriting a mostly dead pack, index and tombstone bodies, one request per pack, folder-scoped tombstone lists read by another device of the folder, a hostile tombstone that cannot kill a claimed block, places outside the read limits refused), `storage::bounded_read_tests` (a pack is read up to `MAX_PACK`), `s3::tests::large_objects_are_uploaded_in_parts`, `tests/packs.rs` (the same 10 MiB file in two folders gives other pack layouts and no block objects; a mostly dead pack is rewritten and a device that joins afterwards reads the moved block), `tests/chunk_sizes.rs`, and every test that damages, loses or counts blocks through `tests/common` (packs are found through the record of packs a debug build keeps of what it wrote, `packs::debug_registry`).

## 63. Folder protection: the safety indicator of the interface

**Why.** The folder list and the navigation show, for every folder, whether its files are safe. The answer is computed by the service from the ledger view, not guessed by the page, and is part of every folder in `GET /api/status` (`FolderStatus.protection`; a key limited to some folders sees it for those only). Nothing is stored; no format changes.

**What counts.** For a folder, the unique blocks of its current files (a block used by several files counts once; a deleted file counts nothing), weighted by their plaintext size (the same bytes as the folder's `bytes`, deduplicated). A block has a copy in a *class* when the ledger shows a claim on a storage of that class (`Location.claimed_by` not empty):

- `cloud`: external storage, an S3 or rclone storage by its kind (the storage's `place` label does not change this).
- `disks`: the user's own disks, a directory storage or a disk pool. A pool disk that is away still counts: the copy exists, only its reading waits.
- `devices`: a device holds the plaintext (this device's files, judged from the local index like the data map; other devices as the ledger lists them for the block) or an untrusted replica (`replica:` storages) holds the block. A storage that is not configured on this device and is not a replica is counted here too, never as external: the safe reading.

Transferrers (carriers) keep no copy and count nowhere. A block can count in several classes.

**Fields.** `protection` has `state`, `pct`, `total_bytes`, `total_blocks`, the classes `cloud`, `disks`, `devices` (each `bytes`, `pct`, `blocks`, `verified_bytes`, `verified_pct`), `external_bytes`, `unprotected_bytes`, `unprotected_blocks`, `missing_bytes` and `policy_state`. A percentage is rounded down to one decimal and is 100 only when every byte is covered. `pct` is the share of bytes with a copy in `cloud` or `disks`. *Verified* is the part that a device other than the writer has read back (`Location::independently_verified`); the state is decided by claimed copies, so a vault with one device is not shown as unsafe for lack of a second checker, and the interface shows the verified share beside it. `unprotected_*` are the blocks with a copy in neither `cloud` nor `disks`; `missing_bytes` are those that no device holds either. `policy_state` is the folder's durability policy verdict (section 14: `ok`, `at_risk`, `violated`, `unknown`) or absent without a policy. `FolderStatus.last_change_utc` is the newest modification time of a current file.

**States.** `state` is the first that holds:

| `state` | meaning | interface colour |
|---|---|---|
| `empty` | no current file in the ledger | grey |
| `cloud` | every byte has a copy in external storage | green, "100 % in external storage" |
| `disks` | every byte has a copy on own disks, not all in the cloud | blue, "100 % on your own disks only (no cloud copy)" |
| `mixed` | every byte has an external copy, but neither class has them all | blue, dashed |
| `partial` | some bytes have an external copy | amber, "Partly protected: N %" |
| `device` | no byte has an external copy | red, "Only on devices: no external copy" |

The interface (`ui/folders.js`) uses colour-blind-safe hues (Okabe-Ito), an icon and words for each state, in both themes. The badge is shown in the navigation, in the folder overview on the Files page and on a phone's folder cards; a tooltip (and the folder header) lists the classes with their verified shares, what has no copy outside devices and the policy verdict.

**Limits.** The ledger is what this device knows: claims of devices it has not pulled yet are not counted. A folder whose file list this device never fetched is `empty`. A file published but not yet pushed to any storage is `device`.

Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/protection.rs` (unpushed, directory, transferrer, cloud via rclone, disk pool, claimed versus verified on two devices), `engine::protection::tests`, and `desktop::tests::status_carries_the_protection_of_each_folder` (the API field).

## 64. Live visualization (`/api/viz`)

**Why.** One screen, readable from across a room, that shows where the data is, what moves where right now, whether it is safe and what it costs. The page (Visualization in the navigation, `ui/viz.js`) draws an isometric board on one `<canvas>` from a single snapshot; it needs no other request.

**`GET /api/viz`.** The session token as for the other `/api` routes (`X-Varsto-Token` or `Authorization: Bearer`; 401 without), answered on a thread of its own without waiting for the state lock (section 71), `Cache-Control: no-store`. The page asks once a second while it is shown. The snapshot is joined from two parts that the engine computes only when the lock is free and the service keeps: the ledger part (devices, storages, folders, copies, policies; one pass over the ledger view and the folder states; at most every 20 s) and the small part (pool disks from the pool indexes, drains, staged moves, repair, verification and bit-rot schedules, backups; at most every 3 s). While a sync holds the engine the last parts are served with `"stale": true`; the live part (tasks of this device and of the peers, peer traffic) is always current. Nothing is read from a storage. A locked or empty vault gives `"unlocked": false` and empty lists; the page then draws demo data and says so.

All figures are bytes, bytes per second, UTC seconds or counts; `eta_utc` is `null` when it cannot be told (the page shows a dash). Names are display names. Node ids tie flows to nodes: `d:<device id>`, `s:<storage name>`, `p:<disk label>`.

| Key | Contents |
| --- | --- |
| `v`, `utc`, `unlocked`, `stale`, `data_utc`, `light_utc` | layout version (1), the service's clock (the page corrects its own by it), whether the vault is open, whether engine parts are old, when each part was computed |
| `this_device`, `vault_name`, `key_epoch`, `member`, `total_bytes` | this device's id and name, the vault key epoch (section 21), a member device of another user's vault, bytes of the current files' blocks |
| `devices[]` | `id`, `name`, `role` (this, peer, replica, member), `platform`, `online`, `busy`, `path` (direct-lan, direct, relayed via ...), `rx_bps`, `tx_bps`, `app_version`, `last_sync_utc`; removed devices are left out |
| `storages[]` | `name`, `id`, `kind` (directory, s3, rclone, pool, transferrer, replica, unknown), `place`, `configured`, `cold`, `carrier`, `data_bytes`, `verified_bytes` (checked by a device other than the writer), `used_bytes`, `quota_bytes`, `percent`, `state` (none, ok, warning, full; section 44), `price` (`per_gb_month`, `egress_per_gb`, `retrieval_per_gb`, `currency`, `source`), `monthly_cost`, `read_all_cost`, `drain` (`phase`, `percent`, `bytes_left`, `eta_utc`, `last_error`) or `null` |
| `disks` | `count`, `docked` (labels), `working_on` (the label a disk task runs on), `list[]`: `pool`, `label`, `place`, `docked`, `retired`, `capacity_bytes`, `used_bytes`, `free_bytes`, `objects`, `last_verified_utc`, `overdue_share`, `failing` (reason or `null`), `reclaimable_bytes`, `phase` (verify, fill, tidy, fsck, fetch, init, unmount, idle, wanted, away), `progress` (percent), `eta_utc`, `wanted_by` (id of a move that waits for a disk of the pool) |
| `folders[]` | `name`, `bytes`, `files`, `blocks`, `mode`, `shared`, `read_only`, `strongroom`, `paused`, `policy` (in words), `policy_state` (none, ok, at_risk, violated, unknown; section 14), `policy_reasons`, `policy_warnings`, `blocks_short_of_copies`, `blocks_unverified_in_window`, `oldest_verification_days`, `unprotected_blocks`, `verified_blocks`, `unverified_blocks`, `local_only_blocks`, `missing_blocks`, `storages` and `devices` (bytes by name or id), `targets` (the storages new blocks go to on this device) |
| `copies` | `blocks` and `bytes` by kept copies (0, 1, 2, 3 or more) and the byte-weighted `average` |
| `flows[]` | `id`, `kind` (upload, download, disk_fill, drain, move, p2p, relay), `from`, `to` (node ids), `folder`, `bps`, `unit`, `done`, `total`, `remaining`, `eta_utc`; an upload has one flow per target storage sharing the task's rate; a download comes from the storage holding most of the folder; p2p and relay come from the traffic counters (section 15) |
| `jobs[]` | the activity tasks of this device and of the peers (section 32): `id`, `kind`, `phase`, `subject`, `detail`, `device`, `device_name`, `done`, `total`, `unit`, `percent`, `rate`, `eta_utc`, `started_utc`; `eta_utc` is the task's own, or from `rate` and the remaining count |
| `moves[]`, `drains[]`, `backups[]` | open staged moves (section 59) with `waiting` (disk, room, place) and `waiting_disk`; drains (section 44); backups with the last and the next snapshot |
| `alerts[]` | `level` (bad, warn, info), `code`, `subject`, `text`: a ledger problem, forked devices, a policy at risk or violated, missing blocks, a paused folder, a full or nearly full storage, a blocked drain, a failing disk, damaged copies, a move waiting for a disk |
| `health` | `overall` (ok, warn, bad), `ledger`, `verify` and `scrub` (`enabled`, `last_run_utc`, `next_run_utc`, `last_error`), `repair` (`queued`, `losses`, `last_error`) |
| `traffic` | `rx_bps`, `tx_bps`, `relay_bps`, `active` and the last 60 seconds of each (section 15) |
| `cost` | `estimate` (always true), `month` and `read_all_once` (amount by currency, never converted), `unpriced` (storages holding data with no price), `note`; the prices are the user's or the built-in data of section 20 and exclude request fees and tax |

**The page.** Reached from the navigation (and the More page on a phone); a plain `<canvas>` with no scripts from elsewhere, the files `/viz.js`, `/viz.css` and `/viz/*.webp` are static assets of the service under the page's strict content security policy (`/viz/manifest.json` gives each sprite's floor anchor and its width in metres). The sprites are the website's Blender objects (laptop, phone, tablet, monitor, NAS, disk in four states, shelf, USB stick, cloud, bucket, archive, and the disk dock: an open-top docking bay with a status LED and a power button, alone or with a 3.5-inch drive standing in its slot in the drive's state) rendered on a transparent background by `website/tools/render/viz_sprites.py` in the light and the dark palette at 512 and 1024 px. The board has three rows (devices, home, cloud); flows are tubes with particles running from sender to receiver, their label showing folder, rate and estimated finish. The particles move at a speed that follows bytes per second but is bounded by the size of the board: a board width takes between 14 and 7 seconds, nothing is faster than 240 CSS pixels per second, and no more than two particles passing a point per second; it is computed from the time between frames, not from the canvas size, the pixel ratio or the refresh rate, and with `prefers-reduced-motion` they only creep (4 px/s). A flow's label keeps its place: the place is chosen once per flow (same two nodes, kind and folder) and kept while it is free; a label that overlaps something moves only after 3 seconds of continuous overlap; a refresh changes the text only, and the label's width grows at once but shrinks only after 10 seconds, so changing numbers do not move or breathe it; parallel flows keep their lanes (they are ordered by their key, not by the order of the data). Verification, repair and drain run as pulsing rings; a docked disk stands in the dock's slot (its own picture, light or dark) with its phase beside the dock and its progress above it; storages show their fullness, price estimate and verified share. Full screen (button or `F`; `Esc` and the button leave it, and so does Back on a phone) is built in layers so that it is really the whole screen wherever the page runs: the board always switches to a cover mode (it moves to the document body and fills the viewport, `100dvw` by `100dvh`, with all other page content and the scrollbars hidden); on top of that, inside an app shell the shell is asked to put its window into full screen (Android: `VarstoAndroid.setFullscreen` hides the system bars, the page answers the Back key itself; macOS: the `varstoShell` script message toggles the window's full-screen mode; Windows window: `window.ipc` message `varsto:fullscreen:1|0` sets borderless full screen), and in a browser the Fullscreen API is used; a shell tells the page about its own changes through `window.varstoShellFullscreen(on)`. The mouse pointer and the bar are hidden after 4 idle seconds in full screen; the canvas is sized from the element's CSS size times `devicePixelRatio` (up to 8192 px and 36 megapixels) and sized again on `resize`, a `change` of the screen, an orientation change and a change of the pixel ratio; the display's native size (`screen.width` x `devicePixelRatio`) is shown beside the canvas size. The 1024 px sprites are used when a sprite is drawn wider than 440 device pixels. Drawing is limited to about 30 frames per second while a flow or ring moves and once a second otherwise (with `prefers-reduced-motion` about 8 frames per second). The background follows the theme, or is Light, or pure black (`#000`, with the dark sprites) from a control that is kept in `localStorage` (`varsto-viz-bg`). Below 1100 px the panels stack under the picture.

**What the page shows before and without data.** The page draws only what `/api/viz` has answered; it has no example content of its own to fall back on. Until the first answer it shows a quiet "Loading..." note over an empty board (no devices, flows, costs or panels); a first answer that fails shows "No answer from the service" with a "Try again" button; a locked vault shows that it is locked; an unlocked vault without storages draws its devices and says that no storage has been added. Every time the page is opened it starts again from nothing, so an earlier session's picture is never shown as current. An answer marked `stale` (the service is busy) or a later failed request keeps the last real data but draws it dimmed with the age of the last live answer in the status line, never as live. The built-in example scene used for the screenshots is reachable only when the screenshot driver sets `window.__VARSTO_VIZ_TEST = true` before the page loads; no URL parameter, stored value or service answer enables it. Tests: the state machine in `scripts/test/viz-rules.test.js`, `viz::tests::page_has_no_demo_scene_entry_points`, and `scripts/test/viz-first-paint.py` (Playwright at 390 x 844 with a delayed and a failing first answer).

Tests: `tests/viz_snapshot.rs` (a fixture vault with a home storage, a priced cloud storage, a pool with one docked disk and a running upload and a running disk check given by hand: storages, price and cost, the docked disk's phase and progress, one flow per target with the shared rate and the finish time, determinism; the empty snapshot), `viz::tests` in the command line (the token rules, the static assets and their types, no external address or inline script in the new files, every sprite present, Finnish and Swedish for every word the page shows), and `scripts/test/viz-rules.test.js` (node; run from `viz::tests`: particle speed bounds and the label rules), and Playwright screenshots at 3840 x 2160 (pixel ratio 1 and 2) and at phone width.

## 65. The Windows window, installer and automatic updates

The interface of section 10 is one web page served by the background service on 127.0.0.1. Every platform shows that same page in a window of the platform's own kind, so the screens are alike and only the frame follows the system: the macOS app in a WebKit view (`apps/macos`), the Android app in a WebView (`apps/android`), and on Windows `varsto-window.exe` (`crates/varsto-window`) in a WebView2 view through the `wry` and `tao` crates. Linux keeps the browser (a WebKitGTK shell would need system libraries the static musl build cannot carry).

**Window.** `varsto-window [--home DIR]` reads `service.json`, starts `varsto.exe tray` (which starts the service) when no service answers, and opens `http://127.0.0.1:<port>/?boot=<code>` with a one-time code from `POST /api/session/boot`, exactly as the browser flow of section 10. When `service.json` changes (the service restarted after an update) it opens the page again with a new code. The view is in-private (the session token and the page's storage never reach the disk), allows the camera for the QR scanner and nothing else, keeps navigation on 127.0.0.1 and hands every other address to the default browser. One window per user: a named mutex, and a second launch finds the first by a window property and brings it to the front. Size, position and the maximised state are kept in `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Varsto\window.json` and dropped when their monitor is gone. Closing the window ends only the window program. The tray's "Open Varsto" starts it (`varsto-window.exe` beside `varsto.exe`), and falls back to the browser when the file is missing. Without the WebView2 runtime, or when the view cannot start, the interface opens in the default browser and a message offers Microsoft's bootstrapper. `varsto-window --background` is the login starter: it starts the tray app invisibly and exits.

**Installer.** `installer/windows/varsto.iss` (Inno Setup) builds `Varsto-<version>-setup.exe` from `varsto.exe`, `varsto-window.exe` and the icon (`make-ico.py`, from `brand/png`). The `AppId` never changes; a new setup reads the installed version from the uninstall entry, says whether it upgrades, repairs or downgrades, closes the window, the tray and the service of that installation (tray first, because it restarts the service; the service is asked to quit; what remains is ended), replaces the files, runs `varsto install` (Explorer verbs, section 10) and starts the tray again. A downgrade is a question in the wizard and refused when silent. The data folder (`%APPDATA%\Varsto` or `VARSTO_HOME`) is never touched by an upgrade, and by an uninstall only when asked (`/DELETEDATA=1` when silent). Login start is a `Run` key naming `varsto-window.exe --background`. Per-user is the default; "for all users" asks for elevation.

**Automatic updates.** A copy installed by the setup program (its `unins000.exe` sits beside the binary) takes `Varsto-<version>-setup.exe` as its update file: it is listed in the signed `SHA256SUMS` like every file and passes the same checks as an archive (section 10, section 60: signature, version named in the trusted comment, signing time not older than the last list accepted, GitHub as the second channel, SHA-256 of the download), is saved in a private folder and run detached with `/VERYSILENT /SUPPRESSMSGBOXES /NORESTART /CLOSEAPPLICATIONS`; the setup program restarts Varsto itself. Windows has no other package: there is no portable zip, and a copy the setup program did not install (an old portable alpha.14) does not update itself; `varsto update` and `varsto update --daily` tell it to download and run the installer once, after which updates continue automatically. The service checks once a day (the first check two minutes after it starts; `auto-update-checked` in the device directory); where the setup program installed it, it installs by itself, elsewhere it shows a notification once per version. The setting `auto-update` (file in the device directory; `varsto config auto-update on|off`, `GET/POST /api/update/settings`, the Service card of the settings page) turns the daily check off. Nothing is ever installed without the signature chain.

## 66. What formatting a drive means: GPT, one aligned partition, one filesystem choice

Every place that formats a drive for Varsto follows one layout, defined in `varsto_core::diskformat` and nowhere else.

**Layout.** A GPT partition table (never MBR/msdos) with its protective MBR, so that old tools see a fully used drive and leave it alone; exactly one partition that starts at 1 MiB (`sgdisk -n 1:1M:0`; `sgdisk` rounds the start up to its own alignment, so 512e, 4Kn, SSD and RAID drives are all aligned) and runs to the last usable sector; the GPT partition type that fits the filesystem (Linux filesystem data `0fc63daf-8483-4772-8e79-3d69d8477de4`, `8300`, for ext4; Microsoft basic data `ebd0a0a2-b9e5-4433-87c0-68b6b72699c7`, `0700`, for exFAT and NTFS; Apple APFS `7c3457ef-0000-11aa-aa11-00306543ecac`, `AF0A`, for APFS); a random PARTUUID chosen beforehand (it is how `disks.conf` finds the disk again, section 34); the partition name `varsto-<disk name>` (36 characters at most); and a volume label reduced to ASCII letters, digits, `-` and `_` (16 bytes for ext4, 11 for exFAT, 32 for NTFS), which is also what makes the label safe inside a shell or PowerShell string. FAT32 is never made.

**Filesystem choice.** ext4 stays the default on Linux and is the only filesystem `varsto disk init` makes: a pool keeps one file per encrypted block, needs POSIX permissions and rename, is checked with `e2fsck` before every mount (section 34) and was always ext4, so nothing changes for existing disks. The other choices are defined for the platforms that cannot make ext4: exFAT is the cross-platform filesystem for a drive that moves between Linux, macOS and Windows (no 4 GiB file limit, no extra driver on macOS or Windows), APFS is for a drive only a Mac reads, NTFS for one only Windows reads. `Fs::default_for(os)` is ext4 on Linux and exFAT on macOS and Windows; `Fs::supported_on(os)` says what each system can make.

**Commands.** Pure functions turn a device, a name and a filesystem into command lines and run nothing:
- Linux (`linux_commands`, run by `blockdev::INIT_SCRIPT` in one privileged call): `wipefs -a -q` on every partition and on the drive, `sgdisk -Z`, `sgdisk -o`, `sgdisk -n 1:1M:0 -t 1:8300 -u 1:<uuid> -c 1:varsto-<name>`, `partprobe`, `mkfs.ext4 ...`. A unit test substitutes the script's arguments into its `sgdisk` lines and compares them with the pure list.
- macOS (`macos_commands`, defined, not yet run because `disk init` is still Linux only): `diskutil unmountDisk /dev/diskN`, then `diskutil eraseDisk ExFAT|APFS <name> GPT /dev/diskN`. `eraseDisk` with `GPT` writes the table with its protective MBR and aligns the partitions itself; it also adds the small EFI system partition every Mac GPT disk has.
- Windows (`windows_commands`, defined, not yet run): `Clear-Disk -Number N -RemoveData -RemoveOEM`, `Initialize-Disk -Number N -PartitionStyle GPT`, `New-Partition -DiskNumber N -UseMaximumSize -Alignment 1048576 -GptType '{ebd0a0a2-...}' | Format-Volume -FileSystem exFAT|NTFS -NewFileSystemLabel '<label>'`.

**Refusals.** Only whole drives are formatted, and only the one the user named and confirmed. Linux: the path must be a block device `lsblk` lists as a whole disk (not a partition, not a mapping), with nothing mounted or used as swap on it, no volume manager, RAID or encryption signature, and none of `/`, `/boot`, `/home`, Varsto's directory or the program on it (section 34); the path must be under `/dev` with no `..` (`prepare_drive` refuses `/tmp/disk.img` before running anything), and the existing confirmation (typing the device path, or `--confirm`) still applies. macOS: only `/dev/diskN` with N above 0, never a slice (`disk4s2`) or raw node. Windows: only a decimal disk number above 0. A filesystem the system cannot make is an error, never a silent substitute.

**Existing disks.** Nothing changes for them: a drive is recognised by the PARTUUID in `disks.conf` and must be ext4 (section 34), whatever table it has. Earlier `disk init` runs already made a GPT with a partition at sector 2048 (1 MiB), type 8300 and the same name, so those drives match the layout above. A disk that was made by hand with an MBR table keeps working as long as it is ext4 and listed in `disks.conf`; Varsto never converts a table in place. To move such a disk to GPT, first make sure the pool holds its blocks elsewhere (or copy the pool files off the drive), then run `varsto disk init` on the drive under a label that is free: that erases the drive, makes the layout above and fills the new disk like any other; the old label is retired by hand.

Tests: `diskformat::tests` (the exact Linux, macOS and Windows command lists, GPT and no legacy tools in any of them, 1 MiB start and alignment, partition types per filesystem, defaults and support per system, refusals of partitions, raw nodes, disk 0, `..` paths, injection through names, unsupported filesystems; an end-to-end run of the plan's `sgdisk` commands on a 64 MiB image file, skipped where `sgdisk` is missing, that checks the GPT header, the protective MBR (type `0xEE`, signature `55AA`, a pre-existing fake MBR gone), first sector 2048, type GUID, PARTUUID and name), `blockdev::tests::init_script_matches_the_pure_command_list`, `prepare_refuses_a_path_that_is_not_a_device_before_running_anything` and the unchanged drive-check tests. No test touches a block device.

## 67. The daily update channel (`varsto update --daily`)

Daily builds (development builds of the main line, published at `https://<site>/daily/`) can be installed by `varsto update --daily` (and checked by `varsto update --check --daily`). The channel is separate from releases (sections 10, 60 and 65) in its key, its files, its state and its trust.

**Key and files.** The channel has its own minisign-compatible Ed25519 key (`daily-key.pub`, built in beside `release-key.pub`; its private half is kept away from CI and the web server).
