The updater trusts the daily key only for the daily list and the release key only for releases: `verify_signature` takes the key it is given and the key id in the signature must be that key's, so a signature of one channel never validates the other, and the daily check also requires `channel:daily` in the signed comment. The files, below `daily/latest/`, are `SHA256SUMS` (stable names `varsto-daily-linux-x86_64.tar.gz`, `varsto-daily-windows-setup.exe`, `varsto-daily-windows-x86_64.zip`, `varsto-daily-macos-apple-silicon.dmg`, `varsto-daily-android-debug.apk`, `varsto-daily-ios-unsigned.ipa`) and `SHA256SUMS.sig`. The signed trusted comment is `timestamp:<unix seconds>\tfile:SHA256SUMS\tchannel:daily\tbuild:<YYYYMMDD-commit>`. `latest.json` and the page are unsigned and are not read by the updater.

**Checks, in this order.** Signature; `channel`, `file` and `build` in the comment (the build id must be `YYYYMMDD-` and 7 to 40 lowercase hex digits); signing time not older than the newest accepted so far (`update-state-daily.json` in the device directory, written like `update-state.json` of section 60 and only after the download has been verified), not more than one day ahead of the clock, and not more than 30 days old (a frozen site or a replay is refused, not just reported); the build not older than the installed daily build by the date in its id (a different build of the same day is newer only when its list was signed later; the same build is "up to date"); the file for this platform listed in the signed list; the SHA-256 of the download equal to the signed value. The package version is not used: daily builds of one version share it. There is no second channel for daily lists, so `--allow-single-source` has no effect on them.

**Installing.** The file is installed like a release file of the same kind (section 10; section 65 for Windows): a `.tar.gz` replaces the binary by a rename from a staging file beside it, the setup program runs silently (the only Windows file), and the service is restarted. The macOS disk image, the Android APK and the iOS package are named but not installed by the command line, which says so and points at the daily page. After a successful install `update-channel.json` (`channel`, `build`, `signed_at`, `version`) records the build; `varsto --version` and `varsto status` then name `daily build <id>`. A release installed later removes the record. The background updater and the interface never take the daily channel; they stay on releases.

**Switching.** `--daily` and `--stable` exclude each other. `--stable` (the default) goes back to releases only when the newest release is newer than the running binary, since an update never installs an older or equal version (`MIN_ACCEPTED_VERSION`, section 60); otherwise the command says which release to download by hand and verify with `varsto verify-release`.

## 68. Pairing: which address to offer, and how joining falls back

Code: `crates/varsto-core/src/netif.rs` (interfaces, ranking), `crates/varsto-core/src/pair.rs` (`join`, `Connector`, `NotFound`, discovery), `crates/varsto-cli` (`pair join`, `POST /api/pair/join`, `GET /api/pair/join-status`, the pairing form). Pairing itself (SPAKE2 on the nine digits, section 9 and 51) is unchanged; no stored format changes.

**Why.** An offer used to list one address: the one the OS would use towards a public host. With a VPN on the default route (or any tunnel the OS prefers) that is the tunnel address, which a phone on the shared Wi-Fi cannot reach, and the same single interface was used for the multicast and broadcast discovery queries, so "find the device by code" failed too.

**Candidates.** The offering device lists every IPv4 interface address (`getifaddrs` on Linux, macOS, iOS and Android; elsewhere only the old default-route address) and ranks them (`netif::rank`). Skipped: loopback, interfaces that are not up and running, `0.0.0.0`, `169.254.0.0/16`, multicast and broadcast addresses, and interfaces a peer cannot reach by name prefix (`docker`, `br-`, `veth`, `virbr`, `vmnet`, `vboxnet`, `awdl`, `llw`, `bridge`, `lo`, container and VM bridges). The same address is listed once. Order: private addresses (`Lan`), then public ones, then overlay ones (tunnel interfaces `utun`, `tun`, `tap`, `wg`, `tailscale`, `ppp`, … and 100.64.0.0/10), each group in the OS order; at most 8. IPv6 is not offered (the listener is IPv4). The offer's `addresses` and the `a=` parameter of the pairing link (section 51) carry all of them as `host:port`, **best first**, so the link format needs no new field: old readers already accept up to 16 addresses and take the first as before. The interface shows the first in the address field and in the text under the code.

**Joining.** Order of attempts (`pair::join`): (1) the typed address, (2) the addresses of the scanned link, those on a subnet this device is on first (`netif::sort_by_reach`), (3) a search of the local network by the code (multicast and the subnet's directed broadcast, sent from every usable interface, not only the default route), whose answers are tried the same way. An address is tried once. Each connection attempt has a 3 s timeout, the search runs 8 s. The code's digits are the SPAKE2 password: an offer that answers and rejects the code counts as one of its three attempts, so a wrong code (`WrongCode`) ends joining at once instead of being retried on other addresses. The address field stays optional and editable; a typed address that is not an address is reported and skipped.

**Progress and errors.** The service publishes the current step while `POST /api/pair/join` runs (`GET /api/pair/join-status` → `{active, step: trying|looking, address, tried}`); the form shows "Trying 192.168.1.20:41234 …" and "Looking for the device on the network …" and logs them, and `pair join` prints the same lines to stderr. When nothing worked the error is `NotFound`, and the response carries `tried: [{what, why}]` (`why`: `no answer`, `refused`, `unreachable`, `connection closed`, `not an address`) and `found` (offers the search found): the page names every address with its reason, whether the network search found anything, and what to check (same Wi-Fi, guest network or client isolation, VPN or firewall).

**Not done.** There is no rendezvous or relay path for pairing: before pairing the new device knows nothing of the vault's storages, and no server is involved, so the code's LAN search is the only route without an address. Pairing across networks still needs a reachable typed address.

Tests: `netif::tests` (ranking tables for a MacBook with VPN, VM bridge and link-local addresses, a Linux host with Docker, down interfaces, duplicates and Tailscale, an Android phone; subnet ordering; directed broadcast), `pair::tests` (order of attempts with an injected connector: typed, link, search; no repeats; a wrong code stops at once; the final error names what was tried; a refused real port falls through to the next address), `qr::tests` (a pairing link with ranked candidates round trips in order and fits one QR code at the maximum).

## 70. Forwarding: devices that carry other devices' blocks to the storages

**Why.** A phone sends its camera folder to the vault, but only the laptop has the credentials of the S3 bucket the folder's policy wants a copy on (or the phone's own upload is blocked by a quota, a slow link or a storage the phone cannot reach). Until now a device wrote a block to its storages only when it held the file: a laptop that attached the folder with selective sync (placeholders) never wrote the phone's blocks anywhere, so the policy stayed violated for as long as the files were only on the phone. Forwarding lets any device that is attached to the folder and holds its key carry those blocks from a peer to the storages it can write, without keeping the files.

**Attaching with a choice.** The interface's "Attach <folder> on this device?" (and on phones "Add <folder> to this phone?") asks how to keep the folder: *Full sync* (every file is downloaded and kept) or *Selective sync* (files are placeholders until opened or pinned); desktops also ask the direction: *Both ways*, *Send only* or *Receive only* (section 47). `POST /api/folder/attach` takes `selective` (as before) and `mode` (`normal`, `send-only`, `receive-only`; absent: `normal`; receive-only is refused for a folder kept encrypted on the device, as `varsto folder mode` refuses it), and `varsto folder attach <folder> <path> [--selective] [--mode send-only|receive-only]` does the same. Forwarding works in every one of these: it does not depend on whether the device keeps files.

**Who forwards.** A device forwards a folder when all of these hold:

- the folder is attached here (a mount and a folder state exist) and this device holds its key now: a locked Strongroom is skipped (`no_key` in the report) and so is a folder this device has no record of; a member of a shared folder forwards nothing (it is not a peer of the vault, section 15);
- forwarding is on (`config.json` `forward.enabled`, default true; `varsto forward set --on|--off`, `POST /api/forward {enabled}`);
- the vault is unlocked and the service is not paused (the service runs it after a sync).

**What it forwards.** For every block of every current file of the folder (each block once), the device works out from the ledger the storages a push from this device would write it to and that do not hold it yet: the folder's write targets (`WriteTargets::wants`: the placement, or the default fewest storages that keep the policy, section 23; storages being drained take nothing, a block moved away from a storage is not put back), transferrers and cold storages left out (transferrers carry for devices, not for the policy), within the vault-wide quotas (section 44). A block is forwarded only when that list is not empty *and* the block's copies do not keep the folder's copy rules yet (`min_copies`, `min_per_place`, counted from the ledger as the policy check counts them, section 14; the verification window plays no part), or, in a folder without a policy, when it has no storage copy at all. So a block that the phone already put on storages that keep the policy is not written again, and the push of a device that holds the file stays the usual path: forwarding takes what is left.

**Where the bytes come from.** In this order, the first that has them: this device's block cache; a peer (section 15), usually the device that wrote the file, since peer-to-peer costs nothing per gigabyte (peers that lacked 32 blocks in a row are not asked again in the same run); another hot, non-transferrer storage of this device that the ledger says holds the block (a copy from one storage to another, which a storage may bill as egress). Cold storages are never read, and are not written by forwarding either (a push from a device with the file, or a data move, puts copies there). The quota room for a block is taken before it is fetched, so a block that no target can take is not asked of anyone. Every object is checked before it is written: the hash of its bytes must be its name, and it must open under the folder's key as the block of the file entry (`chunk_seal::open_chunk` with the chunk id, epoch and size of the entry), so neither a peer nor a storage can hand in another object, and a device without the folder key cannot forward at all.

**Writing and recording.** Blocks are collected in memory per target storage and written as packs (section 62) when the buffer (`forward.buffer_mib`, default 64 MiB) is full and at the end of the run; each written block is read back and its hash checked, then recorded as `chunk_stored` for that storage by this device. Every group is sealed into a ledger batch and pushed before the next group is read, so a run that stops halfway keeps what it wrote and the next run continues with what is still missing (the ledger says what is done). There is no new event type. The forwarding device is the writer of the copy, so its own read-back is not recorded as a verification, and the copy counts as verified only when another device verifies it (sections 6 and 20). No `chunk_on_device` is recorded either: the forwarding device does not keep the plaintext. Nothing is written to disk on the forwarding device apart from its ledger and state files: the buffer is memory and is dropped after every group, so a selective folder stays as it was (placeholders) and the block cache does not grow.

**Two forwarders: leases.** Two devices that could forward the same blocks (two laptops with the same bucket, or with two buckets of the same place) would write them twice, in different packs. Before forwarding a folder, a device takes a *lease* on every hot, non-transferrer storage of this device that holds records: `vault/forward-leases/<folder id>/<slot, 12 digits>.json`, JSON `{"device": "<id>", "taken_utc": <utc>}`, written with put-if-absent, where the slot is the UTC time divided by 600 seconds. A lease of another device in the current or the previous slot on any of those storages means that device is forwarding the folder: this device leaves the folder for this run (`busy`, with the holder's name) and deletes the leases it took in this attempt, so that the holder (or whoever comes first in a later run) can take them all. A device that holds the leases forwards for at most 480 seconds from taking them (it never works past the time the others honour the lease), deletes its leases when the folder has nothing left to forward, and keeps them when it stopped for its budget or the time limit, so that another device does not start on the same blocks meanwhile; they lapse by themselves after at most two slots. Stale leases (older than the previous slot) are deleted by whoever sees them. A lease that does not parse counts as another device's. Leases are not encrypted: they reveal the folder id (as `thumbs/<folder>/` already does), a device id and a time; anyone who can write the storage can delay forwarding with them, which is less than such a writer can do anyway. On S3 without conditional writes (section 12) two devices can both believe they took a lease; the result is a block written twice in two packs, which costs space, not correctness. Once a forwarder's batch is pushed, every other device sees the claims and has nothing left to forward.

**Limits per run.** The service runs forwarding after every sync, after repair: at most `forward.max_mib` (default 1024 MiB, 256 MiB on phones) and 4096 blocks per run, the first block always taken. The device that serves the blocks keeps its own limits: a phone answers peers only while its service runs, its camera upload keeps its Wi-Fi-only and charging-only conditions for its own uploads, and the forwarder asks it for one object at a time, not with the download window of a pull. Metered networks are not detected by the core: a phone that should not forward over mobile data turns forwarding off.

**Report and interface.** `Engine::forward(&ForwardOptions)` returns a `ForwardReport`: per folder the blocks and bytes forwarded, per target storage and per source (`block cache`, `storage <name>`, `peer <name>`), the devices whose blocks were carried (holders in the ledger), blocks waiting because no source had them (`unavailable`) or no target could take them (`waiting_for_target`), objects refused by the check (`rejected`), folders skipped without a key (`no_key`) or held by another device's lease (`busy`), storages that could not be written, and whether work is left. The last run is kept in `state/forward.json`; `GET /api/service` carries `forward` and a one-line `forward_text` ("forwarding: last run …: 12 blocks (48.0 MiB) of camera for phone"), `varsto forward` prints the status, `varsto forward run` runs once now, and `GET /api/forward` and `POST /api/forward {enabled}` back them. While it runs, the activity list (section 32) shows a `forward` task, "Forwarding blocks to the storages for «phone: camera»", with bytes done and total; the visualization lists it like the other tasks.

**Failure cases.** No peer online and no other copy: the block is counted `unavailable` and tried again on the next run. A target storage that fails a write: it is left out for the rest of the run (`storages_failed`), the blocks stay missing and the next run tries again; a block whose read-back does not match is not claimed. The vault locked, the service paused, the ledger not writable or forked: nothing runs. A peer that disappears halfway: what was written is recorded, the rest is `unavailable`. The device holding the lease crashes: its leases lapse within two slots (at most 20 minutes) and another device forwards.

**Transfers.** Inside a run every block is a *transfer* (`engine/forward.rs`): the block (chunk id, object name, epoch, size), its targets (storages of this device), its source once found (`block cache`, a peer, or a storage of this device) and a state: *waiting for source* (no source has it now), *waiting for target* (no target can take it now: quota, a write that failed), *held* (read and checked; held in memory by forwarding) and *recorded* (written, read back, claimed). Only *recorded* is a copy anywhere: a held block counts for no policy. Copying is by ciphertext object, named by its hash, so the model does not depend on the kind of storage at either end. The report counts `waiting_for_target` beside `unavailable`.

**Mailbox for offline disks (future).** Not built yet; this is how it builds on transfers. Case: a block is only on pool disk A, which is away most of the time (or on A and a place the policy does not have yet), and the policy wants it also on disk B, another place or an online storage. The device with the dock is the *mailbox*:

- *Tasks.* The same transfers with any storage as source or target, a pool disk that is only sometimes attached included, and two more states: *waiting for source* now also means "disk A is not docked", *waiting for target* "disk B is not docked", and *held* gains a second place, `outbox`: a durable staging directory on the mailbox device (`<device dir>/outbox/<xx>/<object>`, the ciphertext object exactly as stored, written through a temporary file and a rename, checked against its name when written and again before use). Tasks are per-device state (`state/mailbox.json`: object, folder, chunk, source and target storage identities, state, held where, times), not ledger events: they are this device's plan, and the ledger records only their result.
- *Keyless.* A mailbox task needs no folder key: it moves ciphertext objects and checks them by hash, so a device can carry blocks of folders it cannot open (a Strongroom while locked). Forwarding checks under the folder key in addition because it decides from the folder's file list, which needs the key; a mailbox decides from the ledger's claims and the policy's place counts, which the vault ledger gives every full device.
- *Order of a move.* While A is docked: read the blocks that some target needs and that no attached or online storage can supply, into the outbox, up to its limit. When a target is reachable (B docked here, or an online storage; B docked on another device takes the objects from that device's outbox over peer-to-peer like any peer object): write, read back, record `chunk_stored` for the target. Only after the target copy is recorded and verified (read back here, and independently by another device's verification when the policy asks for it, section 20) may a source copy be dropped, and only when the move asked for that (`chunk_dropped`, section 23); then the staged object is deleted from the outbox.
- *Staging limits.* The outbox has a size limit (`mailbox.max_mib`, a share of the free space that keeps section 53's minimum free) and an age limit; a full outbox stops reading new blocks, never drops a held one before its target copy is recorded, and an abandoned task (the target removed or drained) frees its object.
- *Policies.* Staged blocks never count as durable copies. They count as *in transit*: the policy check reports "N blocks in transit to B" instead of a plain violation for them, and the verification grace period of automatic verification (package E) treats a block in transit as covered until its task finishes or expires.
- *Interface.* Per task group: "2.1 GiB from disk A waiting for disk B (held on this computer)", "waiting for disk A to be docked"; the activity line while copying; the outbox size and limit in the device's settings.
- *Next disk to dock.* The suggestion of sections 40 and 59 ranks a disk higher when tasks wait on it (as source or target), with the reason ("12 GiB are waiting to be copied from it to disk B"), and a staged move to a pool (section 59) can use mailbox tasks for the blocks whose only copy is on a disk that is away.

Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/forwarding.rs` (a phone whose only storage is full and a laptop with selective sync and an S3-like directory: the phone's blocks reach the bucket through the laptop from the phone as a peer, the laptop keeps placeholders and caches nothing, the policy that needs a cloud copy goes from violated to ok, the claims are the laptop's and are not counted as verified by it; a locked Strongroom is not forwarded until it is unlocked; two laptops with the same bucket: the second leaves a folder the first holds a lease on, a budget stops a run halfway and the next run continues, and every block is on the bucket exactly once; forwarding turned off), and `desktop` tests for the attach request with `selective` and `mode`.

## 71. A responsive interface: reads during long operations

**Why.** The service keeps its state (the engine and the service's own summary) behind one lock. A sync, a verification, a scrub or a ledger merge holds it for seconds to minutes on a big vault, and every request of the interface used to wait for it in one queue: on a vault of 50 000 files the summary (`/api/status`) took 30 to 70 s to answer during a sync, the browser's few connections to the service filled with waiting polls, and the page froze until the sync ended.

**Reads.** A `GET` with the session token in a header (`X-Varsto-Token` or `Authorization: Bearer`) of a route that only reads (the list `readcache::ROUTES`: `/api/state`, `/api/service`, `/api/status`, `/api/folders`, `/api/files`, the policy, verification, scrub, repair, ledger, trash, history, storage, disk, backup, organization and folder-settings reads) is answered on a thread of its own, at most 32 at a time (beyond that it joins the queue of the other requests). It waits at most 50 ms for the state. When it gets the state it answers fresh and the service keeps the answer, by address (path and query), at most 128 addresses and 64 MiB (an answer over 32 MiB is not kept). When the state stays busy it answers with the last kept answer of the same address, with the headers `X-Varsto-Stale: 1` and `X-Varsto-Age-Ms: <age>`; a JSON object also gets `"stale": true` and `"stale_ms"`. With no kept answer it waits for the state as before. Every request that is not a `GET` empties the kept answers when it arrives and again when it has been answered, so a page never gets an answer from before its own change; the requests that only run something (`/api/sync`, `/api/verify/run`, `/api/scrub/run`, `/api/fsck`) keep them, as the service's own syncs and checks do: while the state is busy the answers from before are sent marked stale, and the next fresh answer replaces them. A request with an MCP key (section 37) is not answered from them. `/api/viz` (section 64) is computed on a thread of its own as well, so the page's assets, the event stream and the activity routes are never held up by it.

**The service's own work.** After a sync the service runs its upkeep (ledger, verification, scrub, repair, drains, backups, trash, restic checks, staged moves, disk suggestions, device details) one step at a time and lets go of the state between the steps, so that a waiting request gets it. A sync itself still holds the state from start to end (the remaining cause, listed in the change that introduced this section).

**The page.** A background read (one that no pressed button started) is asked once at a time per address: a poll that comes while the same read is on its way shares its answer. The summary is drawn once at a time (asked again meanwhile, it runs once more afterwards), and an answer equal to the one drawn, or a stale one, changes nothing on screen when the page asked by itself (after a sync or an event). The file list draws 300 rows at a time and more as the end of the list comes into view (or with "Show more files"), keeps the rows already drawn when it is drawn again, is not drawn again for an equal answer, and filters the rows it has without asking again. While answers are stale a line under the title says that the service is busy; while the service cannot be reached it says that the page reconnects; the page is never reloaded for either. The visualization (section 64) stops asking while the page is hidden and asks at once when it shows again.

**Measuring.** `scripts/bench/ui-latency.py <varsto> <work dir> [--files 50000] [--browser 60]` creates a vault of that many files and four folders under a throw-away home, times every read the interface makes idle and while a sync and a verification run, and optionally counts long tasks, requests per minute and the JS heap of the page in Chromium.

Tests: `readcache::tests` (kept by address until a change, bounded, an answer computed before a change is not kept, a held state answered from the cache after the short wait and waited for without one, stale marking, the route list) and `desktop::tests::reads_are_answered_while_a_long_operation_holds_the_state` and `a_change_through_the_api_drops_the_cached_reads` (a real server whose state a test thread holds).

## 72. Remembering the passphrase without a desktop session

**Why.** The background service starts locked unless it finds the passphrase. `varsto service remember` used to store it only in the desktop keyring (Secret Service), so over SSH, on a server or in a session without a D-Bus session bus it refused. No stored format changes: the passphrase is never part of the vault.

**Stores, in this order.** `remember` uses the first that works and says which one:

1. *Desktop keyring.* Linux: Secret Service through `secret-tool` (skipped without a desktop session: no display and either an SSH login or no session bus; skipped when the keyring is locked and no display could show the unlock window). macOS: the login keychain through `security -i` (the passphrase on standard input); over SSH a failure adds the advice to unlock the keychain or use `varsto unlock`.
2. *systemd-creds* (Linux, systemd 256 or newer, user manager running). `systemd-creds encrypt --user --name=varsto-passphrase - -` gets the passphrase on standard input and writes the credential to standard output, which is stored as `passphrase.cred` in the device directory with mode 600 (atomic write). With a TPM2 the key is bound to it, otherwise to the host key. The service reads the file with `systemd-creds decrypt --user --name=varsto-passphrase - -` (file on standard input, passphrase on standard output), so it works without a desktop session and after a restart. The credential name is bound into the file. A file that cannot be decrypted (another computer, TPM cleared) is logged and ignored: the service stays locked.
3. *Kernel keyring* (Linux). A `user` key `varsto:<device directory>` in the user keyring (`@u`) through `add_key` and `keyctl`, readable by its owner only (`KEY_POS_ALL | KEY_USR_ALL`) so the service, another process of the same user, finds it. It lives in kernel memory only: gone when the computer restarts or the user has no process left. The message says so and points to `varsto unlock`.

Windows and Android do not use `remember` (unchanged). The passphrase is never written in plain text to a file, never put in the environment or an environment file, never in a process argument and never printed. Note that without a TPM2, systemd-creds binds the file to the host key, which protects against copying the file elsewhere but not against root on the same computer.

**Reading.** At service start the stores are read cheapest first: `passphrase.cred`, the kernel keyring, then the desktop keyring (whose lookup may wait up to 20 seconds on a locked keyring). `VARSTO_PASSPHRASE` still wins over all of them. After a successful `remember` the copies in the other stores are removed, so one store holds the truth and an old passphrase never shadows a new one.

**Forgetting and reporting.** `varsto service forget` removes the passphrase from every store (the credential file, the kernel key, the keyring entry, the keychain item) and reports which ones held it; the running service stays unlocked until it restarts. `varsto service status` shows `passphrase: remembered in the desktop keyring`, `... encrypted with systemd-creds`, `... in the kernel keyring (until this computer restarts)` or `not remembered`; its JSON has `passphrase_store` (`keyring`, `systemd-creds`, `kernel-keyring`, `keychain` or null) and `passphrase_text`.

**When nothing works** the error lists each store tried and why it failed, then names the alternatives: `VARSTO_PASSPHRASE` for the service, or `varsto unlock`. The passphrase is read as before (the `VARSTO_PASSPHRASE` variable, else a terminal prompt) and checked against the vault before it is stored.

Tests: `remember::tests` (injected backends: a failing keyring falls to systemd-creds, then to the kernel keyring; the failure text names everything tried; a new store clears older copies; forget removes whichever store was used and is idempotent; the status and message texts; an end-to-end round trip with the real `systemd-creds` (mode 600, no plain text in the file, replace, forget) and with the real kernel keyring, each skipped where the tool or syscall is unavailable) and `crates/varsto-cli/tests/remember_cli.rs` (the command line with a throw-away home: `service remember`, `service status`, `service forget`).

## 73. A device that joined shows at once; a device is never its own peer

**Why.** Two reports from daily use. After pairing, the new device showed on the first one only minutes later; and a phone's traffic view showed peer-to-peer traffic from the phone to itself.

**Finding the new device.** A device learns of a joined device from three things, and each was refreshed only by the periodic sync (every 300 s by default): its device record under `vault/devices/`, its rendezvous record under `vault/peers/` (published only when its first sync ended) and the LAN beacon (heard at once, used at the next sync). The `/p2p/changed` message from the joiner covered the device list when the joiner reached the first device over peer-to-peer, once, after its own first sync; it never covered the peer table or the paths. Measured on two throw-away homes with a local-directory storage and both services on loopback (debug build, the first device started 20 s earlier, the joiner's service started after `pair join` / `join`; seconds from the joiner's service start): the first device listed the joiner after 3 s (through the message), but showed it as a peer only after 303 s, and the joiner showed the first device as a working path after 303 s.

**What the service does now.**

- *Device watch.* While the vault is unlocked the service lists the device records (`Engine::refresh_devices`: one pass over the registry, no ledger) 4 s after it starts or the vault is unlocked, after a pairing offer of this device was taken up, and after a peer announced a change (`/p2p/changed`); the gap doubles after every look up to 60 s and starts over whenever something may have added a device. A new device found this way makes the service sync at once (the ledger holds its enrolment) and publish the event `devices.changed`.
- *Peer table.* The same look compares the rendezvous records on the storages with the ones the peer table was built from, and a LAN device nobody had heard before (the beacon thread sets a flag) rebuilds the table at once instead of at the next sync. The new table is asked once on a thread of its own (`/p2p/info`), the paths it found replace the displayed ones, and `peers.changed` is published, so the first path to a new device is known within seconds.
- *Announcing.* Before its first sync after a start, a device publishes its rendezvous record, builds its serving snapshot and sends `/p2p/changed`, instead of after the sync: a device that joined a big vault no longer stays invisible while it downloads.
- *Cached reads.* The kept answers of section 71 are emptied (`readcache::invalidate_all`, a process-wide counter every cache follows) when the watch finds a device or the table changes, so a poll that finds the state busy during the sync that follows is not given a device list from before.
- *Interface.* `devices.changed` and `peers.changed` make the page load the service summary and status once and, on the Peers page, the peer-to-peer card.

Measured the same way after the change: the first device listed the joiner and showed it as a working path after 2.7 s and the joiner showed the first device as a working path after 2.5 s (vault-key join and pairing alike; with a pairing the first device already listed the joiner when the joiner's service started, 8 s after the join).

**A device is never its own peer.** The traffic view showed the device's own entry because a request carrying its own device id reached its own server: another record (an old id of the same install that still carries its certificate, or a record whose address is one of the device's own) led the peer table to an address that was this device. The server counted the answer to the asker, which was itself. Now:

- The server refuses a request whose token carries its own device id: `403` with the body `self`, no traffic counted (over TCP and QUIC alike).
- The client that gets that answer from an address remembers the address as its own (`Peers::is_self_addr`) and does not try it again; the table is rebuilt every sync and carries the knowledge over.
- A record that carries this device's own certificate is dropped when the peer table is built; a QUIC connection to a pinned certificate equal to the own one is refused before the handshake, and a QUIC client presenting the own certificate is closed.
- The traffic counters ignore this device as a peer and a relayed flow from a device to itself (`Traffic::record`, `begin`, `record_relay`, the report), and the visualization (section 64) drops any peer-to-peer or relay flow whose ends are the same device.

Tests: `crates/varsto-core/tests/device_discovery.rs` (a joined device is found by one look without a sync, found once, three devices), `tests/nat.rs::a_device_is_never_its_own_peer` (a record with the own id or certificate gives no peer, a handshake with the own certificate is refused, an address that answers `self` is learned and not asked again, nothing is counted), `p2p::tests::a_request_from_this_very_device_is_refused_and_never_counted`, `p2p::traffic::tests::nothing_is_counted_for_this_device_itself`, `tests/viz_snapshot.rs::flows_of_a_device_with_itself_are_filtered`, `readcache::tests::work_of_the_service_itself_empties_the_answers`.

## 74. Ledger heads between peers

**Why.** Section 58 (Limits) said a storage that withholds a ledger batch from every reader, or rolls the whole ledger back to an older state, is not caught: nothing signed is compared with another source, because devices learned of each other's progress only from the `seen` acknowledgements inside batches that the same storage serves. Devices that can talk to each other directly have a second source.

**No format change.** `FORMAT_VERSION` stays 2. Batches, checkpoints and the storage layout are unchanged. What is new is a peer-to-peer message, one optional field in the rendezvous record and a local file; older versions ignore all three (the unknown path answers `404`, the field is skipped, the file is never read).

**The statement.** A head statement (`ledger_heads::HeadStatement`) is signed with the sender's device key (the hybrid signature of section 11) and holds: the vault id, the sender, its Lamport clock and wall clock when it signed, and a list of at most 512 entries `{device, seq, hash, checkpoint}`: for every device of the vault whose ledger the sender holds, the end of its unbroken chain (`seq`), the hash of that batch and the newest checkpoint held (`checkpoint`, 0 for none). What the sender signs about *its own* ledger is limited to what a storage is known to hold (`pushed.json`): a batch not pushed yet is not one a storage withheld. A device that signs no ledger batches (a member of shared folders, a removed device) sends none. The size is about 5 KB plus 130 bytes per device.

**Where it travels.** In every peer-to-peer session, whatever the transport (TCP, QUIC, through a relay):

- `GET /p2p/heads` answers with the statement of the device asked (`404` while it has none). The service asks every peer in its table, at most every ten seconds and never for more than three seconds at a time, before a ledger pull.
- `GET /p2p/changed` (a device announcing that it published something, section 73) now carries the sender's statement as a path segment, `/p2p/changed/<base64url of the statement>` when the path stays under 24 KB (about 150 devices; larger vaults send the bare message and are asked for `/p2p/heads`), and is answered with the receiver's statement instead of `ok`. One message exchanges both directions. The head of a request, which was limited to 16 KB over TCP and 8 KB over QUIC, is limited to 32 KB for both.
- The rendezvous record (`vault/peers/<device>.enc`, section 15) carries a statement of the sender with just its own head, signed with the device key like the larger one (`PeerRecord::head`; the record is encrypted as before). Devices that only meet through the storage still compare their progress with what others published. A record is republished when the head it names changes, not when it is signed again (`same_as`).

**What a receiver does.** A statement is kept only when the sender is a trusted full device of this vault other than this one (not removed, not a replica or a member), the vault matches, the signature verifies against the registry's key for the sender, the times are not absurd (Lamport clock not beyond the reader's clock by more than 2^40, wall clock not more than a day ahead), and it is newer than the one kept from that sender (by Lamport clock, then wall clock, then the sum of the heads). Anything else is dropped silently. The newest statement of each sender is kept in `ledger/heads-seen.json` in the device directory; what a sender said is forgotten when it is removed. Statements heard on the server side (`/p2p/changed`) or read from records are queued and checked at the next ledger pull, because the server has no engine.

**What it does with it.** A statement never moves a head, ingests a batch, retires anything or makes this device refuse a batch; it only raises the question of section 58's freshness check. After a pull, for every device A that the sender lists and this device knows, the reach of the sender (the larger of its `seq` and its `checkpoint`, never beyond a removed device's cut-off) is compared with this device's head for A, exactly as the `seen` acknowledgements are: when it is higher, `ledger_health.behind` records `{device, name, held, seen, pulls, source}` with `source` `peer` (`acks` for a gap learned from acknowledgements, `pending` for batches waiting for a predecessor). The pull just asked every storage for the missing numbers. When the same gap is still there after three pulls in a row, `ledger_health.warnings` says "a storage may be withholding or rolling back ledger batches of <A> (a peer holds #<seq>, the storages give #<seq>)"; `varsto status` and `varsto ledger status` show it after "ok", the Help page shows a warning (English, Finnish, Swedish) and the event `ledger.health` is published again with the warnings. The gap and the warning go as soon as a storage delivers the batches. Statements older than two weeks are not compared.

**Limits.** Said plainly:

- A storage is not caught when it is the only channel: no peer-to-peer path to any other full device and rendezvous records the storage withholds or replays as well. Devices must meet, directly or through a relay, or read each other's rendezvous record at a time the storage still serves it.
- A rollback is noticed by a device that has not yet pulled the lost batches; a device that already holds them keeps them (heads only move forward) and does not warn about its own copy. Its next push does not restore other devices' batches.
- A statement is the word of one trusted full device: a device that is itself compromised can raise a false warning (and nothing more: no batch is refused or moved). A sender that is not reachable, or pushed nothing yet, adds nothing.
- A gap that is real but explained by a storage that is only unreachable looks the same until the next successful pull.
- The statement of the sender's own head counts what its `pushed.json` says; a device that lost that file understates its head until it pushes again.

Tests: `ledger_heads::tests` (a statement round trips and verifies; an edited, renamed, foreign or wrongly signed one is refused; only a newer one replaces the kept one; a statement rides a `/p2p/changed` path and a large one does not; heard statements are kept per vault), `crates/varsto-core/tests/ledger_gaps.rs` (`a_peers_heads_reveal_batches_the_storage_withholds`: two devices on a directory storage, the storage drops the newest batches of A for B, B meets A over loopback peer-to-peer and shows `behind` with source `peer` and, after three pulls, the warning, in `describe`, in the Help page and with nothing ingested; `a_rolled_back_storage_is_caught_by_a_peer`; `statements_from_removed_or_forged_senders_are_ignored`; `a_rendezvous_record_carries_the_senders_own_head`; `the_changed_message_exchanges_statements`), and the lab scenario `ledger-rollback` (not run when it was written).

## 75. Format 3 peers and transport (behind the `format3` feature)

Work package 9 of `format-3-design.md` (its section 8). Built behind the `format3` cargo feature as library code that the engine switches to with the format (work package 12): the format-2 transport of sections 15, 60, 73 and 74 is unchanged in every build, with or without the feature, and nothing here is unconditional. The TCP listener, the QUIC node and the peer table take a format-3 mode (`Server::with_tls`, `quic::Node::start_v3`, `Peers::build_v3`); everything else is in `p2p::v3`, `pair::v3` and `fingerprint`.

**Peer records** (`p2p::v3::peer`). A SignedRecord of kind `peer` at `vault/peers/<device hex>/<seq 16>-0000.rec`, key id `registry@<e>`, newest seq wins. Body (postcard): `device`, `seq` (both checked against the envelope), `name` (at most 128 bytes), `port`, `lan_addrs` (ranked as section 68 ranks them), `public_addrs`, `udp_public`, `udp_local`, `mapped_tcp`, `mapped_udp` (at most 32 each), `cert_sha256` (32 bytes, the TLS pin, never zero), `nat`, `relay_via` (at most 16 device ids), `reachable`, `heads` (at most 256 `(device, seq, body_hash)`), `pushed` (at most 64 `(storage identity, seq)`). A writer publishes put-if-absent at the next free seq and then deletes its records older than the one before, so the stream holds two. The **directory** is the newest accepted record (acceptance rule of the design's section 5: a trusted full device's signature, the registry key) of every device; it leaves out this device, any record carrying this device's certificate, and a certificate two devices claim (neither is admitted).

**Admission.** The TLS server of both transports admits a client certificate only when the directory names it; this device's own certificate is refused when accepting and is never dialled (the self-probe of reachability, which dialled the own certificate, answers "no" in format 3). ALPN `varsto-p2p/3`.

**Request tokens** (`p2p::v3::token`): `X-Varsto-Auth: <device>:<responder>:<unix>:<nonce 32 hex>:<mac 64 hex>`, `mac = keyed_hash(exporter, aad3("p2p-token", [device, responder, unix le, nonce, path]))`, `exporter` = the TLS exporter of the connection (label `EXPORTER-varsto-p2p-token`, empty context, 32 bytes). The responder requires: the certificate of the connection belongs to a device of the directory and that device is `device`; `responder` is itself; `|unix - now| <= 300 s`; the nonce unseen (at most 65 536 kept). Chosen over a per-request K6 signature (the design's wording, "tied to the TLS connection itself"): the client's identity is already proved by the mutually authenticated handshake against a pin from a signed record, so a MAC keyed by the exporter adds the binding to this connection, path and responder at the cost of one keyed hash instead of a 3.3 KB hybrid signature per request (a download asks for thousands of blocks). No vault secret is involved; the `p2p-auth` key is not used.

**Relays.** A token cannot travel past the connection it was made for. The relay checks the asker's token (for the full `/p2p/via/<device>/<path>` path, responder = the relay) and asks the registrant on its registration connection with a token of its own for `<path>` plus `X-Varsto-For: <asker>`. The registrant accepts `X-Varsto-For` only on its registration connection with a relay of its directory and only for a device of its directory, and answers as to that device.

**TLS over TCP** (`p2p::v3::tls`): the TCP listener runs a TLS 1.3 handshake (5 s) with the same certificate, pin and client verification as QUIC before reading any request; inside the session the requests are those of section 15. A client that does not start with a TLS handshake gets a TLS alert and the connection closes; no request is read and no byte of the protocol is sent in the clear. Kept-alive sessions are pooled per (address, pinned certificate). TCP is kept rather than removed: section 15 measured QUIC slower than TCP beyond the LAN, and QUIC remains the path through NATs.

**LAN beacons** (`p2p::v3::beacon`): `VARSTO3 <tag> <device hex> <port> <seq> <sig hex>`, `sig` the Ed25519 part of K6 (64 bytes) over `aad3("p2p-beacon", [tag, device, port le, seq le])`. A listener ignores a beacon that does not parse, is of another vault tag, is its own, is from a device that is not a trusted full device, does not verify, or whose seq is not above the highest it accepted from that device. Sequence numbers start at `unix seconds << 16` and rise per beacon. An address from a beacon is added only to a device of the directory (one with a pinned certificate); a beacon of a device without a record adds nothing.

**Pairing** (`pair::v3`): the joining device's hello is `{"v": 3, "name", "spake", "k6", "k8"}` (hex public keys); both key confirmations are `keyed_hash(spake key, "varsto-pair/3 confirm <side>" || BLAKE3(aad3("pair-hello", [name, k6, k8])))`, so keys swapped by a device in the middle fail the confirmation. After the confirmation the offering device calls its `Approve` with the name, both keys and the six-word fingerprint (`fingerprint::of_keys`: the section 24 construction over K6 and K8, context `varsto format-3 device fingerprint v1`, the same words as folder requests of work package 8a) and sends nothing when the person declines (`{"error": "declined"}`, the offer closes). The bundle travels under the SPAKE2 key with `vault_key` empty; the vault key is sealed to the joiner's K8 inside it (hybrid KEM, AEAD with `aad3("pair-vault-key", [vault id, BLAKE3(k8)])`). Discovery answers to `rendezvous(code)`, 10 bits of `derive_key("e2ee-sync-format/3/pair-rendezvous", digits)` as a decimal number: concurrent offers are told apart as well as by three digits, a listener learns no more about the code than from three digits (about a million candidates stay, three online tries), and no part of the password is sent.

**Head exchange** (`p2p::v3::heads`), built on section 74's occasions: a device's head list (at most 256 `(device, seq, body_hash)`) travels in its peer record and in `/p2p/changed/<list>` and `/p2p/heads/<list>` (postcard, base64url; the bare paths ask for everything). The answer (postcard) is the responder's list and, for every device where it holds a newer batch, that batch's envelope with an empty body, at most 64 and 512 KiB. An envelope verifies with the writer's K6 (kind `batch`, zero scope, one part, a `ledger@` key, this vault) and proves the head; the writer's own entry in its accepted peer record proves it as well. Proofs are kept per writer in `ledger/watch.json`. The batches a device lacks up to a proof are fetched from the peer that proved it (`GET /p2p/ledger/<device hex>/<seq>`, the whole record, at most 64 per device and round), checked by the acceptance rule under their storage name, the proved one against the proved hash, and handed to the ledger store, which does not push them. Head lists a peer sends are its claim only; the responder keeps the newest per sender for the engine.

**Rollback warning.** After every pull, for each (storage S, device D) where S is expected to hold D's batch n (D's accepted peer record lists S in `pushed` with a seq of at least n, or this device pushed there itself), S was listed, and a proof of n older than the pull exists: when S shows less than n, the pair is suspect; after two such pulls in a row the warning is "storage <S> may be withholding or rolled back: it shows ledger batches of <D> up to <shown>, but <source> proved <n>", once a day as a notification. A storage that was not reachable is not judged and keeps its state; the pair clears when S shows the batch or D's record stops listing S. Showing the warning in `varsto status`, `varsto ledger status`, the Help page and notifications (with Finnish and Swedish text) is the engine's part (work package 12).

Tests (`p2p::v3::tests`, `pair::v3::tests`, `fingerprint::tests`, run by CI with the feature): newest peer record wins, untrusted and foreign-key records are refused and counted, a record with this device's certificate and a contested certificate are left out; tokens for another responder, connection, device or path, stale, replayed or garbage tokens are refused, and `X-Varsto-For` counts only on a registration; TLS over TCP admits only certificates of the directory and refuses an unpinned server; a token of one TLS connection is refused on another; a plain HTTP client gets an alert record (`0x15`) and no protocol bytes; the own certificate is refused when dialling and when accepting, over TCP and QUIC; QUIC serves directory devices with connection-bound tokens and refuses others; a relay checks the asker's token and asks with its own, also through the peer table; beacons that are unsigned, forged, foreign, unknown, own or replayed are ignored; pairing shows name, keys and fingerprint, delivers a vault key that only the joiner's K8 opens, sends nothing to a declined device, shows nothing for a wrong code, and is found by its tag; head exchange proves a withheld batch and the peer hands it over, heads ride on `/p2p/changed` and in the peer record, forged or foreign proofs and mismatched batches are refused; the rollback warning comes after two pulls and only for a storage the writer confirmed.

## 76. Format 3 folder membership for people: requests, approval, roles, removal, recovery copies (behind the `format3` feature)

Work package 8b of `format-3-design.md` (section 6). The records are the ones of work package 8a (`membership3`); this section is how a person reaches them. Nothing here changes a stored format, `FORMAT_VERSION` stays 2, and a build without the `format3` feature has the same commands and routes, which answer "not available in this build".

**Where the keys come from until the format flips.** The engine of a format-2 vault stands in for the device keys of format 3 (`Engine::f3_*`, `engine/membership3_ops.rs`): K6 is the device signing key, K8 the KEM key that `vault-keys.enc` already holds (made on first use), K3 the vault key (every epoch the engine holds). Each operation opens a `Membership` over the metadata storages and publishes this device's device record when the registry lacks it. Work package 12 replaces this with the keys `varsto init` makes.

**Commands** (`varsto`): `folder request <folder> [--role editor|viewer]` prints this device's six words; `folder requests [<folder>]` lists pending requests (device name, role asked, six words) for the folders this device owns; `folder approve <folder> <device> [--role editor|viewer] [--yes]` prints the device's name and six words to standard error and asks "Do the six words match? [y/N]" on standard input, then issues the folder record entry and the grant (an answer other than y writes nothing; `--yes` skips the question); `folder deny <folder> <device>`; `folder members <folder>` (participants with roles, denied devices, pending requests, folder epochs two owners started at once); `folder role <folder> <device> <owner|editor|viewer>` (an owner's folder record and a grant carrying the role; a lower role does not take keys back); `folder remove <folder> <device> [--yes]` (asks; starts folder epoch e+1 through `start_folder_epoch`, with the device's newest ledger batch as the cut-off); `folder resolve-epoch <folder> <n> --keep <device>`; `recovery escrow [<folder>] [--with-recovery-key] [--recovery-file F]` writes the escrow records that `missing_escrows` lists, with the recovery words from a file, `VARSTO_RECOVERY_WORDS`, a pipe, or a hidden prompt (never the command line; `--with-recovery-key` uses the environment copy only); `recovery kit --recovery-words | --new-recovery-key` prints `escrow::kit_text` (both word sets). A folder is a name or an id (hex prefix of four digits or more), a device a name or an id likewise. `varsto status` prints `warning: folder <name> has no recovery copy: run varsto recovery escrow <name>` for every folder epoch this device holds with no escrow, on standard error (and through the service's `/api/status` as `recovery_warnings`). `join --recovery-words` is a stub that says what work package 12 connects (`Membership::recover_all_folders`). `folder create-f3 <name>` (hidden) makes a folder in the membership records until `folder add` does.

**Local API.** `GET /api/folder/requests[?folder=]`, `POST /api/folder/request {folder, role}`, `POST /api/folder/approve {folder, device, role?, fingerprint?, fingerprint_confirmed: true}` (refused unless `fingerprint_confirmed` is the boolean `true`; with `fingerprint` the words must still be the request's), `POST /api/folder/deny {folder, device}`, `GET /api/folder/members[?folder=]` (all folders without it), `POST /api/folder/role {folder, device, role}`, `POST /api/folder/remove {folder, device, confirm: true}` (the same path as the vault-wide folder removal, which takes `name`; a body with `device` is a removal from the folder), `POST /api/folder/resolve-epoch {folder, epoch, keep}`, `GET /api/recovery/escrow` (the missing copies), `POST /api/recovery/escrow {words, folder?}`. `/api/state` says `format3`. A successful change publishes `folder.changed` on the event stream and asks for a sync; the service publishes `folder.request` once per new pending request (and a notification) after each sync round. MCP keys reach none of these routes.

**The background service never answers a request.** It lists (`f3_requests`) and announces; approving, denying, granting, changing roles, removing and writing recovery copies need a person at a command line or the page. A test in `service.rs` reads its own source and fails if any of those calls appears.

**Pairing, offering side.** `POST /api/pair/start {"format": 3}` opens `Offer::start_v3`; its `Approve` callback holds the joining device's name and fingerprint (`ask` in `GET /api/pair/status`, with the folders this device owns) and blocks up to five minutes. `POST /api/pair/answer {allow, folders[], fingerprint?, role?}` writes, for the ticked folders (default none), a folder record entry and a grant sealed to the K8 of the joining device's hello (`Membership::grant_to_joiner`: the hello's keys stand in for a device record, which the joiner publishes after joining), and only then lets the vault key go out; `allow: false` declines. The page opts in only when the device owns a format-3 folder. The joining side shows its own fingerprint once its keys exist (work package 12 wires the joining engine).

**Page.** The Files page has a "Folder access" card per folder: requests with name, role asked and six words and Approve/Deny (Approve opens a dialog with the words and a box "The six words match on both devices" that must be ticked), members with a role selector and Remove (the confirmation names the consequence: a new folder epoch; the device keeps what it read), unresolved folder epochs, and a banner with "Write recovery copies…" that asks for the recovery words.

Tests: `crates/varsto-cli/tests/folder_approval_cli.rs` (the commands through the binary), `desktop.rs` tests (each route, the refusal without `fingerprint_confirmed`, pairing with ticked, no and declined folders, the not-available answers), `service.rs` (`the_service_never_approves_or_grants`), the interface coverage tests (fi, sv).

## 77. Format 3: a pack-level ledger, folder block maps and signed records (behind the `format3` feature)

**Status.** Format 3 ships only when `FORMAT_VERSION` becomes 3, which the integration package (work package 12) does. Until then this section describes code that is built behind the `format3` cargo feature of varsto-core (passed through by varsto-cli and varsto-ffi, off by default): every build still reads and writes format 2 (section 62), no function below is called by the format-2 engine, and CI compiles, lints and tests the feature. Where this section says "is", it describes that code; where the code is silent the binding text is [format-3-design.md](format-3-design.md), and a line marked **As built** or "design only" says which. Where the code and the design differ, the code is described and the difference is named in one clause. Vaults are created fresh; nothing older is read (no migration, 77.15). Sections 75 and 76 describe the peers and the membership commands of the same series in their own words; this section repeats only what the other parts need.

Notation is that of the design. Ids are binary on the wire: vault, device, folder and backup 16 bytes; chunk id, object name, pack id and body hash 32 bytes; hex only in object names on a storage. Integers are little-endian. "postcard" is the `postcard` 1.x encoding of the Rust struct as written, "zstd" is level 3, `aad(type, fields)` is `crypto::aad_for_format` (length-prefixed format number, AEAD id, type and fields; the records bind format 3, 77.5), `derive(K, purpose, scope...)` is BLAKE3 `derive_key` with the context `e2ee-sync-format/3/<purpose>` and length-prefixed scope ids (`membership3::derive3`).

| Part | Where | Subsection |
|---|---|---|
| keys | `membership3::{keys, grants, escrow}` | 77.1 |
| packs and size classes | `packs`, `storage` | 77.2 |
| folder block map, marks, collection, repair | `blockmap`, `gc3` | 77.3 |
| ledger | `ledger3` | 77.4 |
| signed records | `record` | 77.5 |
| folder membership and recovery | `membership3`, `fingerprint`, the commands of section 76 | 77.6 |
| manifests, trash, history, snapshots | `manifest3`, `trash3`, `snapshot3` | 77.7 |
| peers and transport | `p2p::v3`, `pair::v3` (section 75) | 77.8 |
| replicas, pools, transferrers, moves, drains, quotas | `storages3` | 77.9 |
| fsck, rebuild, scrub | `fsck3`, `rebuild3`, `scrub3` | 77.10 |
| authorization, what each party learns, limits, tests | | 77.11 to 77.14 |

### 77.1 Keys

All keys are 256 bits. Changes against section 3 (and sections 21, 24, 62):

| Key | Purpose | Made | Change from format 2 |
|---|---|---|---|
| K1 recovery key | escrow of every folder key epoch | 256 random bits, 24 BIP-39 words, optional Shamir 2-of-3 (`recovery::split`) | designed in format 2 and never shipped; now the second word set of the kit (77.6) |
| K2 = derive(K1, `recovery-wrap`) | wraps escrow | derived | as designed |
| K3ₑ vault key, epoch e | registry, vault ledger, peer records, vault epoch records, local keyring, pool markers | 256 random bits; epoch 0 is the first word set of the kit; later epochs only as vault grants (77.6) | no longer derives any folder key |
| K4 device unlock key | wraps K3ₑ, K6, K8 on one device | Argon2id (64 MiB, 3, 1) of the NFKC passphrase | unchanged |
| K6 device signing key | signs every record, batch, checkpoint and peer token binding | Ed25519 + ML-DSA-65, made on the device | signs records, not only batches; the device id is BLAKE3(K6 public)[..16] and the device record carries K6 |
| K8 device KEM key | receives grants and vault keys | X25519 + ML-KEM-768, made on the device | in the device record (no separate `vault/kem/` file) |
| K9ₑ folder key, folder epoch e | one folder, one epoch | 256 random bits, made by the owner that starts the epoch | **never derived from K3**; grants and escrow carry it; folder records carry no key |
| K10ₑ = derive(K9ₑ, `folder-metadata`, folder) | manifests, maps, trash and history, snapshots, thumbnails, notes, access records | derived | also block maps |
| K11 = derive(K9₀, `dedup-hash`, folder) | chunk ids and content hashes, fixed at the folder's first epoch | derived once, carried in every grant and escrow | unchanged |
| K12, K14, chunk nonce | chunker seed, chunk key, nonce | as section 62 | unchanged |
| K13ₑ Strongroom key | as K9ₑ for a Strongroom folder | as section 17; escrowed like K9 only if the user opts in | never under K3 (design only: no Strongroom code is part of this series) |
| registry key = derive(K3ₑ, `device-registry`) | device, folder, request, peer, policy, placement, settings and storage-name records | derived | also folder records and requests |
| ledger key = derive(K3ₑ, `ledger`) | vault ledger batches and checkpoints | derived | unchanged |
| vault epoch record key = derive(K3ₑ, `epoch-record`) | the record of vault epoch e | derived | new; the design names the `epoch@<e>` key without a derivation |
| share-ledger key = derive(K9ₑ, `share-ledger`, folder) | folder-scoped batches of members | derived | unchanged |
| escrow key = derive(K2, `folder-escrow`, vault, K3₀) | escrow records | derived | new (77.6) |

Removed: folder keys derived from K3, folder keys in folder records, the `pack-tombstones` key, the `p2p-auth` token key (77.8), plain and `vst1` share tokens, bare (unsigned) epoch records and `SHARE_EPOCH_BASE`: with no vault-derived folder keys, one folder epoch sequence 0, 1, 2, ... serves removals of own devices and of members alike.

**Key ids** name the key that opens a body and are inside every signature (77.5): `registry@<e>`, `ledger@<e>`, `epoch@<e>`, `meta:<folder hex>@<e>`, `share-ledger:<folder hex>@<e>`, `kem:<recipient device hex>`, `recovery`, `replica`, `none`. The grammar is strict (`record::KeyId::parse`): lower-case hex, decimal epochs without leading zeros, at most `MAX_KEY_ID` = 64 bytes, so one key has one spelling. `none` (body not encrypted) is allowed for `request` records only.

**What a holder of K3 alone sees.** A device joined with the vault words and not yet approved for any folder reads the registry (device names, folder names and settings, participants), the vault ledger (pack ids and size classes, which storages hold which packs, folder ids, writers, record announcements) and the peer records. It can verify packs against their ids, copy packs between storages and sign vault batches. It cannot open any manifest, map, trash record, snapshot or block, and learns no chunk id, object name or place of a block (test `a_device_with_the_vault_words_sees_names_and_no_content`).

### 77.2 Blocks and packs

Blocks are as in section 62: the keyed chunker with size tiers (`chunking: 2`), chunk id = keyed BLAKE3(K11, plaintext), `pack(plain)` = method byte || payload, the chunk object `nonce (24) || ciphertext || tag (16)` under K14 with the derived nonce, object name = BLAKE3(chunk object), and the readers' three checks (nonce against the packed bytes, plaintext against the chunk id, object against its name). One function seals (`chunk_seal::seal_chunk`), one opens (`chunk_seal::open_chunk`).

**Pack layout.** `packs/<xx>/<pack id hex>`: member objects back to back from offset 0, then random padding, nothing else: no header, trailer or index. The pack id is the BLAKE3 hash of all bytes, padding included. Padding is bytes of the operating system's CSPRNG (`packs::build`); a zero tail would show where the members end to anyone holding the pack.

**Size classes.** The length of a pack is exactly one class: 256 KiB, 1, 4, 16 or 64 MiB on hot, local, pool and transferrer storages, and the same plus 256 MiB on cold storages (KiB = 2^10, MiB = 2^20). The class code is 0 for 256 KiB up to 5 for 256 MiB (`ledger3::events::class_bytes`: 2^(18 + 2c) bytes). `packs::padded_len` takes the smallest class that holds the members, never above the storage's top class (`top_class`: 64 MiB, or 256 MiB for a storage with a pack target above 64 MiB). A reader refuses a pack, a place list entry or a map pack reference whose length is no class: `"pack <id> has length <n>, not a size class"` (`packs::Reader::loc` under the feature; `blockmap::BlockMap::check_limits` and `verify_pack` for maps and verification). `storage::MAX_PACK` is the largest class, 256 MiB. Members never overlap and never pass the end of the members area, and a member object is at least 41 bytes (method byte, nonce and tag) and at most `MAX_CHUNK_SIZE + 1 + 40` (16 MiB, the method byte, nonce and tag; `packs::MAX_OBJECT`).

**Choosing the class.** A push (and an import, a repair, a data move, a rewrite) collects members in a buffer and places them first-fit decreasing into bins of at most the storage's top class (`packs::first_fit_decreasing`; blocks of one file stay in their original order inside a pack); a pack closes when the next member would pass the top class, and the last pack of an operation takes the smallest class its members fit in. Padding is therefore below the next class step: a pack is never larger than the next class above its members. Measured on the benchmark data (section 62's Limits): 50 000 small files stored 22 packs, 1.3 % over the data; one 1 GiB incompressible file stored 17 packs, 1.6 % over the data. **Imports** collect contents across files exactly like pushes, so a pack never shows one file's size. A content counts as imported, and its blocks are claimed, once every pack holding one of its blocks is written.

**Writing.** Put-if-absent; S3 multipart in 16 MiB parts above 64 MiB; a HEAD before the PUT finds a pack that is there. Packs are written before the map record that lists them and before the batch that claims them (77.3, "Writer").

**Format-2 readers keep lenient reads** (the amendment to section 62 made with the size classes): a build without the feature reads a pack, place, index or tombstone entry of any length up to `storage::MAX_PACK`, so packs of older builds (not padded) are still read, verified and collected, and only writes produce size classes; only a format-3 reader refuses a length that is no class. A build from before the size classes (0.0.1-alpha.15) reads padded packs but, in garbage collection, leaves a padded pack alone, and an emptied one stays until a newer device removes it; garbage collection in format 2 accounts for the members from offset 0, and what follows the last known member must be the padding of the pack's class.

**As built.** The design builds one pack per target set (the set of storages a group of blocks goes to), so that a pack is byte-identical on every storage of the set and has one id there. The push path still builds one pack per storage (section 62's "Not yet done"): two storages of one push hold packs of the same members with different padding and ids. The ledger and the map handle one pack held on several storages under one id, and rewrites, compaction, whole-pack copies, replica rounds and moves write it byte-identical. Cost of small pushes: one small edit costs a 256 KiB pack on every storage it goes to, until compaction folds them (77.3).

### 77.3 The folder block map

The block map says, for every block of a folder, in which pack and at which offset it is. It is per folder, per writer device, and sequence-numbered; it takes over from format 2's pack indexes, tombstones and per-block claims.

**Stream and record.** `maps/<folder hex>/<device hex>/<seq 16 digits>-<part 4 digits>.rec`, a SignedRecord (77.5) of kind `block-map`, scope = the folder, key id `meta:<folder>@<e>` with e the newest folder epoch the writer holds, announced in the writer's batch (`record_published`). `seq` starts at 1 and rises by 1 per record. Body (`blockmap::BlockMap`; postcard, then zstd, then sealed):

```text
BlockMap {
  folder: [u8;16], device: [u8;16], seq: u64, lamport: u64,
  base: u64,                 // seq of the base this record builds on; == seq for a base
  prev: [u8;32],             // record_hash of record seq-1; zeros for seq 1
  packs: Vec<PackRef>,       // table, referenced by index
  entries: Vec<MapEntry>,    // base: every live place this writer wrote; delta: places added since seq-1
  marks: Vec<Mark>,          // dead / undead / lost marks on places of any writer of the folder
}
PackRef  { id: [u8;32], class: u8 }     // 0 = 256 KiB ... 5 = 256 MiB
MapEntry { object: [u8;32], chunk: [u8;32], pack: u32, offset: u32, len: u32, plain_len: u32, epoch: u32, flags: u8 }
Mark     { object: [u8;32], pack: [u8;32], offset: u32, kind: u8 }   // 1 dead, 2 undead, 3 lost
```

A place is identified by (pack id, offset); `object` repeats the object name so a reader checks every place it uses; `len` is the object length (packed length = `len - 40`), `plain_len` the plaintext length (the chunk AAD needs both). `flags` bit 0 (`FLAG_DEAD`) is the writer's own settled view that its place is dead, kept in bases until the place is collected; other bits must be 0. Limits (`check_limits`, step 6 of the acceptance rule): at most 4 194 304 entries and 1 048 576 marks per part and 65 536 packs, `offset + len` within the class size, a class of at most 5, an object of at most `packs::MAX_OBJECT` bytes, `plain_len` at most 16 MiB, mark kinds 1 to 3, an index into `packs` for every entry, `seq` ≥ 1 and `base` between 1 and `seq`, and the body's folder, device and seq equal to the envelope's. **As built:** the per-part limits are applied as that many times the record's part count (parts are byte ranges of one compressed body), and the 65 536 packs are counted over the whole record; the map record accepts any class from 0 to 5 whatever the storage, the cold-only rule of the 256 MiB class is checked where the storage is known (fsck, 77.10).

**Base and delta.** A base lists every live place the writer wrote (its packs that the view still shows on at least one storage) and the marks it made that are not gone with their place; a delta lists places added since `seq - 1` (new packs, rewrites, repairs) and new marks. The writer publishes a base for its first record, and after that when 1024 deltas follow the last base or the deltas' stored bytes reach 1/4 of the base's (`blockmap::BASE_AFTER_DELTAS`, `BASE_BYTES_DIVISOR`, whichever comes first; stored bytes are the padded sizes of `record_published`). The rule is looser than the manifests' (77.7): a map base of a large folder is hundreds of MB of incompressible hashes and deltas are pure additions. In a base, the marks kept per place are the newest dead or undead mark of this writer and its lost mark, unless a pending mark replaces them; marks on places whose pack no storage holds any more leave the base, and so do entries of packs held nowhere. A record over 32 MiB is written in parts (77.5). A writer that finds a record under its next seq that no batch announced (a crash between the record and its batch) skips that seq and writes a base after it, because a delta would name a `prev` no reader can check.

**Who writes.** Every device that writes packs of the folder with the editor or owner role: own full devices, members of shared folders with the editor role, forwarders (they hold the folder key), and the collectors and repairers that write the new packs of rewrites and repairs. Viewers, replicas and holders of K3 alone write no maps (the acceptance rule refuses them, 77.5).

**Writer.** One push (or import, data move, repair, rewrite, forward run) writes its packs, then one map record listing their members to every hot metadata storage (put-if-absent; the first storage decides a seq race), then seals one batch with `pack_stored` for every (pack, storage) written and `record_published` for the map record (and the manifest record, 77.7), and pushes it (`blockmap::MapWriter::publish`, `events`). A crash before the batch leaves packs no claim names, which garbage collection deletes after 7 days when no map lists them (orphans, below).

**Reader and the local table.** A device that holds K10 of the folder keeps a table per folder, `state/maps/<folder hex>.tbl` (device-local state, not a wire format): the union of every accepted writer's newest base and the deltas after it, as fixed-width rows of 88 bytes sorted by object name, a second array of row numbers sorted by (chunk id, epoch), and the marks in a side table sorted by place (88 bytes each), with a footer (`VBMT`, version 1, the folder, counts) and a BLAKE3 over the file. Lookups are binary searches with positioned reads, so memory is O(1) in the number of blocks; disk is about 88 bytes per place. Every change writes a new file beside the old one (`.tbl.tmp`, flushed) and renames it over it, so a crash leaves the old table or the new one, and a table that fails its hash or its sizes is treated as empty and rebuilt from the bases. Building sorts with bounded memory (runs of 2^20 rows, merged from files). A pull (`blockmap::pull`) lists only streams whose announced newest seq is past the one applied, reads from a newer announced base when there is one (that writer's rows and marks are replaced), accepts records through `record::accept`, and requires the announced base to equal the body's; a delta applies only when it is the next seq, names the applied base and its `prev` equals the applied record's hash, else it and the writer's later records are skipped and reported. A mark on a pack the view gives to another folder is refused and counted (`marks_refused`).

**Finding a block** (fetch, restore, verification, P2P serving): the manifest gives the object name; the table gives its places (pack, offset, length); the ledger view gives the storages that hold each pack (claimed, not dropped, not retired); one ranged read; the object is checked against its name; it is opened with the chunk's epoch (`blockmap::find_block`, `open_block`). Places with a dead or lost mark are tried only after the others (`LedgerView::block_places`).

**Copies of a block** are the storages holding a live place of it: the pack held there and the place without a dead or lost mark (`LedgerView::block_copies`; the storage code of 77.9 also counts the writer's own dead flag like a dead mark). Policies, placement, staged moves, disk groups, drains and quotas count this way (77.9).

**Dedup for a writer.** Before writing a block the writer looks up its chunk id in its table (`blockmap::needs_write`). A place counts for storage S when its pack is held on S in the view and the place has no dead or lost mark this device holds (settled or not; an undead mark newer than every dead one revives it). If every target holds such a place, nothing is written; for the targets that lack one, the block goes into new packs for those storages only, with the same object. A block whose only places carry a dead mark is written again, never revived by reuse. Across epochs a chunk the folder already holds keeps its object: the oldest epoch with a live place anywhere wins, and within one epoch the object held on most targets.

**Dead marks, settling and collection.** Liveness is decided by references, never by marks alone. An owner or editor device that finds an object referenced by nothing publishes a `dead` mark for each of its places in its next map record. Referenced means: a current (not deleted) file in any accepted writer's newest manifest, an unexpired trash or history entry, an unexpired snapshot of the folder (`gc3::References`; `trash3::Refs` combines the three). The mark made in map record `m` of marker `M` is **settled** when every current writer of the folder (owners and editors, own devices and members, not revoked) has signed a batch whose `seen` names `M` at or after the batch that announced `m` (`LedgerView::settled`; members' folder-scoped batches count here and for pruning, never for the vault ledger's). A place is **collectable** when a dead mark of a current writer is on it and settled, no undead mark with a larger (Lamport time, device) stamp is on it, and nothing references the object (`gc3::place_verdict`). The race "a block deleted on one device while another skips writing it" is closed by the ordering: a writer that skipped a block did so before it pulled the mark, so its manifest naming the block is announced in a batch before the one in which it acknowledges the mark, and the collector sees that manifest before the place becomes collectable. A device that finds a reference to an object whose place is dead-marked publishes `undead` marks (`gc3::mark_round`). A hostile or mistaken mark delays nothing and deletes nothing that is referenced. Between several marks of one place, the newest by (Lamport time of the carrying record, marker device) decides, and a `lost` mark is sticky.

**As built (garbage collection).** The collector requires that it applied every reference record (manifest, trash, snapshot) its view shows announced by any writer of the folder, not only those announced up to the batches that settled the mark: the view keeps each writer's newest acknowledgement, not the batch that first carried it, and the stronger condition implies the design's (`References::behind`; a writer whose stream is forked counts as behind until the fork is resolved). Dead marks are likewise made only when no writer is behind. Only marks whose marker is a current writer count for collection (others are ignored and counted in `marks_ignored`), on top of the acceptance rule refusing the map records of viewers, K3-only devices and replicas outright. Undead marks are published by any writer that finds a reference, not only the marker. A pack whose known members do not follow one another from offset 0 holds a member the collector's table does not list and is neither dropped nor rewritten.

**Who may mark.** Owners and editors that hold K10 (`gc3::Standing::may_mark`), members of shared folders with the editor role included. Marks from viewers, replicas and K3-only devices cannot exist (those devices write no `block-map` record), marks of devices demoted or removed since are ignored, and marks naming a place in a pack of another folder are refused and counted.

**Garbage collection** (`gc3::collect`), run by the trash upkeep on own full devices with the write role that hold K10; members do not collect (`Standing::may_collect`: they may not hold the owner's storages). One run is one action: new packs first, then one map record for them, then one batch with `pack_stored` (new packs), `record_published` and `pack_dropped`, pushed; only then are old packs deleted (a failed commit deletes nothing):
