- a pack whose every member place is collectable is dropped on every storage that holds it; on a cold storage not before the price model's minimum storage duration, counted from the newest claim's batch time there (`drops_later`);
- a hot pack (held only on hot storages) whose collectable members make up more than half of its member bytes (padding not counted) is rewritten: one GET, the id and length checked, every live member checked against its name, live members into new padded packs for the same storages, the new places in a map record;
- cold and pool packs are never rewritten; their dead space goes when every member is collectable;
- **compaction**: hot packs of a class below 64 MiB, grouped by the set of storages that hold them, whose live members together fill at least 64 MiB (and are more than one pack) are merged in the same way (this bounds the cost of small pushes).

Entries of a dropped pack leave their writer's next base; marks on places that are gone leave the marker's next base. A pack the view shows deliberately dropped from a storage but still there (a crash between the batch and the delete) is deleted at once.

**Orphans.** A pack on a storage that the view does not show held there, that the view does not show dropped, and that no map table lists is an orphan. Storages keep no write times the collector can read, so the 7-day rule counts from when the collector first saw it (`gc3::OrphanState`, kept by the caller, `ORPHAN_SECS` = 7 days); then it is deleted. **As built:** this stands in for the design's "older than 7 days"; `fsck` reports orphans with an age when its caller supplies modification times (77.10).

**Verification and repair** (`gc3::verify`, section 26 per pack). Verification reads a whole pack (one GET up to `MAX_PACK`) and compares its length with its class and its BLAKE3 with the pack id (`PackCheck`: `Good`, `Missing`, `WrongLength`, `Mismatch`); a good pack yields `pack_verified`. It needs no folder key, so K3-only devices and replicas verify too. A pack missing (or damaged) on a storage but held intact on another is copied whole from it, checked against its id, and claimed under the same id (`copy_missing_pack`; a replica claims with the zero folder and map seq 0). Repair (`repair_pack`) is for a writer: it checks every member the table lists against its name, takes a corrupt member from the same place on another storage that holds the pack, else from the caller's fetchers (another pack, a peer, the local file, the block cache), writes the good members into new packs on that storage (cold storages too: a damaged pack must not keep counting, so repair is the exception to "cold is never rewritten"), publishes a map record with the new places and `lost` marks, and seals one batch with `pack_stored` (new) and `pack_dropped` (damaged), then deletes the damaged pack. A member with no good copy anywhere keeps its `lost` mark and is reported (the file is damaged) until a device that holds the file writes it again. **As built:** a `lost` mark goes on a place only when no other storage holding the same pack gives a good member there, since a place is (pack id, offset) on every storage of the pack; a member fetched from another pack, a peer, the local file or the cache still leaves the `lost` mark, so reads prefer the new place. Repairs, like marks, need the writer role.

**Pruning** (`gc3::prune_map`). A writer deletes its map records below its newest base B on every hot metadata storage once every reader of the folder (every participant holding K10: owners, editors and viewers, own devices and members, not revoked) has acknowledged the batch that announced B; cold storages are not pruned, and a storage that does not hold B is not pruned. Readers that join later read B and its deltas. `varsto status` is meant to say which device a base or a collection waits for (`GcPlan::waiting`, `map_readers_waiting`); the commands are not part of this series.

**Worked example** (the design's, run as the test `worked_example_a_pushes_deletes_b_marks_a_acks_b_collects`). Device A pushes `photo.jpg` (3 MiB, three blocks) to hot storages H1, H2 and cold C: pack P (class 4 MiB) for {H1, H2}, pack Q (class 4 MiB) for {C}; map record A/17 lists both packs and six places; batch A/240 carries `pack_stored(H1,P)`, `pack_stored(H2,P)`, `pack_stored(C,Q)`, `record_published(block-map, F, 17)` and `record_published(manifest, F, 52)`. B pulls and reads a2 from P on H1. After A deletes the photo and the trash days pass, B's upkeep finds the blocks unreferenced and publishes B/9 with dead marks in batch B/77. A acknowledges B/77 in A/251; B's settle check passes (A and B are the only writers), B finds no reference, and seals `pack_dropped(H1,P)`, `pack_dropped(H2,P)`, `pack_dropped(C,Q)` and deletes them (Q after C's minimum duration). If device D had re-added the photo before pulling B/9, D's push skipped writing the blocks and announced D's manifest in D/30; D acknowledges B/77 only in D/31; B waits for D/31, reads D's manifest, finds the reference and publishes undead marks (`worked_example_d_re_adds_the_photo_before_pulling_the_mark`).

### 77.4 The pack-level ledger

Unchanged from section 6, 22 and 58, and reused as they are where the types are format independent (`ledger3::store` follows the format-2 store): per-device hash chains, Lamport clocks with reservations, order-independent application, the incremental mailbox (`ledger/<device>/`, a pull lists only what follows the head), batches waiting for a missing predecessor (`<device>/pending/`), fork detection, fencing on verified objects only, the scopes of section 58, acknowledgements (`seen`), the pruning rule (a checkpoint's batches go once every reader acknowledged it) and one batch per user action. What changes is the unit: the ledger claims packs, not blocks, and its objects are SignedRecords.

**Batch body** (postcard, then zstd; replaces JSON): `Batch { device: [16], seq: u64, prev: [32], lamport: u64, created_utc: i64, seen: Vec<([16], u64)>, events: Vec<RawEvent> }` with `RawEvent { code: u16, payload: Vec<u8> }`; each payload is the postcard encoding of the event's fields. `prev` is the body hash of batch `seq - 1` of the device (zeros for seq 1); `seen` is the newest batch of other devices the writer held when it sealed this one (full devices: every device; members: the folder's devices only). The envelope is a SignedRecord (77.5) of kind `batch` at `ledger/<device hex>/<seq 16>-0000.rec` (a batch is always one part); its `body_hash` is the chain hash and its `key_id` is signed. `MAX_BATCH_BODY` is 16 MiB compressed, `MAX_EVENTS` 65 536 per batch, and a reader decompresses at most 64 MiB; an action that would exceed either seals further batches, each with the next Lamport time and its own `seen` (`events::split_events`; the one rule of "one batch per action" that bends). A reader skips a code it does not know (counted in `events_unknown`) and refuses a known code whose payload does not parse or breaks a limit (counted in `events_refused`); the rest of the batch counts.

| Code | Event | Fields | Who may make it |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `device_enrolled` | name (at most 128 bytes) | any scope (the signer's own name) |
| 2 | `folder_added` | folder | full |
| 3 | `pack_stored` | storage (name, 1 to 64 bytes), pack [32], class u8 (0 to 5), folder [16] (zeros from a replica), map_seq u64 (0 from a replica) | full; member: its folder; replica: zero folder and map seq 0 |
| 4 | `pack_verified` | storage, pack | full; member and viewer: packs of its folder; replica |
| 5 | `pack_dropped` | storage, pack | full; member: packs it stored |
| 6 | `record_published` | kind u8 (manifest, block-map, trash or snapshot), scope [16], seq u64 (≥ 1), base u64 (1 to seq), parts u16 (1 to 1024), body_hash [32], size u64 | full; member: kinds manifest, block-map and trash of its folder |
| 7 | `storage_retired` | storage | full |
| 8 | `snapshot_taken` | backup, seq, taken_utc, expires_utc, files, bytes | full (the source device) |
| 9 | `snapshot_expired` | backup, device, seq | full |
| 10 | `backup_stopped` | backup | full |

Removed against section 6: `chunk_stored`, `chunk_verified`, `chunk_dropped` (replaced by the pack events), `manifest_published` (replaced by `record_published`, which also announces maps, trash records and snapshots) and `chunk_on_device` (below). `snapshot_taken` carries times and sizes only; the hash a restore checks is in the snapshot's `record_published`. The `body_hash` a multi-part record announces is BLAKE3 over the concatenated `body_hash` of its parts in order; a one-part record announces its part's hash (77.5).

**`chunk_on_device` becomes a per-file flag.** Its users decide per folder and already hold the folder key: transferrer targeting and emptying, the folder safety indicator ("another device holds a copy"), `where`, and the forwarding report. "Device D holds object O" now means: some entry of D's newest manifest with `held` names O (77.7, `manifest3::HeldIndex`). No ledger event; holders of K3 alone lose the per-device byte counts of `where`, which they could not attribute to files anyway.

**Scopes** (`ledger3::EventScope`, selected by the batch's key id, `ledger@<e>` full, `replica`, `share-ledger:<folder>@<e>` member): the table above and 77.11. Events outside the signer's scope are left out and counted in `events_refused`. A member's `pack_stored` must name its own folder, and its `pack_stored`, `pack_verified` and `pack_dropped` count only when the pack's folder (named by the newest claim of a full device, else by the newest member claim) is the member's folder; a `pack_dropped` also needs that the member stored the pack. Those depend on the rest of the view, so they are kept aside and decided once all batches are applied (`LedgerView::finish`), which keeps application order-independent. A viewer's batch counts `pack_verified` of its folder's packs and `device_enrolled`, nothing else.

**View.** `ledger3::LedgerView` (in memory; the cache `ledger/view.rec` is postcard, then zstd, then AEAD under a local key, view format 1 of format 3, `ViewCache`):

```text
LedgerView { packs: HashMap<[u8;32], PackRecord>,
             streams: BTreeMap<(kind, scope, device), Stream>,   // announced records
             tables (interned devices, folders, storage names), devices, devices_at, folders, max_lamport,
             retired, batches, acks, folder_acks, forked, forked_streams, snapshots, backups_stopped,
             events_refused, events_unknown, marks_refused, ... }
PackRecord { class: u8, folder: u32, writer: u16, map_seq: u64, at: PackStamp,   // interned; from the newest claim
             places: SmallVec<[(storage u16, PackPlace)]> }
PackPlace  { claimed_by, verified_by, dropped_by: u64 device bitsets, stored_at, verified_at, dropped_at: Lamport,
             stored_utc, verified_utc }
Stream     { records: BTreeMap<seq, Announced { base, parts, body_hash, size, batch }>, floor }
```

Devices numbered 64 and above are kept in a side map (`wide`). Application is order-independent: claims and verifications are unions, times are maxima, a drop cancels the claims on that storage with a smaller Lamport time (a place counts while its newest claim is newer than its newest drop and than the storage's retirement), and the folder, writer, class and map seq of a pack come from the claim with the largest (Lamport time, device, batch seq, position) stamp among full devices' claims that name a folder. A stream keeps the newest base any announcement named and the records after it (`floor` is the largest base seen, a maximum, so arrival order does not matter); snapshot streams keep every record, since a differential names an older base. Two announcements of one seq with different hashes mark the stream forked. Interned numbers depend on arrival order, so two views are compared through `canonical()`. `holders(pack)`, `copies(pack)`, `is_held`, `dropped_from`, `packs_on(storage)`, `block_places`, `block_copies`, `announced`, and the settling queries `acked`, `settled`, `settle_waiting_for`, `vault_acked_by_all` are the questions the rest asks.

**Checkpoints** keep their rules (written by the device itself, speak for their signer only through `LedgerView::restricted_to`, read only from full devices, refused when more than 65 536 beyond the newest batch held unless the batch they end at is beside them) with the pack-level view as body: `Checkpoint { device, seq, head_hash, created_utc, view }`, a SignedRecord of kind `checkpoint` at `ledger/<device>/checkpoint-<seq 16>-<part 4>.rec` (parts as in 77.5; checkpoint names still sort after every batch name), at most 1 GiB decompressed. **Acknowledgements** keep `seen`; a member's folder-scoped batches carry `seen` for the folder's devices only (`LedgerStore::folder_seen`), which count for settling marks and for pruning folder records, never for vault ledger pruning.

**Estimates** (the design's, not measured here): for 1 M files and 10 TB in four devices and three storages, the first upload is about 337 000 `pack_stored` events (about 27 MB) instead of about 12.9 M `chunk_stored` events (about 3.4 GB of JSON before zstd), the view about 187 000 pack records (about 40 MB in memory, about 26 MB stored) instead of at least 3 GB, and the block places about 730 MB on disk at the devices that hold the folder key. The ignored benchmarks `ledger3_scale_benchmark` (`crates/varsto-core/tests/ledger3_scale.rs`) and `manifest3::tests::a_million_file_base` measure these; their results are not part of this text.

**As built.** `ledger3` carries its own stand-in for the envelope (`ledger3::envelope`), written before `record` was merged; it follows section 5 field for field but binds the AAD and the signature message to `crypto::aad`, i.e. to the build's `FORMAT_VERSION` (2 until work package 12), where `record` binds format 3, and it does not parse the `key_id` grammar or check the size class when decoding. Until the integration replaces it by `record`, the two are not byte-compatible with each other; nothing mixes them. Names are interned in 16 bits for devices and storages: a view takes at most 65 534 of each and counts events beyond that refused.

### 77.5 Signed records

Every object a device publishes, except packs, is a SignedRecord (`record::SignedRecord`): `"VSR3" || postcard(envelope)`.

```text
SignedRecord {
  format: u16 = 3, kind: u8, vault: [16], scope: [16] (zeros = vault-wide), device: [16],
  seq: u64, part: u16, parts: u16, key_id: String, body_hash: [32], sig_alg: u8 (1 = ed25519+ml-dsa-65),
  sig: bytes,   // 64 + 3309 bytes, over aad("record-signature", [kind, vault, scope, device, seq, part, parts, key_id, body_hash])
  body: bytes,  // nonce || AEAD ciphertext || tag
}
body AAD = aad(<kind name>, [vault, scope, device, seq u64, part u16, parts u16, key_id])
body_hash = BLAKE3(body)
```

`kem:` bodies are `KEM ciphertext || nonce || ciphertext || tag` (the AEAD key is the KEM secret, the AAD the generic one above), and `none` bodies are the framed plaintext without encryption. The byte fields are postcard byte strings (the same bytes as a `Vec<u8>`). The signature covers `body_hash`, so an envelope with an empty body proves that its signer wrote a record with that hash (head exchange, 77.8); `verify` refuses an empty body.

**Padding.** The plaintext of a part is `len: u32 || data || random padding`, where `len` is the length of the data (a slice of `zstd(postcard(body))`) and the padding is CSPRNG bytes chosen so that the length of `body` (nonce, KEM ciphertext, ciphertext and tag included) is a multiple of 64 KiB below 4 MiB and a multiple of 1 MiB from 4 MiB up (`record::pad`; a decoder refuses any other length: `"its body length <n> is not a size class"`). A reader takes `len` bytes after the length field and ignores the rest; a `len` past the end of the plaintext is junk. This applies to every kind, batches and checkpoints included.

**Parts.** A body larger than `MAX_PART_BODY` = 32 MiB is split into at most `MAX_PARTS` = 1024 parts, which are cut from the compressed bytes (zstd runs over the whole body first, the compressed stream is cut), each part sealed, hashed and signed on its own with `part` and `parts` inside the signature. A record is complete when all its parts are there; a reader fetches each part with a limit of `MAX_PART_READ` = 33 MiB, and decompresses at most `MAX_DECODED_BODY` = 4 GiB. The hash a batch announces is `record_hash`: the part's `body_hash` for one part, else BLAKE3 over the parts' hashes in order. Storage name: `<stream prefix>/<seq 16 digits>-<part 4 digits>.rec`.

**Kinds and streams** (`record::Kind`, `StreamRef`). "Announced" kinds count only once a batch of the same device announces them (`record_published`); the others are "newest seq of the stream wins" (put-if-absent at the seq; a writer starts after the highest it finds). The last column says whether the code in this series has a body type for the kind.

| Code | Kind | Stream prefix | key id | Announced | Body in code |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | `manifest` | `manifests/<folder>/<device>/` | `meta:<folder>@<e>` | yes | `manifest3` |
| 2 | `block-map` | `maps/<folder>/<device>/` | `meta:` | yes | `blockmap` |
| 3 | `trash` | `trash/<folder>/<device>/` | `meta:` | yes | `trash3` |
| 4 | `snapshot` | `snapshots/<backup>/<device>/` | `meta:` of the backup's folder key | yes | `snapshot3` |
| 5 | `peer` | `vault/peers/<device>/` | `registry@<e>` | no | `p2p::v3::peer` |
| 6 | `epoch` (vault epoch) | `vault/epochs/` (seq = epoch; device = issuer, in the envelope only) | `epoch@<e>` | no | `membership3::epochs` |
| 7 | `share-epoch` (folder epoch) | `vault/folder-epochs/<folder>/` (seq = epoch) | `meta:<folder>@<new epoch>` | no | `membership3::epochs` |
| 8 | `grant` | `vault/grants/<folder or vault>/<epoch, 10 digits>/<recipient>/<issuer>/` | `kem:<recipient>` | no | `membership3::grants` |
| 9 | `request` | `vault/requests/<folder>/<device>/` | `registry@<e>` or `none` | no | `membership3::requests` |
| 10 | `revocation` | `vault/revocations/<device>/<issuer>/` (scope = the removed device) | `registry@0` | no | `membership3::epochs` |
| 11 | `policy` | `vault/policy/<device>/` | `registry@` | no | none yet |
| 12 | `placement` | `vault/placement/<folder>/<device>/` | `registry@` | no | none yet |
| 13 | `checkpoint` | `ledger/<device>/checkpoint-` | `ledger@<e>` (full devices) | no | `ledger3` |
| 14 | `batch` | `ledger/<device>/` | `ledger@<e>`, `share-ledger:<folder>@<e>` or `replica` | no | `ledger3` |
| 15 | `folder` | `vault/folders/<folder>/<device>/` | `registry@` | no | `membership3::folders` |
| 16 | `device` | `vault/devices/<device>/` | `registry@` | no | `membership3::devices` |
| 17 | `escrow` | `vault/escrow/<folder>/<epoch, 10 digits>/<device>/` | `recovery` | no | `membership3::escrow` |
| 18 | `settings` | `vault/settings/<device>/` | `registry@` | no | `trash3::settings` |
| 19 | `storage-names` | `vault/storage-names/<device>/` | `registry@` | no | none yet |
| 20, 21, 22 | `access`, `notes`, `thumbs` | `access/`, `notes/`, `thumbs/` + `<folder>/<device>/` | `meta:` | no | none yet |

The vault grant (zero scope) is named `vault/grants/vault/<epoch>/...`. `Kind::key_fits` refuses a key id of the wrong class for the kind when an envelope is decoded and when a writer seals (for `share-epoch` the epoch in the key id must equal the record's seq; `meta:` keys of folder-scoped kinds must name the record's scope; `batch` with `share-ledger:` must name the scope folder; `peer`, `policy`, `settings`, `storage-names` and `device` have zero scope). Forward leases (section 70) stay plain JSON: they decide nothing a reader trusts.

**Acceptance rule.** One function, `record::Acceptor::accept` (`open` for steps 1 to 4 and `finish` for 5 and 6), for every kind:

1. The envelope parses; `format` is 3; the kind is known; the key id is of the grammar and fits the kind; the signature algorithm is 1 and the signature has its length; `part` is below `parts` and `parts` at most 1024; the body is a size class of at most 32 MiB; `vault` is this vault; the name's prefix, scope (where the name has one), device (where it has one), seq, part and, for grants, recipient equal the envelope's; all parts of a record agree on every signed field.
2. The signature verifies against the K6 public key of an accepted device record of the writer (for a `device` record, against the key in the body, whose BLAKE3 prefix must be the device id). A removed device's key is no longer asked for, except for folder-scoped `folder`, `share-epoch`, `grant` and `escrow` records, whose standing is decided on the body (77.6).
3. The writer's role allows the kind in the scope (`record::section9_allows`, the matrix of 77.11) at the epoch the key id names.
4. The body opens under the key the key id names (`RecordKeys`), with the AAD above, and decodes.
5. For announced kinds, a batch of the same device that this device accepted carries `record_published` with the same part count and hash; and for a delta, the body's `prev` equals the announced hash of seq - 1 (seq 1 has none, and a delta at seq 1 is a fork).
6. Kind-specific checks (`RecordBody::check`): the body names the envelope's scope, device and seq; manifest entries pass the checks of section 28 and the limits of 77.7; maps pass 77.3's limits; and so on.

A record failing 1 to 4 or 6 is **junk**: counted in `records_refused` by kind, never applied, reported once per name (`RecordsRefused::note`). A record that passes 1 to 4 but has no announcement yet **waits** (not counted; at most `MAX_AHEAD` = 64 per stream, more are not fetched and are listed again later); fewer parts than the record has are **incomplete** (not counted, read again later). A record whose announcement names another part count or hash, or a delta whose `prev` is not the announced hash of seq - 1, is refused and the writer is forked on that stream. Removed devices' records count only up to the last announcement in their accepted batches (the cut-off): the engine's `announcement` stops answering past it.

**Refusal messages** (`record::Refused`): `record <kind> <seq> of <device hex> refused: <reason>` with a reason from `Junk` ("its signature does not verify", "its writer is not a trusted device", "its writer may not write it", "the key it names is not held here", "its body does not open", "its key_id does not belong to its kind", "its body length <n> is not a size class", "it belongs to another vault", and so on), `record <kind> <seq> of <device hex> does not match what its batch announced` for a fork, `... waits for the batch that announces it` and `... is incomplete: <have> of <parts> parts`. **Writers refuse what readers would refuse**: sealing checks the key id against the kind and scope, that the stream's device is the signer's, that a grant is sealed to the device its name gives, and that the body fits 1024 parts.

**Reading streams** (`RecordStore`): `list_after(stream, after)` lists a stream from a start-after name, `highest_seq` is where a writer continues, `read_after` returns accepted records in seq order, waiting ones (at most 64) and refusals, noted in the counter.

### 77.6 Folder membership, grants, requests, roles, removal and recovery

The records are those of `membership3` (work package 8a); the commands, routes and pages are sections 76 and the CLI. `Membership` ties them to one device: it reads the records of every metadata storage, keeps what it accepted and writes new ones.

**Devices.** A `device` record (`vault/devices/<device>/`) carries the name, the K6 public key, the K8 public key and a stamp (Lamport time, device); the device id is the first 16 bytes of BLAKE3 of the K6 public key, so a record cannot be written for someone else's id, and the record is signed by the K6 it carries. Newest by (stamp, registry epoch) wins. Format 2 makes device ids at random; the switch to ids derived from K6 is work package 12.

**Folder records** (`folder`, scope = folder, key id `registry@<e>`): id, name, settings, `participants: Vec<{device, role: owner|editor|viewer, stamp}>`, `denied: Vec<{device, stamp}>`, never a key. The creator's first record (the one with `creation`) makes it the owner; the folder id is `BLAKE3("varsto folder id 3" || creator || nonce)[..16]` and the first record carries the nonce, so exactly one device can be the creator of an id (the design says "the creator's first record makes it the owner" without saying who the creator is). Later entries count only when their writer was an owner just before the entry's stamp (a replay in stamp order; entries from writers that were not owners are counted in `entries_refused`). Participants and denied entries merge per device by the largest (Lamport time, device) stamp, so two owners editing at once converge and removing a participant is a `denied` entry with a newer stamp. A demoted owner can still backdate entries below its demotion's stamp; Lamport stamps cannot tell that from a slow device.

**Grants** (`grant`): `vault/grants/<folder>/<epoch 10>/<recipient>/<issuer>/<seq>-0000.rec`, key id `kem:<recipient>`; body = the hybrid KEM ciphertext to the recipient's K8, then the AEAD (generic record AAD) of `{folder, epoch, recipient, issuer, role, K9ₑ, K11, older: Vec<(epoch, K9)>}`, where `older` holds the earlier epochs the recipient should read, ascending and below `epoch`. Accepted when the issuer is an owner of the folder at that epoch (folder records and folder epoch records). A device that holds a grant to itself holds the folder. **As built:** the epoch, recipient and issuer that the design puts in the AAD are in the body and checked against the storage name and the envelope, which the signature covers. A **vault grant** (zero scope, `vault/grants/vault/...`) carries `{epoch, recipient, issuer, K3ₑ}` for an epoch above 0.

**Requests** (`request`, `vault/requests/<folder>/<device>/`): `{device, name, kem_pub, role_wanted}` with `role_wanted` editor or viewer, under the registry key. Every owner device lists pending requests (a request counts as pending when the requester is a device of the vault whose device record carries the request's K8 key and which is neither a participant nor denied); `approve` writes a folder record that lists the device and a grant of the current epoch (and every older one held); one owner's approval suffices; `deny` adds a `denied` entry. Approval is manual: the background service only lists and announces (a test reads the service's own source and fails if a call that approves or grants appears, section 76). An approval shows the requester's device name and the **six-word fingerprint** of its K6 and K8 public keys (`fingerprint::of_keys`, `membership3::fingerprint`: the construction of section 24, BLAKE3 `derive_key` over length-prefixed parts, the top 66 bits as six BIP-39 English words, under the context `varsto format-3 device fingerprint v1`, over the two keys). **Design only:** other people's devices ask out of band with the `vsr1` request code and its fingerprint words of section 24, the owner answers with a grant to the code's KEM key and a folder record listing the member, and a member reads its grant from the storage it was given; `membership3` knows the devices of its own vault only, and a participant that stays without a device record here is listed as `pending` by `start_folder_epoch`.

**Pairing** (`varsto pair offer`, section 75): the joining device's hello carries its K6 and K8 public keys and name; the offering device shows name and fingerprint and asks; it issues grants for the folders the person ticked (`Membership::grant_to_joiner`, where the hello's keys stand in for a device record, which the joiner publishes after joining) before it reports success. Pairing is the only path that grants without a separate approval, and it is chosen by the person at the offering device. The vault key K3 arrives sealed to the joiner's K8 inside the SPAKE2-sealed bundle.

**Roles.** Owner: may grant, change roles, start folder epochs, write every folder kind. Editor: writes manifests, maps, trash records and its folder-scoped batches; marks blocks dead. Viewer: reads; its manifests, maps, trash records and marks are refused by every reader (it holds K9 and could write them, so the refusal is by acceptance rule, not by key). Role changes are folder records by an owner; a lower role does not take keys back.

**Folder epochs and removal.** Removing a participant starts a **folder epoch**: the owner makes a random K9ₑ₊₁ and writes `vault/folder-epochs/<folder>/<e+1>` (put-if-absent at the number; kind `share-epoch`, key id `meta:<folder>@<e+1>`, body `{folder, epoch, issuer, participants that stay with their roles, removed: [{device, cutoff}]}` with the removed device's newest ledger batch as the cut-off), `denied` entries for the removed devices, grants of the new epoch to every participant that stays, and an escrow when K1 is at hand (`start_folder_epoch`). One sequence 0, 1, 2, ... per folder serves removals of own devices and of members; epoch 0 has no record. Two owners writing a record under one number: both are recorded and neither is applied, nor anything above, until the user keeps one (`FolderEpochs::resolve`, `varsto folder resolve-epoch`), as section 58's "two epochs under one number" does per folder. Re-encrypting old data under the new key is a separate, budgeted job (`varsto folder reencrypt`, not part of this series).

**Vault epochs.** Removing an own full device from the vault (`remove_device`) writes a vault epoch record (kind `epoch`, `vault/epochs/<e>`, key id `epoch@<e>` = `derive(K3ₑ, "epoch-record")`; body `{epoch, issuer, previous_epoch, previous_k3, members, revoked}`, so the chain of records leads from any vault key down to K3₀), a **revocation** (`vault/revocations/<device>/<issuer>/`, scope = the removed device, key id `registry@0` so the removed device reads it too; body `{device, name, issuer, cutoff_seq, wipe, new_epoch}`), vault grants of the new K3 to every device that stays, and a folder epoch for every folder the device held. A revocation counts only with the epoch record that backs it, applied in `(new_epoch, issuer)` order as in section 58 (a revocation by an issuer that was removed before it is skipped); two records under one vault epoch number are recorded in `vault_forks`. A vault key from before a removal does not open the newest records: such a device sees an old vault and gets no grant.

**Recovery escrow.** Every folder epoch is escrowed as `vault/escrow/<folder>/<epoch>/<device>/<seq>-0000.rec`, key id `recovery`, body `{folder, epoch, K9ₑ, K11}` under the escrow key `derive(K2, "folder-escrow", vault, K3₀)`, signed by the writer (it counts when the writer is an owner of the folder). Binding K3₀ keeps "the recovery words alone give nothing" true even for someone who can list the storage: escrow names show folder ids, and K2 alone would otherwise open them. Every full device holds K3₀ (a newer vault key opens the older ones through the epoch records, `walk_vault_chain`). The recovery kit prints two word sets (`escrow::kit_text`): the **vault words** (the newest vault key K3ₑ, with a key check: device names, folder names and the storage ledger, no content) and the **recovery words** (K1, with a key check: with the vault words, every folder's content; alone they open nothing); `varsto recovery kit --recovery-words` prints both, `--new-recovery-key` makes a new K1, which gives nothing until folders are escrowed under it. Vault words plus recovery words rebuild every folder (`Membership::recover_all_folders`); the vault words alone give the registry and the pack-level ledger, no content; the recovery words alone give nothing. A vault epoch (removing a device) makes the kit's vault words stale, as today (`join` refuses them and the user prints a new kit); K1 does not change, so the escrows stay valid. Tests: `vault_words_and_recovery_words_restore_every_folder`, `recovery_words_alone_give_nothing`, `a_folder_without_escrow_is_reported`.

**The user's decisions on writing the escrow** (design section 6; the commands that make them are work package 12 unless named): `varsto folder add` asks for the recovery words and writes the folder's escrow at once; `varsto unlock --with-recovery-key` keeps a copy of the recovery words for the session, so that adding a folder and rotating a folder epoch need no prompt; until an epoch has an escrow, `varsto status` warns `warning: folder <name> has no recovery copy: run varsto recovery escrow <name>` (the design writes `<folder>` for the second name; the code prints the name twice) for every folder epoch this device holds without one (`Membership::missing_escrows`, also `recovery_warnings` in `/api/status`). **As built:** what exists is `varsto recovery escrow [<folder>] [--with-recovery-key] [--recovery-file F]` (section 76), which writes the copies `missing_escrows` lists with the recovery words from a file, `VARSTO_RECOVERY_WORDS`, a pipe or a hidden prompt, never the command line; `Membership::create_folder` and `start_folder_epoch` escrow at once when K1 is given. `folder add` and `unlock --with-recovery-key` do not yet ask or keep anything, and `join --recovery-words` is a stub that names what work package 12 connects.

### 77.7 Manifests, trash, history and snapshots as base + delta

All four are SignedRecords whose body is a base (`base == seq`, the whole record) or a delta against the base before it, in a stream per (folder or backup, device) that is written to every hot metadata storage and announced in the writer's batch. A reader reconstructs a writer's view as the newest announced base and the deltas after it in order, `prev` checked (`WriterView`, `DeviceTrash`). A pull lists only streams whose announced newest seq is past the one applied (section 25 with "base" in place of "manifest"); a stream announced with a newer base is read again from that base. A writer that cannot publish a seq (taken, or a half-written earlier attempt) moves to the next seq and writes a base there, so a reader never needs an unannounced `prev`. A delta that does not follow, names another base or another predecessor, or a record that differs from its announcement, forks the writer's stream: its view stays as last accepted until the fork is cleared.

**Manifests** (`manifests/<folder>/<device>/`, `manifest3::ManifestBody`):

```text
ManifestBody { folder, device, seq, lamport, created_utc, base, prev,
               devices: Vec<[16]>,          // sorted, unique: interned ids of version vectors and modified_by
               files: Vec<WireEntry>,       // base: the device's whole view; delta: entries changed since seq-1
               removed: Vec<path bytes> }   // delta only: paths that left the view (normally none: deletions are entries with deleted = true)
WireEntry { path: bytes, version: Vec<(device index, u64)>, deleted, size, mtime, content_hash: Option<[32]>,
            chunks: Vec<Chunk3 { chunk: [32], object: [32], size: u64, epoch: u32 }>,
            modified_by: index, modified_clock, meta: Option<WireMeta>, chunking: u8, held: bool }
```

`FileEntry` keeps the format-2 fields of `FileState` (path, version vector, deleted, size, mtime, content hash, chunks, modified_by and clock, metadata of section 49, `chunking`) and gains **`held`**: this writer holds every block of this version (the file on disk, or every block in its block cache). Paths travel as bytes and must be UTF-8 and pass the synced-path checks (`names::check_synced_path`). Limits: 4 096 bytes per path, 4 194 304 entries plus removed paths per record whatever its number of parts, 2^20 chunks per entry, 65 536 devices in the table and in one version vector, a chunk of 1 byte to 16 MiB, the chunk sizes adding up to the file size, unique paths in a record, a base without `removed`. **Base rule** (`manifest3::BaseRule`): the record `n` places after a base is a base when `n` ≥ 64 and the stored bytes of the deltas since the base are at least 1/16 of the base's, or when `n` ≥ 1024. So a base is followed by at most 63 deltas under the size condition and at most 1023 otherwise (the design's test "base + 63 deltas, then a base"); bytes are the padded sizes of `record_published`. Reason for both conditions: at 1 M files a base is about 350 MB (paths compress, the 4.3 hashes per file do not); a count rule alone would rewrite it every 64 publishes, several GB a day per device on a busy folder, while small folders still get short chains. Worst case (design): small publishes never meet the size condition, so a base is rewritten once per 1024 publishes and a joining reader reads the base plus at most 1024 deltas under 22 MB; a mass metadata change meets the size condition at once. The merge rules of section 7 are unchanged (`manifest3::merge`): applying only the changed entries of a delta gives the same result as merging the full views (property tests). **Pruning**: a writer deletes the records below its newest base once every reader of the folder (every participant holding K10, members through their folder-scoped batches) has acknowledged the batch that announced the base; cold storages are not pruned and neither is a storage that does not hold the base (`manifest3::prune_stream`).

**`held` replaces `chunk_on_device`** (77.4): `HeldIndex` answers "which devices hold object O" from every writer's newest view, for transferrer targeting and emptying (77.9), the safety indicator, `where` (bytes and blocks per device) and the forwarding report. The references garbage collection starts from are the objects of the current (not deleted) files of every writer's newest view (77.3).

**Trash and history** (`trash/<folder>/<device>/`, `trash3::TrashBody`): `{folder, device, seq, lamport, created_utc, base, prev, entries, removals, versions, forget}` with `Entry { id, path, size, mtime, content_hash, chunks, deleted_utc, deleted_by, clock, author }`, `Removal { id, utc, restored, deleted_utc, lamport }`. `entries` are the deletions this device found, `removals` the entries (of any device) it restored or purged, `versions` the versions it replaced (the version history of section 50), `forget` the ids it no longer lists (a delta only; applied before the lists of the same record, and it removes the id from entries, removals and versions alike). The id of an entry or version is the first 16 bytes of BLAKE3 over a fixed label, the folder, the path, the deleting or replacing device and the Lamport clock, so a reader recomputes it, and an entry must be the deletion of its writer. A writer keeps an entry, a removal and a version listed for 7 days past its expiry so every device sees it go (`KEEP_LISTED_SECS`), then forgets it. Limits: 4 194 304 items per record, unique ids per list, a removal not from the future, the entry checks of section 43. The union-and-removal semantics of sections 43 and 50 are unchanged; of several removals of one entry the one with the largest (Lamport time, purge before restore, device) decides, and a removal carries no `by` (the record's device is the remover). Removals by members of a shared folder do not count on the owner's devices, and records of revoked devices are not used (`trash3::Trust`). `<utc>`-named records disappear. **As built:** the trash record uses the manifests' base rule (64 deltas and 1/16 of the base, forced at 1024), where the design's text for trash said "every 64 deltas or at 1/4"; pruning is the manifests'. The change history of section 57 is not a record: it is derived from manifests and kept in local files.

**Settings** (kind 18, `vault/settings/<device>/`, not announced): `{device, seq, lamport, updated_utc, trash_days, history_days, trash_storage, storage_id, folder_trash_days, folder_history_days}`; trash days 1 to 3650 (default 30), history days 0 to 3650 (default 90, 0 keeps no new versions), per-folder overrides sorted by id. The newest record of any accepted device wins, by (`updated_utc`, Lamport time, device). Members cannot read the registry key and keep the defaults.

**Snapshots** (`snapshots/<backup>/<device>/`, `snapshot3::SnapshotBody`): `{backup, device, seq, lamport, taken_utc, expires_utc, base, devices, files, removed}`, never changed once written. A snapshot is either a base (a full listing of live files) or a **differential** against a base (`base` = that base's seq): every entry that differs from the base and every path removed since. Differential, not incremental, so expiring any snapshot never breaks another: a base is kept (body retained, hidden from listings) while any unexpired snapshot names it, and goes with the last one. A new base is taken when 32 snapshots (the base included) name the current base, or when the differential's packed size (zstd of postcard, before padding) exceeds 1/4 of the base's packed size (`SnapRule`). Entries are the manifests' wire entries with `held` always false (a backup is held by no device) and no deleted entry; a snapshot has no `prev` (a reader checks that the base a differential names is a base the same device announced), and expires when the ledger says `snapshot_expired` or `expires_utc` of its signed body has passed (the owner owes the mark). `snapshot_taken` carries times and sizes; `record_published` carries the hash a restore checks. Snapshot records are pruned only by expiry. A restore reads one snapshot and its base only.

### 77.8 Peers and transport

Sections 75 and 76 are the as-built text; the rules of the design's section 8 in short, with the differences:

- **Peer records** (`peer`, `vault/peers/<device>/`, newest seq wins): the device's name, port, addresses (LAN, public, UDP public and local, mapped TCP and UDP, at most 32 each), `cert_sha256` (the TLS pin of both transports), NAT type, relays, `reachable`, `heads` (the newest batch it holds of every full device, at most 256) and `pushed` (its own newest batch confirmed on each metadata storage, by storage identity from `vault/storage-id.json`, at most 64). A writer keeps two records. The servers of both transports admit only certificates named by an accepted peer record of a trusted full device, never their own, and never one that two devices claim.
- **No plaintext transport.** TCP connections run TLS 1.3 with the same pinned self-signed certificates and client authentication as QUIC (ALPN `varsto-p2p/3`); a client that does not open with a TLS handshake gets an alert and no byte of the protocol. Inside the session the requests are those of section 15. QUIC stays the path through NATs.
- **Request tokens** are keyed with the TLS exporter of the connection (label `EXPORTER-varsto-p2p-token`, 32 bytes): `X-Varsto-Auth: <device>:<responder>:<unix>:<nonce 32 hex>:<mac 64 hex>`, `mac = keyed_hash(exporter, aad("p2p-token", [device, responder, unix, nonce, path]))`. The responder requires that `device` is the device of the certificate of that very connection, `responder` is itself, |unix - now| ≤ 300 s and the nonce is unseen in that window (at most 65 536 kept). No vault secret is involved: a token cannot be made by anyone who merely holds K3, and cannot be replayed on another connection. **As built:** the design's nonce is 16 hex digits, the code's 32; the token is a MAC, not a per-request K6 signature (the handshake already proves the client against a pin from a signed record, and a download asks for thousands of blocks). A relay checks the asker's token for the full `/p2p/via/<device>/<path>` path and asks the registrant with a token of its own plus `X-Varsto-For`, which the registrant accepts only on its registration connection.
- **Signed beacons**: `VARSTO3 <tag> <device hex> <port> <seq> <sig hex>`, `sig` the Ed25519 half of K6 (64 bytes, for size) over the tag, device, port and seq; a listener keeps the highest seq it accepted per device. A beacon only suggests an address: the pinned handshake checks the device.
- **Pairing hello** carries the joiner's K6 and K8 public keys and name, bound into both key confirmations; the offering device shows the name and the six-word fingerprint (77.6), asks, and only then sends the bundle, with the vault key sealed to the joiner's K8. Discovery answers to `rendezvous(code)`, 10 bits of `derive("pair-rendezvous", digits)` shown as a decimal number, instead of the code's digits (the design said a keyed hash as the record name).
- **Head exchange** (design: a `HEADS\n` message; as built: `/p2p/heads/<list>` and `/p2p/changed/<list>` with the list as base64url postcard, ≤ 256 entries, ≤ 24 KiB of path): on every new connection between full devices and at most every few minutes on a kept one the responder answers with its own list and, for each device where it holds a newer batch, that batch's envelope without body (at most 64 envelopes and 512 KiB). An envelope verifies against the writer's K6 (the signature covers `body_hash`) and **proves** that the signer wrote the batch. The receiver records proofs in `ledger/watch.json` and fetches the batches it lacks from the proving peer (`GET /p2p/ledger/<device>/<seq>`, at most 64 per device and round), checked as if from a storage; it does not push them to storages (only their writers push).
- **Rollback warning.** Storage S is expected to hold batch n of device D when D's accepted peer record lists S's identity in `pushed` with seq ≥ n, or this device pushed it there itself. After a pull, when S shows less than n and a proof of n older than the pull exists, the pair is suspect; after two such pulls in a row the warning is `storage <S> may be withholding or rolled back: it shows ledger batches of <D> up to <shown>, but <source> proved <n>`. Only full devices' heads within their cut-off count; a storage D never confirmed is not judged; an unreachable storage is not judged; the pair clears when S shows the batch or D's record stops listing S. Showing it in `varsto status`, `varsto ledger status`, the Help page and notifications, in English, Finnish and Swedish, is the engine's part (work package 12).

### 77.9 Replicas, pools, transferrers, moves, drains and quotas on packs

`storages3` decides from three inputs only: the ledger view (packs and their holders), the folder's map table (block to place) and the storages themselves. No function opens a vault, a folder key or an engine.

- **Copies** (`copies`): a copy of a block is a storage holding a live place of it: the pack held there (claimed, not dropped, the storage not retired) and the place without a dead or lost mark; the writer's own dead flag counts like a dead mark. Policies (section 14), placement (section 23), the folder safety indicator, `where` and the forwarding report count this way, and need the map only to go from a file to its blocks.
- **Replicas** (`replica`): a replica round copies packs whole, checks each against its id, claims it with `pack_stored` (zero folder, map seq 0) and records that it read it back with `pack_verified`; everything else on the source (maps, manifests, trash and other records, ledger batches) is copied as it is and never opened; a replica writes no map, no mark and no record of any kind.
- **Pools** (`pools`): a pool keeps `packs/<xx>/<pack id>` files on removable disks; which packs it should keep is known from the ledger (each `pack_stored` names the pack and its class; a pack no live claim names is junk). A disk insert tidies away such packs and packs of the wrong size and returns `pack_dropped` for packs the pool holds on no other disk; a disk check hashes every pack against its id (`pack_verified` for good packs, `pack_dropped` for bad or gone ones the pool holds nowhere else; bad packs are removed from the disk and the repair reads them from another storage); "next disk to dock" counts pack copies; a disk group rule counts the places that hold the pack.
- **Moves and staged moves** (`moves`): a pack whose live members are all in scope is copied **whole** (no key, bytes checked against the pack id), claimed with a zero folder, read back, and only then dropped from the source; a pack that holds live members outside the scope is not copied: the members in scope go into new packs on the destination, the members outside into a new pack on the source (the remainder), and then the old pack goes. New places are returned as written packs for the mover's map record. A move never deletes a source pack before the map record is published and the batch with its drop is pushed (`MoveRun::delete_dropped`); a move that stops halfway is continued by planning it again. A pool destination whose disk has no room for a whole pack gets the members in halves, each in the smallest class it fits.
- **Drains and quotas** (`drain`): draining or removing a storage works through the packs the ledger shows held on it; each block must keep, without this storage, the copies the folder's rules held before (capped at what was there), and is never left without a copy; missing copies are whole copies of the pack onto the folder's other storages, and then the pack is dropped; a pack whose needs cannot be met elsewhere is a refusal that says what is missing. A storage's use against its quota is the packs claimed on it and not dropped, counted by class, padding included (`storage_usage`: bytes and packs).
- **Transferrers** (`carriers`): a carrier takes what no other device has yet (with destinations: what one of them still lacks) and is emptied when every destination holds it; "holds" is the `held` flag of the manifests (`ObjectHolders`, `HeldIndex`). A transferrer holds whole packs, so a pack is emptied from it when every live member is delivered.
- A whole pack copied by anyone other than its writer is claimed with a zero folder and map seq 0 (as a replica's claims), so the claim that decides a pack's folder, writer and map record in the view stays the writer's.

### 77.10 fsck, ledger rebuild and scrub

**fsck** (`fsck3::fsck`, `fix`) is a function over what an engine holds: the ledger view, the opened storages, per folder the map table and the manifests, and optionally a probe that reads the announced records. It reports (`Fsck3Report`, JSON for `varsto fsck --json`): every pack the view claims, per claimed storage, present at exactly its class length (a ranged read of two bytes at `class - 1`, no download; `missing_copies`, `wrong_length`), with `--verify` its hash against the id through the per-pack verification of 77.3 (`corrupt_copies`, `verify_errors`); a class a storage may not hold (the 256 MiB class on a hot storage; reported with the wrong lengths); packs on a storage that nobody claims (`orphans`, with an age and `gc_due` after 7 days when the caller can supply modification times; without them an orphan is never due); per map the places that pass the end of their pack (`outside_pack`), lie in a pack the ledger never claimed or gives to another folder (`unknown_pack`), name another class than the ledger does (`class_mismatch`) or overlap (`overlapping`), and packs the view gives to the folder that its applied maps do not list (`unmapped_packs`); per folder the objects a current manifest references (`blocks`: referenced, live, only marked, missing) and the files damaged because a block has no live place on a storage that really holds the pack (`damaged_files`); announced map and manifest records that are gone from the metadata storages, incomplete, waiting or refused (`records`); forked devices and streams and the counters `records_refused`, `events_refused`, `events_unknown`, `marks_refused`; storages that could not be read or whose claims were not checked. **As built:** duplicate claims cannot exist in the pack-level view (claims are a union of devices); the equivalent corruption is two places that overlap or name different objects, reported as `overlapping`. `fix` makes the one repair the design allows without a folder key: a pack missing on one storage is copied whole from another storage that holds it intact (length and hash checked), the claim being in the ledger already, so nothing is announced; corrupt and short packs are handed to the repair callback (77.3). Section 38 for format 3 is `rebuild3`.

**Ledger rebuild** (`rebuild3::rebuild`, `varsto ledger rebuild --json`) is for a device whose local `ledger/` directory is gone and which signed batches the storages may or may not hold; signing a number the storages hold already would fork the identity, so it signs nothing until the end: (1) pull every device's newest checkpoint and the batches after it from every metadata storage (this device's own through `ingest_own_restored`, which moves its head past a gap no storage can fill); a storage that holds a second history of this identity under one number marks it forked, and the rebuild refuses (`two histories of this device exist (...); pair this computer again as a new device (varsto reset --yes, then varsto join)`); (2) when another device said (signed `seen`) that it holds batches of this device that no storage returned, the next number is not free, and the rebuild asks the peers (`fill_own`, peer-to-peer) or refuses (`other devices hold batches of this device up to <n>, the storages only up to <m>; signing now would reuse a number...`); (3) reconstruct the events that were in lost batches and sign them in new batches: `pack_stored` for packs this device's own map records name and that are on a storage at their class length, `pack_stored` for packs no map of this device names and nobody claims after their hash was verified (no verification callback, no claim), `record_published` for its map and manifest records no accepted batch announces, `folder_added` for folders the engine holds that the ledger does not know, `device_enrolled` when nothing of this device was found; claims the ledger has already are not repeated, so a second run changes nothing; (4) push the new batches to the hot metadata storages. The batch is marked as a rebuild only in the local `rebuild3.json`: the ten events of 77.4 are binding, and a new wire event would be one more code older readers count as unknown. Its own records of other kinds (trash, snapshots) are not announced again by this build.

**Scrub** (`scrub3`, section 33 adapted): the unit is the pack. A target (a storage or a pool disk) is swept pack by pack: the items are the packs the view holds on a storage (or the pack files on a disk), their size is the class, and each is read whole (one GET) and hashed against its id. A pack needs no folder key to be checked, so K3-only devices and replicas scrub too. Schedule, budgets, cursor (now the last pack id of the pass) and report are the format-2 ones, so the state file and the status text need no change; reads go through `parverify::run_ordered` in the lanes of section 39, results consumed in key order, so the cursor never passes a pack that was not checked. A mismatch (missing, wrong length, hash differs) is handed to the repair queue of 77.3, not repaired here; scrub checks are not recorded in the ledger (a writer re-reading its own copy is not an independent verification). Budgets count whole classes: a 64 MiB pack costs 64 MiB of the budget however full it is.

### 77.11 Authorization matrix

What a writer may publish is checked twice: by the acceptance rule for records (77.5, step 3: `record::section9_allows` over the writer's class in the record's scope, with the role at the epoch the key id names) and by the scope of its batch for ledger events (77.4, `EventScope`). A record or event outside the writer's row is left out and counted.

| Writer | Record kinds it may write | Ledger events that count | Scope |
|---|---|---|---|
| full device, folder owner | every kind | every event (scope `Full`) | its folders and the vault |
| full device, folder editor | manifest, block-map, trash, access, notes, thumbs, batch | every event | that folder |
| full device, folder viewer | access, batch | every event | that folder |
| full device without a grant for the folder (K3 only) | request, device, peer, policy, placement, settings, storage-names, batch | every event | vault |
| full device, vault-wide records | snapshot, peer, epoch, grant, revocation, policy, checkpoint, batch, device, settings, storage-names | every event | vault |
| member device (another person's), editor | manifest, block-map, trash of that folder; folder-scoped batch | `device_enrolled`; `pack_stored`, `pack_verified`, `pack_dropped` (its packs), `record_published` of those three kinds, for that folder | that folder |
| member device, viewer | folder-scoped batch | `device_enrolled`; `pack_verified` of that folder's packs | that folder |
| replica | replica batch | `device_enrolled`; `pack_stored` and `pack_verified`, zero folder (and map seq 0) | none |
| storage, peer, anyone else | nothing | nothing: every object must verify | none |

A member never writes registry kinds, epochs, revocations, `storage_retired`, snapshots, policy, placement or settings. Only owners write folder records, folder epochs and grants of their folders; escrow records count from owners. Only full devices of the vault write vault epochs, revocations and checkpoints. Folder, folder-epoch and escrow records pass step 3 for any registered device and are judged on the body (the writer was an owner at the entry's stamp or at that epoch), and a removed device's folder-scoped records of those kinds still verify for what they said before the cut-off. **As built:** the scope of a batch comes from its key id, so a full device's batch (`ledger@<e>`) counts in full whatever its role in a folder; the design narrows the events of a viewer and of a K3-only device (for example to `pack_stored` of whole packs, `pack_verified`, `pack_dropped`, `storage_retired` and `device_enrolled` for a K3-only device, and `pack_verified` for a viewer), and the ledger does not: the roles restrict what records a full device may write (the column before), and the events of a device that holds the vault's ledger key are as trusted as the device. A K3-only device may write `placement` records here, where the design lists "placement (of folders it holds)". The member and replica rows are in `section9_allows` and in the ledger scopes (`share-ledger:` and `replica` key ids), but `membership3::Trust` selects only the full-device rows, because member device records of other people's vaults are not built (77.6).

### 77.12 What each party learns

- **The storage**: the vault id; device ids and how often each writes (record names carry device and sequence, never time); folder ids and the number of writer streams per folder; pack size classes only (256 KiB, 1, 4, 16, 64, and on cold storages 256 MiB: from a 256 KiB pack only that its members total at most 256 KiB), how many packs and when they appear and go; record sizes in 64 KiB steps below 4 MiB and 1 MiB steps above, so manifests, maps and trash records show file and block counts and the size of changes only at that granularity; that a folder has grants to KEM keys of other devices. Not: file names, file sizes, file counts per pack, where a block starts or ends, chunk ids, object names of blocks (only pack ids), whether a known file is present. The same 10 MiB file pushed to two folders gives no pack whose size or id reveals it.
- **A holder of K3 only**: everything the storage learns, plus device names, folder names, settings and participants, which storage holds which pack, bytes per folder, record announcements (kind, seq, size) and peer addresses. Never chunk ids, object names, block places or contents.
- **A member of a shared folder**: everything of that folder (K10): file names, contents, maps, the folder's places in packs; nothing of other folders (their packs are listed on the storage, nothing more).
- **A replica**: pack ids, sizes, its own claims; it copies packs whole and needs no folder key.
- **A peer** (a full device of the vault): what any full device knows; an eavesdropper learns nothing over the wire (TLS 1.3).

### 77.13 Limits

- The push path builds one pack per storage, not one per target set (77.2); the ledger and map code are ready for packs shared by several storages.
- `ledger3` seals batches and checkpoints with its stand-in envelope until the integration (77.4).
- Members who are other people's devices (the `vsr1` path, member device records, member folder-scoped batches from other vaults) are not built (77.6); members' folder-scoped acknowledgements exist in the view.
- Collection and map or manifest pruning wait for every writer's or reader's acknowledgement: a device that never syncs again blocks them in its folders until it is removed, as a silent full device blocks pruning today. `GcPlan::waiting` and `map_readers_waiting` name the device; `varsto status` text for it is the engine's (work package 12).
- Folder records resolve concurrent owners by Lamport stamps; a demoted owner can backdate entries below its demotion's stamp (77.6).
- Orphan age counts from when the collector first saw a pack, not from the storage's write time (77.3).
- Repairs, like marks, need the writer role in the folder; a K3-only device can verify and copy whole packs but not rewrite.
- A `lost` mark is sticky; a block with no good copy anywhere stays damaged until a device that holds the file writes it again.
- Kinds 11, 12, 19, 20, 21 and 22 (policy, placement, storage-names, access, notes, thumbs) have key and stream rules but no body type in this series; format 2's records for these remain what the engine uses until work package 12.
- Strongroom keys (K13) and the re-encryption job `varsto folder reencrypt` have no format-3 code in this series.
- The design's estimates (77.4) are not measured in this text; the benchmarks exist and are ignored by default.
- The test list of the design asks for fuzz targets; the series has parser mutation and random-input tests instead (77.14), and no `cargo-fuzz` targets.
- The rollback warning's display and the format-mismatch refusal are the engine's (work package 12).

### 77.15 Migration and the switch

None: format 3 reads nothing older. The integration package (work package 12) sets `FORMAT_VERSION` to 3, removes the feature flag and the format-2 paths, replaces the stand-ins named above (77.4, 77.6, 77.8), and adds the refusals; until then every build, with or without the feature, writes and reads format 2 and `vault/meta.json` says `"format_version": 2` (section 62, `format-versions.md`). Design only, for that package: `vault/meta.json` says 3; a format-3 build refuses a format-2 vault before reading anything else (`this vault uses storage format 2, which this version of Varsto (3) does not read (0.0.1-alpha.15 wrote format 2). Recreate the vault: run varsto reset --yes on this device, then varsto init, and sync your files into the new vault`, with the release number updated for the release that ships format 3), and 0.0.1-alpha.15 refuses a format-3 vault with its own text (`... storage format 3, which is newer than this version of Varsto reads (2). Update Varsto on this device`); `varsto reset --yes` removes the device's vault directory, not the user's folders; `varsto init` makes K3₀, K1 (shown as the two-set recovery kit), the device's K6 and K8, the device record, and an escrow for every folder added; `varsto init` on a storage that already holds a format-2 `vault/meta.json` stops (`storage <name> holds a vault of format 2; use another bucket or directory, or delete it first`). Supersedes in this document, for format 3: sections 3 and 4 (keys, AAD), 5 (layout), 6 (events, batch body), 7 (manifests as full views), 15 (token and plaintext HTTP), 21 (folder keys from vault epochs), 22 (checkpoint body), 24 (share tokens, share keys, `SHARE_EPOCH_BASE`), 25 (manifest pruning unit), 30 and 62 (pack index, tombstones, per-block claims), 43 and 50 (`<utc>` trash records), 58 (limits on unsigned key ids and manifests).
